Unrestricted Quantum Moduli Algebras. I. The Case of Punctured Spheres
Stéphane BASEILHAC and Philippe ROCHE

Abstract
Let Σ \Sigma be a finite type surface, and G G a complex algebraic simple Lie group with Lie algebra 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} . The quantum moduli algebra of ( Σ , G ) (\Sigma,G) is a quantization of the ring of functions of X G ​ ( Σ ) X_{G}(\Sigma) , the variety of G G -characters of π 1 ​ ( Σ ) \pi_{1}(\Sigma) , introduced by Alekseev–Grosse–Schomerus and Buffenoir–Roche in the mid ’90s. It can be realized as the invariant subalgebra of so-called graph algebras, which are U q ​ ( 𝔤 ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) -module-algebras associated to graphs on Σ \Sigma , where U q ​ ( 𝔤 ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) is the quantum group corresponding to G G . We study the structure of the quantum moduli algebra in the case where Σ \Sigma is a sphere with n + 1 n+1 open disks removed, n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , using the graph algebra of the “daisy” graph on Σ \Sigma to make computations easier. We provide new results that hold for arbitrary G G and generic q q , and develop the theory in the case where q = ϵ q=\epsilon , a primitive root of unity of odd order, and G = SL ⁡ ( 2 , ℂ ) G={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C}) . In such a situation we introduce a Frobenius morphism that provides a natural identification of the center of the daisy graph algebra with a finite extension of the coordinate ring 𝒪 ⁡ ( G n ) \mathcal{O}(G^{n}) . We extend the quantum coadjoint action of De-Concini–Kac–Procesi to the daisy graph algebra, and show that the associated Poisson structure on the center corresponds by the Frobenius morphism to the Fock–Rosly Poisson structure on 𝒪 ⁡ ( G n ) \mathcal{O}(G^{n}) . We show that the set of fixed elements of the center under the quantum coadjoint action is a finite extension of ℂ ​ [ X G ​ ( Σ ) ] \mathbb{C}[X_{G}(\Sigma)] endowed with the Atiyah–Bott–Goldman Poisson structure. Finally, by using Wilson loop operators we identify the Kauffman bracket skein algebra K ζ ​ ( Σ ) K_{\zeta}(\Sigma) at ζ := i ​ ϵ 1 / 2 \zeta:={\rm i}\epsilon^{1/2} with this quantum moduli algebra specialized at q = ϵ q=\epsilon . This allows us to recast in the quantum moduli setup some recent results of Bonahon–Wong and Frohman–Kania-Bartoszyńska–Lê on K ζ ​ ( Σ ) K_{\zeta}(\Sigma) .



keywords
quantum groups; invariant theory; character varieties; skein algebras

1
Introduction

Let
\Sigma
be an oriented surface of finite type, and
G
a complex algebraic simple Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. In this paper we begin our investigation of the quantum moduli algebra defined by quantum lattice gauge field theory (qLGFT) on
\Sigma
with gauge algebra
U_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{g})
, where
U_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{g})
is the adjoint unrestricted quantum group
U_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{g})
at a primitive root of unity
\epsilon
. For technical simplicity we focus in this paper on the case where
\Sigma
has genus
0
and
n\geq 2
boundary components, we assume that
\epsilon
has odd order, and we prove our main results in the case of
G={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
. Their formulation for arbitrary
G
has qualitatively the same form.

Our main motivation comes from quantum topology. We aim at showing that the quantum moduli algebras make a very efficient and unifying setting by which quantum invariant theory for manifolds equipped with
G
-characters can be studied. As an example, in this paper we will verify this postulate on the Kauffman bracket skein algebra
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
, where
\zeta={\rm i}\epsilon^{1/2}
is a primitive root of unity of order
4l
, with
l\geq 3
odd. Namely, we will recast some recent results of Bonahon–Wong
[
17
,
18
]
and Frohman–Kania-Bartoszyńska–Lê
[
45
,
46
]
on
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
in the setup of quantum moduli algebras, where they follow from our general results applied to the case of
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
.

Our approach consists genuinely of doing geometric invariant theory for quantum groups. In order to present our results, let us recall a few facts about qLGFTs.

The qLGFTs were introduced in the mid ’
90
s by Alekseev–Grosse–Schomerus
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
6
]
and Buffenoir–Roche
[
19
,
20
]
, who used as gauge algebras the quantum groups
H=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
with
q
generic, or semi-simplifications thereof when
q
is a root of unity. Assuming that
\Sigma
has non-empty boundary, which simplifies this presentation and is the case studied in this paper, the qLGFT on
\Sigma
with gauge algebra
H
associates a
H
-module-algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{\Gamma}(H)
, called graph algebra, to any ribbon graph
\Gamma
embedded in
\Sigma
and onto which
\Sigma
deformation retracts. The
H
-invariant subalgebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{\Gamma}(H)^{H}
is independent up to isomorphism of the choice of
\Gamma
, and so is canonically associated to
\Sigma
. Abusing of notations we call
{\mathcal{L}}_{\Gamma}(H)^{H}
“the” quantum moduli algebra of the qLGFT.

In these papers
{\mathcal{L}}_{\Gamma}(H)
was defined by the method of “combinatorial quantization”, which yields presentations by generators and relations given in matrix form. Assuming
\Gamma
has one vertex and no edge contractible in
\Sigma
, these presentations make
{\mathcal{L}}_{\Gamma}(H)
a natural deformation quantization of the ring of regular functions
\mathcal{O}\big(G^{E}\big)
endowed with the Fock–Rosly Poisson structure
[
4
,
5
,
43
]
, where
E
is the number of edges (loops) of
\Gamma
. Hence
{\mathcal{L}}_{\Gamma}(H)^{H}
is a deformation quantization of
\mathcal{O}\big(G^{E}\big)^{G}\subset\mathcal{O}\big(G^{E}\big)
, the ring of regular functions invariant under the coadjoint action of
G
, i.e., the ring of regular functions on the variety
X_{G}(\Sigma)
of characters of representations
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\rightarrow G
, endowed with the Atiyah–Bott–Goldman Poisson structure.

Bullock–Frohman–Kania-Bartoszyńska provided in
[
22
]
a coordinate free construction of the qLGFTs that works for any ribbon Hopf gauge algebra
H
, based on the Reshetikhin–Turaev functor. They also related the Wilson loop elements of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\Gamma}(H)^{H}
, introduced in
[
20
]
and associated to the isotopy classes of framed oriented links in
\Sigma\times[0,1]
, to the Kauffman bracket skein algebra of
\Sigma
. In particular, by adapting their approach to
H=U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
(which is strictly speaking ribbon in a certain completion), they showed in
[
23
]
that the construction of Wilson loop elements yields an isomorphism from the skein algebra
K_{q}(\Sigma)
defined over
\mathbb{C}(q)
to the quantum moduli algebra for
H=U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
. This isomorphism, that we call Wilson loop map, explained in a very natural way the emergence in qLGFTs of the Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev mapping class group representations and of the Jones polynomial of links, already discovered in
[
6
,
20
]
. We refer to
[
70
]
for a comprehensive account of the axiomatic and algebraic structures of qLGFTs, and to
[
14
]
for their re-appearance in the context of factorization homology.

With the exception of the work of Frolov
[
47
]
, until recently the qLGFTs for (non semi-simplified) quantum groups at roots of unity were not studied, certainly because of their apparent complicated definitions. Although the combinatorial quantization approach is the less intrinsic, it has the advantage of providing computationally transparent connections with representation theory. The more topological approach of
[
22
]
makes some invariance statements obvious, but encapsulates part of representation theory in a diagrammatic calculus, leaving many aspects rather implicit.

A major progress has been achieved recently by Faitg in
[
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
]
. He defined the qLGFTs for arbitrary finite-dimensional ribbon factorizable Hopf gauge algebras
H
, not necessarily semisimple, using combinatorial quantization. He showed that the mapping class group representations associated to such qLGFTs coincide with those of Lyubashenko–Majid
[
67
,
68
]
, which were originally defined by categorical means, and provided explicit and ready-to-use formulas for Dehn twists. In particular this includes (with little adaptation) the restricted quantum group for
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
at a primitive root of unity of even order. In this case, he generalized the Wilson loop map, obtained new non semisimple representations of
K_{q}(\Sigma)
, and established new relationships with the stated skein algebras. He showed also that the corresponding qLGFT mapping class group representations coincide in genus
g=1
with those derived from logarithmic conformal field theory in
[
42
]
.

In the present paper we make a further step in the root of unity case; we note that another approach is being developed in
[
48
]
. We consider the qLGFTs on
\Sigma
with gauge algebra an unrestricted (adjoint) quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
; when
q=\epsilon
is a root of unity we mainly focus on the case
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
. Also for technical simplicity we assume
\epsilon
is a primitive root of unity of odd order
l
; the case of even order can be treated similarly. As mentioned above, for simplicity also we focus on the case where
\Sigma
is a sphere with
n+1
open disks removed,
n\geq 1
(sometimes we say for short that
\Sigma
is punctured), but qualitatively similar results hold for surfaces of non zero genus.

First we construct the graph algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}:={\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}(\mathfrak{g})
associated to the “daisy graph” in
\Sigma
, made of one vertex, one loop encircling each deleted disk, and one “cilium” at the vertex, which provides an ordering of the loops by using the orientation of
\Sigma
. The embedding in
\Sigma
gives the daisy graph a structure of ribbon graph, as shown in the picture below:

Figure 1.1:
The daisy graph.

The graph algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
is a module-algebra over the quantum group
U_{q}:=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
with ground ring
\mathbb{C}(q)
. We define
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
by means of combinatorial quantization based on
U_{q}
, that we reformulate also in terms of twists of module-algebras and braided tensor product. More precisely,
U_{q}
is not a ribbon Hopf algebra, but a suitable extension of the category of finite-dimensional
U_{q}
-modules is ribbon. So, to make sense of the construction of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
we replace
U_{q}
by a categorical completion
\mathbb{U}_{q}
.

In particular,
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}=\mathcal{O}_{q}
as a
U_{q}
-module, where
\mathcal{O}_{q}
is the restricted dual of
U_{q}
endowed with the right coadjoint action of
U_{q}
, and the algebra structure of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
is compatible with that action. Eventually, we find that a map due to Alekseev
[
1
]
yields an equivariant embedding of
U_{q}
-module-algebras

\Phi_{n}\colon\ {\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n},

where
\tilde{U}_{q}
is the simply-connected quantum group associated to
\mathfrak{g}
, and
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
and
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
are endowed with a coadjoint and an adjoint action of
U_{q}
respectively. In the case
n=1
,
\Phi_{1}
coincides with a celebrated isomorphism of
U_{q}
-modules
\mathcal{O}_{q}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{q}^{\rm lf}
, where
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\rm lf}
is the subalgebra of locally finite elements of
\tilde{U}_{q}
, which was first introduced by Drinfeld and Reshetikhin–Semenov-Tian-Shansky
[
73
]
, and further studied by Caldero, Joseph–Letzter and others (see, e.g.,
[
13
,
24
,
53
]
).

For the purpose of defining specializations at
q=\epsilon
, we then introduce an integral form
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
, that is, an
A
-algebra satisfying
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}={\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}(q)
, where
A=\mathbb{C}\big[q,q^{-1}\big]
. It is a module-algebra over the
unrestricted
integral form
U_{A}
of
U_{q}
, as defined by De Concini–Kac–Procesi
[
27
,
30
]
. The construction of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
is based on Lusztig’s
[
66
]

restricted
integral form
U_{A}^{\rm res}
of
U_{q}
and some integrality properties of the
R
-matrix on
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-modules. The Alekseev map yields an equivariant embedding of
U_{A}
-module-algebras
\Phi_{n}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{A}^{\otimes n}
, and the iterated coproduct
\Delta^{(n-1)}
of a (natural) integral form
\mathcal{O}_{A}
of
\mathcal{O}_{q}
defines a morphism of algebras
\Delta^{(n-1)}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
. We show (see Proposition
6.11
and Theorem
6.27
for details):

Theorem 1.1
.

The invariant subalgebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
does not have non trivial zero divisors, and its center is a polynomial algebra, generated by
\Delta^{(n-1)}(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}))
and
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})
.

In the case
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
this result also follows from the isomorphism
\mathcal{W}
of Theorem
1.3
below, and the fact, proved in
[
16
,
72
]
, that the skein algebras satisfy the corresponding properties. In
[
72
]
it is also shown that the skein algebras are finitely generated and Noetherian.

The constructions and results above are developed from Section
2
to Section
6
, for any of the quantum groups
U_{q}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. As they form the basis of all results that follow we give full details, though part of this material has already been considered in some ways in the litterature. Along the text and especially in Section
5
we consider in detail the case of
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
.

Section
7
is devoted to the center of the specializations of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
at roots of unity. For every
\epsilon\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, set
U_{\epsilon}:=U_{A}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}
and

{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}={\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon},

where
{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}={\mathbb{C}}
as a vector space, and as an
A
-module,
q
is evaluated as
\epsilon
on
{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}
. The Alekseev map affords an embedding of
U_{\epsilon}
-modules
\Phi_{n}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
. We study this map when
\epsilon
is a primitive root of unity of odd order
l
.

De Concini–Kac–Procesi
[
27
,
28
,
30
]
showed that the center
\mathcal{Z}(\tilde{U}_{\epsilon})
can be identified with the coordinate ring of a Poisson–Lie group
G^{*}
(dual to
G
endowed with the standard Poisson–Lie structure), and that certain Hamiltonian vector fields on
\operatorname{Spec}\big(\mathcal{Z}\big(\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}\big)\big)\cong G^{*}
can be integrated to define an
infinite dimensional
group
{\mathcal{G}}_{\rm DCK}
acting by
analytic
automorphisms on
\operatorname{Spec}\big(\mathcal{Z}\big(\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}\big)\big)
, and by automorphisms on a suitable completion of
U_{\epsilon}
. The orbits of this action, called
quantum coadjoint action
, lift the orbits of the conjugation action via the natural covering map
G^{*}\rightarrow G^{0}
, where
G^{0}
is the big cell of
G
. They used this action to obtain a series of fundamental results on the simple
U_{\epsilon}
-modules.

It is not hard to make the quantum coadjoint action explicit for
U_{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
. Therefore, starting from Section
7
we restrict to the case of
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
, thus omitting
\mathfrak{g}
from the notations (denoting, e.g.,
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
by
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
), and we put
G={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
. In the case of
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
the Alekseev map affords an isomorphism

\Phi_{n}\colon\ {}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow U_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n},

where
{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is the specialization at
q=\epsilon
of a localization of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
introduced in Section
6
.

After proving preliminary results on the center
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
in Section
7.1
, we extend the quantum coadjoint action by means of the Alekseev map, to get in Section
7.2
an action of a
finite dimensional
Lie group
{\mathcal{G}}
on the fraction ring of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
(hence a
partial action
on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}))
by rational transformations) and on a suitable completion of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
. Then we study the invariant subalgebra
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
. The groups
{\mathcal{G}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}_{\rm DCK}
are different. In fact, it is necessary to adapt the constructions of De Concini–Kac–Procesi in the case
n>1
(see Remark
7.15
). We note that the problem of extending the quantum coadjoint action to graph algebras has already been considered by Frolov in
[
47
]
.

We can summarize the main results of Section
7
as follows (see Corollary
7.20
for a precise and more complete statement). Denote by
X_{G}(\Sigma)
the variety of characters of representations
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\rightarrow G
. We define an
l
-fold branched covering space
\tilde{G}
of
G
, and an
l^{n}
-fold branched covering space
\tilde{X}_{G}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
of
X_{G}(\Sigma)
, related by a natural branched covering identification map
\tilde{\mathfrak{c}}\colon\tilde{X}_{G}^{\prime}(\Sigma)\rightarrow\tilde{G}^{n}
. Then we prove:

Theorem 1.2
.

The center
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
is naturally endowed with a Poisson bracket
\{\ ,\}_{\rm QCA}
inherited from the algebra structure of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
, so that there is an isomorphism of Poisson algebras

\widetilde{\rm Fr}\colon\ \big(\mathcal{O}\big(\tilde{G}^{n}\big),\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}\big)\rightarrow\big(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}),\{\ ,\}_{\rm QCA}\big),

where
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}
is the trivial extension to
\mathcal{O}\big(\tilde{G}^{n}\big)
of the Fock–Rosly Poisson bracket on
\mathcal{O}(G^{n})
. Moreover,
\widetilde{\rm Fr}
yields an isomorphism of Poisson algebras

\widetilde{\rm Fr}\circ\tilde{\mathfrak{c}}^{-1*}\colon\ \big(\mathcal{O}\big(\tilde{X}_{G}^{\prime}(\Sigma)\big),\{\,,\,\}_{\rm Gold}\big)\rightarrow\big(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}},\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}\big),

where
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm Gold}
is the trivial extension to
\mathcal{O}\big(\tilde{X}_{G}^{\prime}(\Sigma)\big)
of the Atiyah–Bott–Goldman Poisson bracket on
\mathcal{O}(X_{G}(\Sigma))
.

The isomorphism
\widetilde{\rm Fr}
provides a precise formulation of what means combinatorial quantization of the
\operatorname{Ad}(G)
-module-algebra
\mathcal{O}(G^{n})
at roots of unity. It maps the elements generating
\mathcal{O}\big(\tilde{G}^{n}\big)
as an extension of
\mathcal{O}(G^{n})
to analogs of Casimir elements in
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
. On the subalgebra
\mathcal{O}(G^{n})
, it is given by a
Frobenius map

{\rm Fr}
, analogous in the case
n=1
to the one defined for
\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}={\rm SL}_{\epsilon}(2)
by Parshall–Wang in
[
71
]
, though more complicated, see Definitions
7.1
and
7.3
. It satisfies the remarkable identity (and a similar one for
n>1
, see Proposition
7.6
)

{\rm Fr}\big(\!\operatorname{Tr}\big(\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}\big)\big)=T_{l}\big(\!\operatorname{qTr}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}\!\big)\big)=\operatorname{qTr}\big({T_{l}(V_{2})}{M}\big),

where
\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}
is the matrix of coordinate functions of
G
in its fundamental representation,
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}
is the matrix of generators of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
in the fundamental representation
V_{2}
of
U_{\epsilon}
,
T_{l}
is the
l
-th Chebyshev polynomial of the first type (suitably normalized),
T_{l}(V_{2})
the corresponding virtual representation in the Grothendieck ring of
U_{q}
-modules, and
\operatorname{qTr}
and
\operatorname{Tr}
are the quantum trace and classical trace of
2\times 2
matrices respectively. This identity shows how
Fr
relates invariant functions on
G
to
{\mathcal{G}}
-invariant central elements. The appearance of the
l
-th Chebyshev polynomial
T_{l}
in this context relies on the fact that it generates the defining relation of
\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
, between the Casimir element
\Omega
and the generators
E^{l}
,
F^{l}
,
K^{\pm l}
of the “small” center
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})\subset\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
.

In the context of the quantum function algebra
{\rm SL}_{\epsilon}(2)
, identities similar to the above one and its extension to
n>1
have been obtained by Bonahon in
[
15
]
.

In Section
8
we develop a topological (i.e., skein theoretic) formulation of some of the previous results.
In Section
8.1
we give two definitions of a
Wilson loop functor

\mathbb{W}
, defined on a category of ribbon oriented graphs in
\Sigma\times[0,1]
colored by
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-modules, extending the Wilson loop map of
[
20
]
and defined for any
\mathfrak{g}
. One of these formulations uses the Reshetikhin–Turaev functor, and is close to the one of
[
22
]
.

In Section
8.2
we consider the restriction
W
of
\mathbb{W}
to
closed
colored ribbon oriented graphs. The image of
W
is
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]
, where we recall that
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}
is the
A
-algebra of
U_{A}
-invariant elements of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
, and
A=\mathbb{C}\big[q,q^{-1}\big]
. Moreover, we prove the following result, which is an integral version (i.e., over the ring
A
) of a combination of
[
22
, Theorem 10]
and
[
23
, Theorem 1]
. Denote by
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
the Kauffman bracket skein algebra of
\Sigma
, defined over the ring
\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
. We have
(
see Theorem
8.6
, and Remark
8.7
(3) for a statement over
\mathbb{Z}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
)
:

Theorem 1.3
.

When
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
, the linear map defined by
\mathcal{W}(L)={\rm i}^{{\rm lk}(L)}W(L)
on ribbon oriented links
L
colored by the fundamental representation, where
{\rm lk}(L)
is the linking number of
L
, descends to an isomorphism of algebras
\big(
where
\zeta:={\rm i}q^{1/2}\big)
:

\mathcal{W}\colon\ K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)\rightarrow\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big].

By using the image by
\mathcal{W}
of the multicurve basis of
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
we prove in Theorem
8.8
the following result, about the specialization
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}=\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon}
.

Theorem 1.4
.

The algebra
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
is contained in
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}
.
Moreover, the bracket
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}
extends to an action by derivations of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
on
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}
.

Theorems
1.1
,
1.2
,
1.3
and
1.4
imply:

Corollary 1.5
.

(1)

The skein algebra
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
does not have non trivial zero divisors, and its center is the polynomial ring generated by the skein classes of the boundary components of
\Sigma
.

(2)

When
\zeta
is specialized to a root of unity
\epsilon^{\prime}
of order
4l
, with
l\geq 3
odd, the center of
K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma)
contains a subalgebra isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}(X_{G}(\Sigma))
, endowed with the image of the Poisson bracket
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm Gold}
, which extends to an action by derivations on
K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma)
.

Details are given in Section
9
. As already mentioned above, (1) has been proved in
[
16
]
and
[
72
]
. The claim (2) belongs to a corpus of results proved in
[
17
,
18
]
and
[
45
,
46
]
, for any finite type surface and root of unity
\epsilon^{\prime}
. One interest of our method is to be intrinsically algebro-geometric, and valid for any complex simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. We note that the Frobenius map
{\rm Fr}
discussed after Theorem
1.2
provides an explicit, geometric realization of the threading map
{\rm Ch}\colon K_{\epsilon^{\prime}{}^{l^{2}}}(\Sigma)\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma)
of Bonahon–Wong, see
[
17
]
and also
[
46
]
(note that
\epsilon^{\prime}{}^{l^{2}}\in\{\pm 1,\pm{\rm i}\}
).

In
[
11
]
and works in preparation we study the structure of the algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
and its subalgebras
({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{U_{\epsilon}}
and
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}
, their representations at roots of unity, and we extend the results of this paper to arbitrary finite type surfaces.

We note that the quantum coadjoint action implies remarkable properties of the intertwiners of quantum moduli algebras. In the
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
case these properties should eventually recast the quantum hyperbolic field theories (see
[
8
,
9
]
and the references therein), and therefore quantum Teichmüller theory (by the results of
[
10
]
) within the theory of quantum moduli algebras. In another direction, integrating the action by derivations of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
should provide interesting information on the space of finite-dimensional
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}
-modules. There should be no major difficulties in generalizing these results to the higher rank case.

2
Categorical completions

We recall here the notion of categorical completion of a Hopf algebra, which is connected to the theory of multiplier Hopf algebras, see
[
79
]
. We need it because our main object of interest, the algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}(\mathfrak{g})
, is built from the Hopf algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and its braided structure which exists only in some completion. The categorical one is suited to algebras defined over
\mathbb{C}(q)
.

Let
k
be a field (in the sequel it will always be
{\mathbb{C}}
or
{\mathbb{C}}(q)
), and
U
a
k
-associative algebra (not necessarily with unit). We denote by
\mu
the multiplication map of
U
, and by
\operatorname{Mod}_{U}
the category of left
U
-modules.

Let
F_{U}\colon\operatorname{Mod}_{U}\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}
be the forgetful functor from the category
\operatorname{Mod}_{U}
to the category of
k
-vector spaces. Denote by
{\mathcal{U}}
the set of natural transformations from
F_{U}
to
F_{U}.
An element of
{\mathcal{U}}
is a collection
(a_{X})_{X\in\operatorname{Mod}_{U}}
, where
a_{X}\in\operatorname{End}_{k}(X)
satisfies
F_{U}(f)\circ a_{X}=a_{Y}\circ F_{U}(f)
for any objects
X
,
Y
in
\operatorname{Mod}_{U}
and any arrow
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{U}(X,Y)
. The direct product
\prod_{X\in\operatorname{Mod}_{U}}\operatorname{End}_{k}(X)
is canonically endowed with a structure of unital
k
-algebra, and
{\mathcal{U}}
is a unital subalgebra of
\prod_{X\in\operatorname{Mod}_{U}}\operatorname{End}_{k}(X)
. The multiplication map is given in each factor by the composition map
\mu_{X}\colon\operatorname{End}_{k}(X)\otimes\operatorname{End}_{k}(X)\to\operatorname{End}_{k}(X),u\otimes v\mapsto u\circ v
. The map

\iota_{U}\colon\quad U\to{\mathcal{U}},\qquad a\mapsto(a_{X})_{X\in\operatorname{Mod}_{U}},

where
a_{X}
is the endomorphism defined by the action of
a\in U
on
X
, is a morphism of algebra. When
U
has a unit
1
,
\iota_{U}
is an isomorphism with inverse

\iota_{U}^{-1}\colon\quad{\mathcal{U}}\to U,\qquad(a_{X})_{X\in\operatorname{Mod}_{U}}\mapsto a_{U}(1),

where
U
is endowed with its structure of left-regular representation.

We will “enlarge”
{\mathcal{U}}
by considering only
k
-finite dimensional
U
-modules; we stress that, in this situation, the map corresponding to
\iota_{U}
is not necessarily surjective nor injective (see below for the case of
U=U_{q}({\mathfrak{g}})
). Let
\operatorname{FinVect}
and
\operatorname{FinMod}_{U}
be respectively the full subcategories of
\operatorname{Vect}
and
\operatorname{Mod}_{U}
whose objects consist of the finite dimensional
k
-vector spaces and
U
-modules. Let
\operatorname{FinF}_{U}\colon\operatorname{FinMod}_{U}\to\operatorname{FinVect}
be the forgetful functor, and
\hat{U}
the set of natural transformations from
\operatorname{FinF}_{U}
to
\operatorname{FinF}_{U}
. As
\mathcal{U}
above,
\hat{U}
is a unital subalgebra of
\textstyle\prod_{X\in\operatorname{FinMod}_{U}}\operatorname{End}_{k}(X)
, and the map (keeping the same notation)

\iota_{U}\colon\quad U\to\hat{U},\qquad a\mapsto(a_{X})_{X\in\operatorname{FinMod}_{U}}

is a morphism of algebra. We will call
\hat{U}
the
\operatorname{FinMod}_{U}
-
categorical completion
of
U
.

We will often use special elements in
\hat{U}
defined by series of elements of
U
. Consider a sequence of elements
x_{j}
of
U
, and assume that for any object
X
of
\operatorname{FinMod}_{U}
, the set of indices
j
such that the endomorphism
(x_{j})_{X}
is not zero is finite. Then we can define an element
\sum_{j}\iota_{U}(x_{j})
of
\hat{U}
by

\bigg(\sum_{j}\iota_{U}(x_{j})\bigg)_{X}=\sum_{j}(x_{j})_{X}.

(2.1)

When
\iota_{U}
is injective this element is also denoted by
\sum_{j}x_{j}
.

Let
U
,
V
be
k-
algebras, and
\hat{U}
,
\hat{V}
their
\operatorname{FinMod}
-categorical completions. We define the categorical completed tensor product of
\hat{U}
and
\hat{V}
, denoted by
\hat{U}\hat{\otimes}\hat{V}
, as the space of natural transformations from
F_{U,V}
to
F_{U,V}
, where
F_{U,V}\colon\operatorname{FinMod}_{U}\times\operatorname{FinMod}_{V}\to\operatorname{FinVect},(X,Y)\mapsto X\otimes_{k}Y
. An element of
\hat{U}\hat{\otimes}\hat{V}
is a collection of linear maps
a_{X,Y}\in\operatorname{End}_{k}(X\otimes Y)
, for
X\in\operatorname{FinMod}_{U}
and
Y\in\operatorname{FinMod}_{V}
, such that for any arrows

f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{U}(X,X^{\prime})
and
g\in\operatorname{Hom}_{V}(Y,Y^{\prime})
one has
(f\otimes g)\circ a_{X,Y}=a_{X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}}\circ(f\otimes g)
(identifying
F_{U,V}(f,g)
with the linear map
f\otimes g
). Again the componentwise composition map endows
\hat{U}\hat{\otimes}\hat{V}
with a structure of associative algebra, and the map
\iota_{U,V}\colon U\otimes V\to\hat{U}\hat{\otimes}\hat{V},u\otimes v\mapsto(u_{X}\otimes v_{Y})_{X,Y}
is a morphism of algebra. If
\mu\colon U\otimes U\to U
is the product of
U
, we define
\hat{\mu}\colon\hat{U}\hat{\otimes}\hat{U}\to\hat{U}
by

(\hat{\mu}(a))_{X}=\mu_{X}(a_{X,X})

for all
a\in\hat{U}\hat{\otimes}\hat{U}
and objects
X
of
\operatorname{FinMod}_{U}
. This construction is generalized straightforwardly to
n
-tuples of algebras
U_{1},\dots,U_{n}
. In particular, if
U_{1}=\cdots=U_{n}=U
we put

\hat{U}^{\hat{\otimes}n}:=\hat{U}_{1}\widehat{\otimes}\cdots\widehat{\otimes}\hat{U}_{n}.

Adapting Sweedler’s coproduct notation
\Delta(x)=\sum_{(x)}x_{(1)}\otimes x_{(2)}
, we find convenient to write a sum
T=\sum_{j}u_{j}\hat{\otimes}v_{j}
in
\hat{U}\hat{\otimes}\hat{V}
as

T=\sum_{(T)}T_{(1)}\hat{\otimes}T_{(2)}.

Assume now that
U
is a
k
-bialgebra, with coproduct
\Delta\colon U\to U\otimes U
and counit
\epsilon\colon U\to k
. Denote by
X_{0}
the
U
-module structure on
k
defined by the counit
\epsilon
. Set

\hat{\Delta}\colon\quad\hat{U}\to\hat{U}\hat{\otimes}\hat{U},\qquad(a_{Z})_{Z\in\operatorname{FinMod}_{U}}\mapsto(a_{X,Y})_{X,Y\in\operatorname{FinMod}_{U}},

where
a_{X,Y}:=a_{X\otimes Y}
, and

\hat{\epsilon}\colon\ \hat{U}\rightarrow k,\qquad a\mapsto a_{X_{0}}.

These maps are morphisms of algebras, and satisfy

\hat{\Delta}\circ\iota_{U}=\iota_{U,U}\circ\Delta,\qquad(\hat{\epsilon}\hat{\otimes}{\rm id})\big(\hat{\Delta}(a)\big)=({\rm id}\hat{\otimes}\hat{\epsilon})\big(\hat{\Delta}(a)\big)={\rm id},\qquad\big(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\otimes}{\rm id}\big)\hat{\Delta}=\big({\rm id}\hat{\otimes}\hat{\Delta}\big)\hat{\Delta}.

We will still call
\hat{U}
a
k
-bialgebra although the tensor product is
\hat{\otimes}
and not the algebraic one. In particular, given
a=(a_{Z})_{Z\in\operatorname{FinMod}_{U}}\in\hat{U}
we have

a_{(X,Y),Z}:=\big(\big(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\otimes}{\rm id}\big)\hat{\Delta}(a)\big)_{X,Y,Z}=\big(\big({\rm id}\hat{\otimes}\hat{\Delta}\big)\hat{\Delta}(a)\big)_{X,Y,Z}=:a_{X,(Y,Z)}.

We say that
U
is
quasicocommutative in its categorical completion
if there exists an invertible element
R\in\hat{U}\hat{\otimes}\hat{U}
such that

\hat{\Delta}^{\rm cop}(a)=R\hat{\Delta}(a)R^{-1}

for all
a\in\hat{U}
, where
\hat{\Delta}^{\rm cop}:=\sigma\circ\hat{\Delta}
, and
\sigma(a\hat{\otimes}b)=b\hat{\otimes}a
for every
a,b\in\hat{U}
. In particular, this implies

\iota_{U\otimes U}\big(\Delta^{\rm cop}(a)\big)=\iota_{U\otimes U}(\sigma(\Delta(a)))=R\iota_{U\otimes U}(\Delta(a))R^{-1}

for all
a\in U
.

Assume that
U
is quasicocommutative in its categorical completion. We say it is quasitriangular, or braided, if moreover we have

\big(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\otimes}{\rm id}\big)(R)=R_{13}R_{23},\qquad\big({\rm id}\hat{\otimes}\hat{\Delta}\big)(R)=R_{13}R_{12}.

(2.2)

Then, we call
R
a universal
R
-matrix. We use the following notations:

R^{+}:=R,\qquad R^{-}:=\sigma(R)^{-1},\qquad R^{\prime}:=\sigma(R).

Finally assume that
U
is a Hopf algebra. For
X
a
U
-module, denote by
X^{*}
the dual module (with the standard action
a\cdot\lambda=\lambda\circ S(a)
, for all
a\in U
and
\lambda\in X^{*}
). If
X
is finite dimensional, let
\psi_{X}\colon X^{**}\to X
be the canonical isomorphism of vector spaces. We define an antimorphism of algebras
\hat{S}\colon\hat{U}\to\hat{U}
by

\hat{S}(a)_{X}=\psi_{X}\circ{}^{t}(a_{X^{*}})\circ\psi_{X}^{-1}

for all
X\in\operatorname{FinMod}_{U}
. It satisfies
\hat{\mu}\big(\hat{S}\hat{\otimes}{\rm id}\big)\hat{\Delta}(a)=\hat{\mu}\big({\rm id}\hat{\otimes}\hat{S}\big)\hat{\Delta}(a)=\hat{\eta}\hat{\epsilon}
, where
\eta\colon k\to U
,
\lambda\mapsto\lambda 1_{U}
, and
\hat{\eta}\colon k\to\hat{U}
,
\lambda\mapsto\lambda 1_{\hat{U}}
, are the unit maps of
U
and
\hat{U}
respectively. Hence
\hat{U}
is a Hopf algebra in the generalized sense where the tensor product
\hat{\otimes}
is used in place of the algebraic one.

When
U
is a Hopf algebra braided in its categorical completion, we say it is
ribbon in its categorical completion
if there exists
\theta\in\hat{U}
such that
\theta
is central,

\hat{\Delta}(\theta)=(R^{\prime}R)^{-1}(\theta\otimes\theta)
,
\epsilon(\theta)=1
and
\hat{S}(\theta)=\theta
.

For the sake of notational simplicity, from now on we will omit the “
\hat{\ \ }
” symbols from the structure morphisms of the categorical completions
\hat{U}
under consideration, thus denoting
\hat{\Delta}
by
\Delta
,
\hat{\otimes}
by
\otimes
, and so on, like for
U
.

3
The case of
\boldsymbol{U_{q}({\mathfrak{g}})}

3.1
Notations

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a finite dimensional simple complex Lie algebra of rank
m
. Denote by
(a_{ij})
the Cartan matrix of
{\mathfrak{g}}
, and by
d_{i}
the unique coprime positive integers such that the matrix
(d_{i}a_{ij})
is symmetric. Fix a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
and a basis of simple roots
\alpha_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}
. Denote by
(\,,\,)
the unique inner product on
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}
such that
d_{i}a_{ij}=(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})
. The root lattice
Q
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice in
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}
defined by
\textstyle Q=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}
. The weight lattice
P
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice formed by all
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}
such that

\displaystyle\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle:=2\frac{(\lambda,\alpha_{i})}{(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})}\in\mathbb{Z}

for every
i=1,\dots,m
. So
P=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}\omega_{i}
, where the
\omega_{i}
are the fundamental weights corresponding to the simple roots
\alpha_{i}
, satisfying
\langle\omega_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle=\delta_{i,j}
. Put
Q_{+}:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\alpha_{i}
and
P_{+}:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\omega_{i}
, the cone of dominant integral weights. Denote by
\rho
half the sum of the positive roots, by
N
the number of positive roots, and by
D
the smallest positive integer such that
D(\lambda,\mu)\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for every
\lambda,\mu\in P
. Note that
(\lambda,\alpha_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}
for every
\lambda\in P
,
\alpha\in Q
, and
D
is also the smallest positive integer such that
DP\subset Q
.

Let
q^{1/D}
be a new variable, and set
q=\big(q^{1/D}\big)^{D}
,
q_{i}=q^{d_{i}}
. The quantum group
U_{q}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is the algebra over
k=\mathbb{C}(q)
with generators
E_{i}
,
F_{i}
,
K_{i}
,
K_{i}^{-1}
,
1\leq i\leq m
, and defining relations (see, e.g.,
[
25
, Chapter 9.1]
):

\displaystyle K_{i}K_{j}=K_{j}K_{i},\qquad K_{i}K_{i}^{-1}=K_{i}^{-1}K_{i}=1,

\displaystyle K_{i}E_{j}K_{i}^{-1}=q_{i}^{a_{ij}}E_{j},\qquad K_{i}F_{j}K_{i}^{-1}=q_{i}^{-a_{ij}}F_{j},

\displaystyle E_{i}F_{j}-F_{j}E_{i}=\delta_{i,j}\frac{K_{i}-K_{i}^{-1}}{q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1}},

(3.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{1-a_{ij}}(-1)^{r}\begin{bmatrix}1-a_{ij}\\
r\end{bmatrix}_{q_{i}}E_{i}^{1-a_{ij}-r}E_{j}E_{i}^{r}=0\qquad{\rm if}\quad i\neq j,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{1-a_{ij}}(-1)^{r}\begin{bmatrix}1-a_{ij}\\
r\end{bmatrix}_{q_{i}}F_{i}^{1-a_{ij}-r}F_{j}F_{i}^{r}=0\qquad{\rm if}\quad i\neq j.

(3.3)

Here we put for
p
,
k
non-negative integers with
0\leq k\leq p
,

[0]_{q}!=1,\qquad[p]_{q}!=[1]_{q}[2]_{q}\cdots[p]_{q},\qquad[p]_{q}=\frac{q^{p}-q^{-p}}{q-q^{-1}},\qquad\begin{bmatrix}p\\
k\end{bmatrix}_{q}=\frac{[p]_{q}!}{[p-k]_{q}![k]_{q}!}.

The algebra
U_{q}
is a Hopf algebra with the coproduct
\Delta
, antipode
S
, and counit
\varepsilon
defined by

\displaystyle\Delta\big(K_{i}^{\pm 1}\big)=K_{i}^{\pm 1}\otimes K_{i}^{\pm 1},\qquad\Delta(E_{i})=E_{i}\otimes K_{i}+1\otimes E_{i},\qquad\Delta(F_{i})=K_{i}^{-1}\otimes F_{i}+F_{i}\otimes 1,

\displaystyle S(E_{i})=-E_{i}K_{i}^{-1},\qquad S(F_{i})=-K_{i}F_{i},\qquad S\big(K_{i}^{\pm 1}\big)=K_{i}^{\mp 1},

\displaystyle\varepsilon(E_{i})=\varepsilon(F_{i})=0,\qquad\varepsilon(K_{i})=1.

In the case of
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
we simply write
E=E_{1}
,
F=F_{1}
and
K^{\pm 1}=K_{1}^{\pm 1}
the generators of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
. For every
\alpha\in Q
,
\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{m}m_{i}\alpha_{i}
, we put

K_{\alpha}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}K_{i}^{m_{i}}.

The element
\ell=K_{2\rho}
is group like and satisfies
S^{2}(x)=\ell x\ell^{-1}
,
x\in U_{q}
. Hence
U_{q}
is a
pivotal
Hopf algebra, with the pivotal element
\ell
.

We will also consider the
simply-connected version

\tilde{U}_{q}
of
U_{q}
. It is obtained by adjoining invertible elements
\ell_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq m
, such that

\displaystyle K_{i}=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\ell_{j}^{a_{ji}},\qquad\ell_{i}E_{j}\ell_{i}^{-1}=q_{i}^{\delta_{i,j}}E_{j},\qquad\ell_{i}F_{j}\ell_{i}^{-1}=q_{i}^{-\delta_{i,j}}F_{j},\qquad\Delta\big(\ell_{i}^{\pm 1}\big)=\ell_{i}^{\pm 1}\otimes\ell_{i}^{\pm 1}.

The expression of the pivotal element is

\ell=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\ell_{j}^{2}.

For instance,
\tilde{U}_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is obtained from
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
by adjoining a square root of
K
. We denote
K^{\frac{1}{2}}=\ell_{1}
.

We fix a reduced expression
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{N}}
of the longest element of the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
. It induces a total ordering of the positive roots,

\beta_{1}=\alpha_{i_{1}},\qquad\beta_{2}=s_{i_{1}}(\alpha_{i_{2}}),\qquad\dots,\qquad\beta_{N}=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{N-1}}(\alpha_{i_{N}}).

The root vectors of
\tilde{U}_{q}
with respect to such an ordering are defined by

E_{\beta_{k}}=T_{i_{1}}\cdots T_{i_{k-1}}(E_{i_{k}}),\qquad F_{\beta_{k}}=T_{i_{1}}\cdots T_{i_{k-1}}(F_{i_{k}}),

where
T_{i}
is Lusztig’s algebra automorphism of
\tilde{U}_{q}
associated to the simple root
\alpha_{i}

[
65
,
66
]
, see also
[
25
, Chapter 8]
). Let us just recall here that the monomials
F_{\beta_{1}}^{r_{1}}\cdots F_{\beta_{N}}^{r_{N}}K_{\lambda}E_{\beta_{N}}^{t_{N}}\cdots E_{\beta_{1}}^{t_{1}}
(
r_{i},t_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda\in P
) form a basis of
\tilde{U}_{q}
.

For every positive root
\beta
, we will denote
q_{\beta}=q^{(\beta,\beta)/2}
and

\bar{E}_{\beta}=\big(q_{\beta}-q_{\beta}^{-1}\big)E_{\beta},\qquad\bar{F}_{\beta}=\big(q_{\beta}-q_{\beta}^{-1}\big)F_{\beta}.

3.2
Categorical completions

By a theorem of Harish-Chandra, the map
\iota_{U}\colon U\to{\hat{U}}
of Section
2
is injective when
U
is
U({\mathfrak{g}})
, the universal enveloping algebra of
{\mathfrak{g}}

[
31
, Theorem 2.5.7]
. It is also injective when
k={\mathbb{C}}(q)
and
U=U_{q}

[
51
, Lemma 7.1.9]
.

In the case of
k={\mathbb{C}}(q)
, only specific categorical completions of
U_{q}
are ribbon. We will use the one, that we denote by
\mathbb{U}_{q}
, defined as follows.

Recall that every finite dimensional
U_{q}
-module
X
is completely reducible (see, e.g.,
[
25
, Theorem 10.1.7]
). If
X
is irreducible, it is a highest weight module parametrized by a dominant integral weight, say
\lambda\in P_{+}
, and signs
\sigma_{i}=\pm 1
,
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. Then
X=\oplus_{\epsilon^{\prime}}X_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
, where the sum ranges over the tuples
\epsilon^{\prime}=(\epsilon_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})
such that
\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}{}^{-1}\epsilon_{i}=q^{(\alpha,\alpha_{i})}
for some
\alpha\in Q_{+}
, where
\epsilon=(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{m})
and
\epsilon_{i}=\sigma_{i}q^{(\lambda,\alpha_{i})}
, and
X_{\epsilon^{\prime}}=\{x\in X\,|\,K_{i}\cdot x=\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}x,\,i=1,\dots,m\}
is the weight space of
X
of weight
\epsilon^{\prime}
. We say that a
U_{q}
-module
X
has
type
1
if it is finite dimensional and the signs
\sigma_{i}
of all its irreducible components are equal to
1
. Equivalently, the generators
K_{i}
are diagonalizable on
X
, with eigenvalues in
q_{i}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. The category
{\mathcal{C}}
with objects the
U_{q}
-modules of type
1
is a semisimple tensor category (where by semisimple we mean that any object is completely reducible). We will systematically denote by
V_{\lambda}
the type
1
simple
U_{q}
-module parametrized by
\lambda\in P_{+}
.

The algebra
\tilde{U}_{q}
is defined over
\mathbb{C}(q)
, and therefore also over
\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
. The finite dimensional
\tilde{U}_{q}
-modules over
\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
are completely reducible. Similarly as for
U_{q}
, one defines the
\tilde{U}_{q}
-modules of type
1
as the finite dimensional
\tilde{U}_{q}
-modules over
\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
on which the
\ell_{i}
are diagonalizable with eigenvalues in
q_{i}^{(1/D)\mathbb{Z}}
. We denote by
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
the category of
\tilde{U}_{q}
-modules of type
1
. Also, we denote by
\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
the category obtained from
\mathcal{C}
by extending coefficients of objects and morphisms to
\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
. The restriction functor
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
is an equivalence of categories.

This can be made explicit in the following way. As in Section
2
, we can define the
{\mathcal{C}}
-categorical completion
\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\prime}=\mathbb{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})^{\prime}
of
U_{q}
as the Hopf algebra of natural transformations from
F_{\mathcal{C}}
to
F_{\mathcal{C}}
, where
F_{\mathcal{C}}\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to\operatorname{FinVect}
is the forgetful functor. Set

\mathbb{U}_{q}=\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\prime}\otimes_{k}{\mathbb{C}}\big(q^{1/D}\big).

Extending the coefficient ring of
\mathcal{C}
from
\mathbb{C}(q)
to
\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
allows one to embed
\tilde{U}_{q}
in
\mathbb{U}_{q}
. Let us explain this. The type
1
simple
U_{q}
-module
V_{\lambda}
can be considered as a
q
-deformation of the finite dimensional simple
\mathfrak{g}
-module of highest weight
\lambda
. Therefore, the generators
H_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}
such that
\alpha_{i}(H_{j})=a_{ji}
define elements of
\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\prime}
:
H_{i}
acts on a weight space
X_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
of weight
\epsilon^{\prime}=(\epsilon^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\epsilon^{\prime}_{m})
, where
\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}=q^{(\mu,\alpha_{i})}
and
\mu\in P
, by multiplication by
(\mu,\alpha_{i})
. Passing to the coefficient ring
\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
, one can define an element
q^{H_{i}/D}\in\mathbb{U}_{q}
, acting on
X_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
by multiplication by
q^{(\mu,\alpha_{i})/D}
. Similarly, recalling that
(\mu,\omega_{i})\in(1/D)\mathbb{Z}
, we can define the action of the generator
\ell_{i}\in\tilde{U}_{q}
on
X_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
as the multiplication by
q^{(\mu,\omega_{i})}
. This provides the claimed embedding
\tilde{U}_{q}\subset\mathbb{U}_{q}
.

Extending the coefficient ring of
\mathcal{C}
from
\mathbb{C}(q)
to
\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
allows also to make sure that
\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
is a braided and ribbon category, and that
\mathbb{U}_{q}
is a braided and ribbon Hopf algebra. To see this, denote by
B\in M_{m}(\mathbb{Q})
the matrix with entries
B_{ij}:=d_{j}^{-1}a_{ij}
. By the same arguments as above one can define

\Theta:=q^{\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}(B^{-1})_{ij}H_{i}\otimes H_{j}}\in\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\otimes 2}

(3.4)

as the operator acting on
X_{\epsilon}\otimes X_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
as the multiplication by
q^{(\mu,\nu)}\in q^{(1/D)\mathbb{Z}}
, where
\epsilon=(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{m})
,
\epsilon^{\prime}=(\epsilon_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\epsilon_{m}^{\prime})
,
\epsilon_{i}=q^{(\mu,\alpha_{i})}
,
\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}=q^{(\nu,\alpha_{i})}
and
\mu,\nu\in P
. Recall that the Drinfeld universal
R
-matrix of the quantized universal enveloping algebra
U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
, defined, e.g., in
[
25
, Chapter 8.3.C]
acts on tensor products
X\otimes Y
of
U_{q}
-modules
X
and
Y
of type
1
, where
q=e^{h}
, and that it can be written in the form

R:=\Theta\check{R},

where
\Theta
is defined in (
3.4
) and
\check{R}
, called the
quasi
R
-matrix
, is

\check{R}=\sum_{t_{1},\dots,t_{N}=0}^{\infty}\prod_{r=1}^{N}q_{\beta_{r}}^{\frac{1}{2}t_{r}(t_{r}+1)}\frac{\big(1-q_{\beta_{r}}^{-2}\big)^{t_{r}}}{[t_{r}]_{q_{\beta_{r}}}!}(E_{\beta_{r}})^{t_{r}}\otimes(F_{\beta_{r}})^{t_{r}}.

(3.5)

Note that all terms in the above sum are zero but a finite number of them, since
E_{\beta_{r}}
,
F_{\beta_{r}}
act nilpotently on objects of
\mathcal{C}
, so from (
2.1
) one derives immediately that

R=(R_{X,Y})\in\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\otimes 2}.

Observe that
R_{X,\cdot}\in\operatorname{End}(X)\otimes\tilde{U}_{q}
. Indeed,
\check{R}_{X,\cdot}\in\operatorname{End}(X)\otimes U_{q}
. Moreover, take weight spaces
X_{\epsilon}
,
X_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
as in (
3.4
), with
X_{\epsilon}
a weight subspace of
X
, and with basis vectors
\{e_{i}\}
of
X_{\epsilon}
, and dual basis
\{e^{i}\}
. Then, setting
\lambda=\sum_{t=1}^{m}k_{t}\omega_{t}
, we have

\big(\langle e^{j}\mid\cdot\mid e_{i}\rangle\otimes{\rm id}\big)(\Theta)=\delta_{i,j}\prod_{t=1}^{m}\ell_{t}^{k_{t}}\in\tilde{U}_{q}.

(3.6)

For instance, in the case of
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
, we have
D=2
and
\Theta=q^{H\otimes H/2}
. Hence, identifying elements of
U_{q}
with their images by
\iota_{U_{q}}\colon U_{q}\rightarrow\mathbb{U}_{q}
we can write

R=q^{H\otimes H/2}\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\frac{\big(q-q^{-1}\big)^{n}}{[n]_{q}!}q^{n(n-1)/2}E^{n}\otimes F^{n}.

(3.7)

One checks similarly that the ribbon element of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
induces an element of
\mathbb{U}_{q}
. In conclusion,
U_{q}
is naturally a braided and ribbon Hopf algebra in the categorical completion
\mathbb{U}_{q}
.

3.3
Integral forms and specializations

Let
{A}={\mathbb{C}}\big[q,q^{-1}\big]
. The (De Concini–Kac)
unrestricted integral form

U_{A}
is the
A
-subalgebra of
U_{q}
generated by the elements (see, e.g.,
[
25
, Chapter 9.2]
)

E_{i},\ F_{i},\ K_{i}^{\pm 1},\ \frac{K_{i}-K_{i}^{-1}}{q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1}}\qquad{\rm for\quad}1\leq i\leq m.

We will also consider the smallest subalgebra
U_{A}’\subset U_{A}
invariant
under the Lusztig automorphisms
T_{i}
and containing the elements
\big(q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1}\big)E_{i}
,
\big(q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1}\big)F_{i}
and
K_{i}^{\pm 1}
,
{\rm for\ }1\leq i\leq m
(which is the unrestricted integral form considered by De Concini–Procesi
[
30
]
).

The (Lusztig)
restricted integral form

U_{A}^{\rm res}
is the
A
-subalgebra of
U_{q}
generated by the elements (see, e.g.,
[
25
, Chapter 9.3]
)

\frac{E_{i}^{r}}{[r]_{q_{i}}!},\ \frac{F_{i}^{r}}{[r]_{q_{i}}!},\ K_{i}^{\pm 1}\qquad\text{for any}\quad i,\,r\quad\text{with}\quad 1\leq i\leq m,r\geq 1.

Note that
U_{A}\subset U_{A}^{\rm res}
. Both
U_{A}
and
U_{A}^{\rm res}
have structures of Hopf
A
-algebras inherited from
U_{q}
, and they satisfy

U_{A}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}(q)=U_{q},\qquad U_{A}^{\rm res}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}(q)=U_{q}.

Moreover they are free
A
-modules, with certain basis of PBW type, described, e.g., in
[
25
, Chapter 9]
. One defines
\tilde{U}_{A}
in a similar way, replacing the generators
K_{i}^{\pm 1}
by
\ell_{i}^{\pm 1}
.

We say a
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-module has type
1
if it is a free
A
-module of finite rank with a basis made of eigenvectors for the elements
K_{i}
, with eigenvalues in
q^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Denote by
{\mathcal{C}}_{A}
the category with objects the
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-modules of type
1
. It is a tensor category, which is not semisimple.

Remark 3.1
.

The fact that
{\mathcal{C}}_{A}
is not semi simple can be seen on the following elementary example. Let
V_{2}
be the two-dimensional type
1
simple
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
-module, with basis vectors
v_{+}
,
v_{-}
such that
K.v_{+}=qv_{+}
,
E.v_{+}=0
,
v_{-}=F.v_{+}
. Define
v^{\prime}:=v_{-}\otimes v_{+}-qv_{+}\otimes v_{-}
and
v^{\prime\prime}:=v_{-}\otimes v_{+}+q^{-1}v_{+}\otimes v_{-}
. Then
v_{+}\otimes v_{+}
generates a
U_{A}^{\rm res}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
-module
M
of type
1
and rank
3
, namely
M=Av_{+}\otimes v_{+}\oplus Av^{\prime}\oplus Av_{-}\otimes v_{-}
, and
v^{\prime\prime}
generates a
U_{A}^{\rm res}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
-module
N
of type
1
and rank
1
. The
A
-module
M\oplus N
is stricly contained in the
U_{A}^{\rm res}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
-module
V_{2}\otimes V_{2}
, which is clearly of type
1
, and it is not a direct summand thereof, for
(M\oplus N)\otimes\mathbb{C}(q)=V_{2}\otimes V_{2}
. So
V_{2}\otimes V_{2}
provides an example of
U_{A}^{\rm res}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
-module of type
1
which is not completely reducible.

We denote by
{\mathcal{C}}_{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]
the category obtained by extending coefficients of objects and morphisms to
\mathbb{C}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]
. We have seen above that
{\mathcal{C}}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
is a ribbon category; let us now explain why this ribbon structure descends to
{\mathcal{C}}_{A}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]
. Define the categorical completion
\mathbb{U}_{A}^{\rm res}
of
U_{A}^{\rm res}
similarly as
\mathbb{U}_{q}
above, that is

\mathbb{U}_{A}^{\rm res}=\mathbb{U}_{A}^{\rm res}{}^{\prime}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big],

where
\mathbb{U}_{A}^{\rm res}{}^{\prime}
is the Hopf algebra of natural transformations
F_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}\to F_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
, and
F_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}\colon{\mathcal{C}_{A}}\to\operatorname{FinMod}_{A}
is the forgetful functor (
\operatorname{FinMod}_{A}
being the category of finite dimensional
A
-modules).

We will use the following property of the
R
-matrix. Recall that
V_{\lambda}
is the type
1
simple
U_{q}
-module parametrized by
\lambda\in P_{+}
. By a result of Lusztig
[
64
, Proposition 4.2]
,
V_{\lambda}
contains an
A
-submodule
{}_{A}V_{\lambda}
, which is a
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-module of type
1
such that
{}_{A}V_{\lambda}\otimes\mathbb{C}(q)=V_{\lambda}
(hence
{}_{A}V_{\lambda}
is a full
A
-sublattice). Moreover
{}_{A}V_{\lambda}
is endowed with a
canonical
basis
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
(the
Kashiwara–Lusztig
basis, see, e.g.,
[
25
, Chapter 14]
).

Theorem 3.2

(integrality property)
.

1.

For any
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-module
X
,
Y
of type
1
, the quasi-
R
-matrix
\check{R}_{X,Y}
is an automorphism of the
A
-module
X\otimes Y
, and
\Theta_{X,Y}
, whence
R_{X,Y}
, is an automorphism of
X\otimes Y\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]
.

2.

Denote by
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda\mu}
the basis of
V_{\lambda}\otimes V_{\mu}
formed by the vectors
x\otimes y
, with
x\in\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
,
y\in\mathcal{B}_{\mu}
. For every
\lambda,\mu\in P_{+}
, the matrix entries in the basis
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda\mu}
of the endomorphisms
R_{V_{\lambda},V_{\mu}}^{\pm 1}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})}(V_{\lambda}\otimes V_{\mu})
belong to
q^{\pm(\lambda,\mu)}\mathbb{C}\big[q,q^{-1}\big]
.

3.

For every
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-module
X
of type
1
we have
R_{X,\cdot}\in\operatorname{End}_{A}(X)\otimes\tilde{U}_{A}
.

Proof.

The claims (1) and (2) are classical results of Lusztig
[
66
, Proposition 24.1.4 and Corollary 24.1.6]
; see also
[
61
]
. They follow also from the formulas (
3.4
) and (
3.5
). The claim (3) is a consequence of the fact that
(E_{\beta_{r}})^{t_{r}}/[t_{r}]_{q_{\beta_{r}}}!
acts on the
A
-lattice
X
, and finally that
\Theta_{X,\cdot}\in\operatorname{End}_{A}(X)\otimes\tilde{U}_{A}
, proved via (
3.6
) above. This concludes the proof.
∎

By Theorem
3.2
(1) the braiding
R
of
\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
yields a braiding on
{\mathcal{C}}_{A}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]
, and from this one derives easily that the same is true of the ribbon transformation. As a consequence,
\mathbb{U}_{A}^{\rm res}
is a braided and ribbon Hopf algebra.

If
\epsilon\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
, the
unrestricted specialisation
of
U_{q}
at
q=\epsilon
is the
\mathbb{C}
-Hopf algebra

U_{\epsilon}=U_{A}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon},

(3.8)

where
{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}={\mathbb{C}}
as a vector space, and as an
A
-module,
q
acts on
{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}
by
\epsilon
. One defines
\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}
in a similar way.
The map
\iota_{U}\colon U\to{\hat{U}}
of Section
2
is injective when
U=U_{\epsilon}
or
\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}

and
\epsilon
is not a root of unity
[
50
, Proposition 5.11]
.

When
\epsilon
is not a root of unity, the finite dimensional
U_{\epsilon}
-modules are completely reducible (see, e.g.,
[
25
, Theorem 10.1.14]
). As above one can define the category
{\mathcal{C}}_{\epsilon}
of
U_{\epsilon}
-modules of type
1
. The categorical
{\mathcal{C}}_{\epsilon}
-completion of
U_{\epsilon}
is a braided and ribbon Hopf algebra. Note that, in the case of
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
, again when
\epsilon
is not a root of unity, a similar construction of categorical completion of
U_{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
has been done in
[
44
]
.

When
\epsilon
is a root of unity, the category of finite dimensional
U_{\epsilon}
-modules is not semisimple and not braided. When
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
a classification of the simple modules is known
[
27
,
75
]
. We will use it extensively in a sequel to this paper.

3.4
Quantum coordinate algebra

Let
U
be a Hopf algebra over a field
k
,
X
an object of
\operatorname{FinMod}_{U}
, and
\pi_{X}
the representation of
U
associated to
X
. For every
v\in X
and
w\in X^{*}
we denote by
{}_{X}\phi{}^{w}_{v}\colon U\rightarrow k
the linear form defined by

{}_{X}\phi{}^{w}_{v}(a)=w(\pi_{X}(a)v),\qquad a\in U.

The linear form
{}_{X}\phi{}^{w}_{v}
is called a
matrix coefficient
of
\pi_{X}
. Denote by
U^{\circ}
the
restricted dual
of
U
, that is, the subspace of
U^{*}
generated by the matrix coefficients of the finite dimensional representations of
U
. It is naturally endowed with a structure of Hopf algebra, such that the bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon U^{\circ}\times U\rightarrow k
is a Hopf pairing (see, e.g.,
[
25
, Chapter 4]
).

When
U=U_{q}
and
k=\mathbb{C}(q)
, we denote by
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
the Hopf subalgebra of
U_{q}^{\circ}
generated by the matrix coefficients of the representations associated to the objects of
{\mathcal{C}}
, i.e., the
U_{q}
-modules of type
1
. Equivalently one can define
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
as the set of
{\mathbb{C}}(q)
-linear maps

f\colon\ U_{q}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}(q)

such that
\operatorname{Ker}(f)
contains a two sided ideal
I\subset U_{q}
of finite codimension, and there is an
r\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\prod_{s=-r}^{r}(K_{i}-q_{i}^{s})\in I
for every
i
. We denote by
\star
the product on
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
, induced from
U_{q}^{\circ}
. Since the
U_{q}
-modules of type
1
are completely reducible, and the simple ones are the highest weight modules
V_{\lambda}
,
\lambda\in P_{+}
, the set of matrix coefficients
{}_{V_{\lambda}}\phi{}^{e^{j}}_{e_{i}}
, where
\{e_{i}\}
is any basis of
V_{\lambda}
and
\{e^{j}\}
the dual basis, provide a basis of
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
over
\mathbb{C}(q)
. The
\mathbb{C}(q)
-algebra
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
is also finitely generated by a finite number of elements, e.g., the matrix coefficients of the modules
V_{\omega_{i}}
associated to the fundamental weights
\omega_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,m
(see, e.g.,
[
25
, Proposition 10.1.16]
); when
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
A
or
C
, the set of matrix coefficients of the natural representation
V_{\omega_{1}}
already generates
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
(see
[
80
]
in the classical setup).

Because the morphism
\iota_{U_{q}}\colon U_{q}\to\mathbb{U}_{q}
is injective (see Section
3.2
), the Hopf pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\times U_{q}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(q)
is non degenerate. By extending the coefficient ring from
\mathbb{C}(q)
to
\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
, we can uniquely extend it to a bilinear pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon\ \big({\mathcal{O}}_{q}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}(q)}{\mathbb{C}}\big(q^{1/D}\big)\big)\times\mathbb{U}_{q}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)

such that the following diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\otimes U_{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}
\scriptstyle{{\rm id}\otimes\iota_{U_{q}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{C}}(q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\big({\mathcal{O}}_{q}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}(q)}{\mathbb{C}}\big(q^{1/D}\big)\big)\otimes\mathbb{U}_{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\ \;\ \;\;\;\ \langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{C}}\big(q^{1/D}\big).}

This pairing is defined by

\langle{}_{Y}\phi{}^{w}_{v},(a_{X})\rangle=w(a_{Y}v)

for every
(a_{X})\in\mathbb{U}_{q}
and
{}_{Y}\phi{}^{w}_{v}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
. It is non degenerate. From now on, we will denote
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\big(q^{1/D}\big):={\mathcal{O}}_{q}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}(q)}{\mathbb{C}}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
, and therefore the pairing above as
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\big(q^{1/D}\big)\times\mathbb{U}_{q}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
.

3.5
Integral quantum coordinate algebra

Finally, we will use the integral form
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
introduced in
[
65
]
, and further studied in
[
66
]
and
[
29
]
(see
[
29
, Remark 4.1]
for the equivalence of the two definitions). One defines
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
as the
A
-module generated by the set of matrix coefficients of the
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-modules of type
1
. Clearly, it is contained in
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
; in fact,
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
is the subset of
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
formed by the linear maps
f\colon U_{q}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}(q)
such that
f(U_{A}^{\rm res})\subset\mathbb{C}\big[q,q^{-1}\big]
(see
[
65
, Section 7]
). Equivalently one can define
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
as the set of
A
-linear maps

f\colon\ U_{A}^{\rm res}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\big[q,q^{-1}\big]

such that
\operatorname{Ker}(f)
contains a cofinite two sided ideal
I\subset U_{A}^{\rm res}
and there is an
r\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\prod_{s=-r}^{r}\big(K_{i}-q_{i}^{s}\big)\in I
for every
i
(
I
cofinite meaning that there exists a free
A
-module
M
of finite rank such that
I\oplus M=U_{A}^{\rm res}
).

The Hopf algebra structure of
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
descends to a structure of Hopf
A
-algebra on
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
. It is a full
A
-lattice in
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
, that is, we have
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}(q)={\mathcal{O}}_{q}
. The Hopf pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\times U_{q}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(q)
restricts to a pairing of Hopf
A
-algebras
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}\otimes_{A}U_{A}^{\rm res}\rightarrow A
. Because
U_{A}^{\rm res}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}(q)=U_{q}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}(q)={\mathcal{O}}_{q}
, this pairing is also non degenerate. We have described generators of
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
in Section
3.4
. The
A
-algebra
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
is generated by the matrix coefficients of the lattices
{}_{A}V_{\lambda}
,
\lambda\in{P_{+}}
, introduced before Theorem
3.2
(see
[
65
, Section 7.1]
).

In
[
66
]
, Lusztig proved that
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
is a free module over
A
(in fact over
\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]
). He provided a basis of
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
by considering a multiplier Hopf
\mathbb{C}(q)
-algebra
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}
, containing a multiplier Hopf
A
-algebra
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}
as a full lattice, such that the so-called
unital

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}
-modules of finite dimension are exactly the
U_{q}
-modules of type
1
, and the unital
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}
-modules of finite rank are exactly the
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-modules of type
1
. The
A
-algebra
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}
is a free
A
-module, it has a canonical basis
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}
, and the dual basis is a basis of
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
.

When
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
,
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
is generated by the matrix coefficients
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
of the
2
-dimensional type
1
simple
U_{q}
-module
V_{2}
in the basis
\{v_{-},v_{+}\}
introduced before Theorem
3.2
, and an
A
-basis is formed by the monomials
a^{\star r}\star b^{\star s}\star d^{\star t}
and
a^{\star r}\star c^{\star u}\star d^{\star t}
, where
r,s,t\in\mathbb{N}
,
u>0
and
a^{\star r}
is the product of
a
’s
r
times, etc. (see
[
29
, Lemma 1.3]
).

4
The loop algebra
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}(\mathfrak{g})}

The algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}={\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}(\mathfrak{g})
first appeared in relation with the reflection equation (see
[
60
]
and the references therein), and as the
braided group
associated to
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
, which is the algebra of automorphisms of the ribbon category
\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
of
U_{q}
-modules of type
1
(see
[
69
, Examples 7.4.1 and 9.4.10]
). In this paper we use a third definition of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
, by means of a twist of the
U_{q}
-bimodule
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
for the left and right coregular actions, following
[
33
]
(but using right modules instead of left ones). One merit of this definition is to be intrinsic, not given by generators and relations. That it is recovers the braided group of
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
is shown in
[
32
, Section 5.2]
. The twist uses
R
-matrices, whence the need of categorical completions. The reflection equation will be recovered in Proposition
4.6
.

Denote the left and right coregular actions of
U_{q}
on
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
by

x\rhd\alpha:=\sum_{(\alpha)}\alpha_{(1)}\langle\alpha_{(2)},x\rangle,\qquad\alpha\lhd x:=\sum_{(\alpha)}\langle\alpha_{(1)},x\rangle\alpha_{(2)}

for all
x\in U_{q}
and
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
, where
\langle\,,\,\rangle\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\otimes U_{q}\to\mathbb{C}(q)
is the duality pairing defined in Section
3.4
, and
\textstyle\Delta(\alpha)=\sum_{(\alpha)}\alpha_{(1)}\otimes\alpha_{(2)}
(Sweedler’s coproduct notation). The actions
\rhd
and
\lhd
commute, and
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
is a
U_{q}
-
module algebra
for both actions, i.e., for all
x\in U_{q}
and
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
we have

x\rhd(\alpha\star\beta)=\sum_{(x)}(x_{(1)}\rhd\alpha)\star(x_{(2)}\rhd\beta),\qquad(\alpha\star\beta)\lhd x=\sum_{(x)}(\alpha\lhd x_{(1)})\star(\beta\lhd x_{(2)}).

All this extends to define a structure of
\mathbb{U}_{q}
-module algebra on
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
. Denote by
\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\rm cop}
the Hopf algebra with the same algebra structure as
\mathbb{U}_{q}
but the opposite coproduct
{\Delta}^{\rm cop}
and the antipode
{S}^{-1}
. Consider the Hopf algebra (equipped with the standard Hopf algebra structure on tensor product)

{\mathbb{D}}_{q}=\mathbb{U}_{q}{\otimes}\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\rm cop}.

It has a right action on
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
defined for pure tensors by

\alpha\cdot(x\otimes y):={S}(y)\rhd\alpha\lhd x

for every
x\in\mathbb{U}_{q}
,
y\in\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\rm cop}
and
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
, where
\otimes
denotes the algebraic tensor product.

Recall the universal
R
-matrix
R\in\mathbb{U}_{q}{\otimes}\mathbb{U}_{q}
. Let

F:=(R^{\prime})_{23}(R^{\prime})_{24}\in\big(\mathbb{U}_{q}{\otimes}\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\rm cop}\big)^{{\otimes}2},

where
(R^{\prime})_{kl}=\mathfrak{i}_{kl}(R^{\prime})
, and
\mathfrak{i}_{kl}\colon\mathbb{U}_{q}{\otimes}\mathbb{U}_{q}\to\big(\mathbb{U}_{q}{\otimes}\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\rm cop}\big)^{{\otimes}2}
identifies the subalgebra
\mathbb{U}_{q}\otimes 1
(resp.
1\otimes\mathbb{U}_{q}
) of
\mathbb{U}_{q}{\otimes}\mathbb{U}_{q}
with the
k
-th (resp.
l
-th) tensorand of
\big(\mathbb{U}_{q}{\otimes}\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\rm cop}\big)^{{\otimes}2}
. The tensor
F
is a
twist
of
\mathbb{U}_{q}{\otimes}\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\rm cop}
: by definition, this means that
F
is invertible and satisfies

(\varepsilon_{{\mathbb{D}}_{q}}\otimes{\rm id})(F)=({\rm id}\otimes\varepsilon_{{\mathbb{D}}_{q}})(F)=1

(4.1)

and

F_{12}({\Delta}_{{\mathbb{D}}_{q}}{\otimes}{\rm id})(F)=F_{23}({\rm id}{\otimes}{\Delta}_{{\mathbb{D}}_{q}})(F).

(4.2)

Put
\textstyle u:=\sum_{(F)}F_{(1)}S(F_{(2)})
. Denote by
{S}_{{\mathbb{D}}_{q}}
the antipode of
{\mathbb{D}}_{q}
and set

{\Delta}_{{\mathbb{D}}_{q}}^{F}(x):=F{\Delta}_{{\mathbb{D}}_{q}}(x)F^{-1},\qquad{S}_{{\mathbb{D}}_{q}}^{F}(x):=u{S}_{{\mathbb{D}}_{q}}(x)u^{-1},\qquad x\in{\mathbb{D}}_{q}.

(4.3)

The maps
{\Delta}_{{\mathbb{D}}_{q}}^{F}
,
{S}_{{\mathbb{D}}_{q}}^{F}
and the counit of
{\mathbb{D}}_{q}
define the comultiplication, the antipode and the counit of a new structure of Hopf algebra on the algebra
{\mathbb{D}}_{q}
. It is called
the twist of
{\mathbb{D}}_{q}
by
F
. Denote it by
{\mathbb{A}}_{q}
. Since
{\mathbb{A}}_{q}
and
{\mathbb{D}}_{q}
coincide as algebras, the right action of
{\mathbb{D}}_{q}
on
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
is also a right action of
{\mathbb{A}}_{q}
. Define a new product on
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
by

\alpha\beta=\sum_{(F)}(\alpha\cdot F_{(1)})\star(\beta\cdot F_{(2)}).

(4.4)

Because of (
4.1
) and (
4.2
), this product defines on
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
a structure of associative unital algebra. Explicitly, by writing
\textstyle R=\sum_{(R)}R_{(1)}\otimes R_{(2)}=\sum_{(R)}R_{(1^{\prime})}\otimes R_{(2^{\prime})}

we have

F=\sum_{(R),(R)}1\otimes R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}\otimes R_{(1)}\otimes R_{(1^{\prime})}.

Then, by using
({S}\otimes{S})(R)=R
we get

\alpha\beta=\sum_{(R),(R)}(R_{(2^{\prime})}{S}(R_{(2)})\rhd\alpha)\star(R_{(1^{\prime})}\rhd\beta\lhd R_{(1)})

(4.5)

and conversely

\alpha\star\beta=\sum_{(R),(R)}(R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}\rhd\alpha)({S}\big(R_{(1^{\prime})}\big)\rhd\beta\lhd R_{(1)}).

(4.6)

Note that, by the expression of
R
and the fact that the generators
E_{i}
,
F_{i}
of
U_{q}
act nilpotently on finite dimensional
U_{q}
-modules, there is only a finite number of non zero terms in the last sum. Therefore the expression (
4.4
) is well defined. It is easy to check that the right
{\mathbb{A}}_{q}
-module
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
endowed with the product (
4.4
) is a right
{\mathbb{A}}_{q}
-module algebra. We denote it
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
, and call it the
twist
by
F
of the
{\mathbb{D}}_{q}
-module algebra
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
.

We claim that the product (
4.4
) restricted to
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
is defined over the subfield
\mathbb{C}(q)
. Recall that
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
is generated by the matrix coefficients
{}_{V_{\lambda}}\phi{}^{e^{j}}_{e_{i}}
, where
\lambda\in{P_{+}}
and
\{e_{i}\}
,
\{e^{i}\}
are dual basis of weight vectors of
V_{\lambda}
. By equation (
4.5
), and using
({\rm id}\otimes S)(R)=({\rm id}\otimes\ell)R^{-1}\big({\rm id}\otimes\ell^{-1}\big)
, we have

\alpha\beta=\sum_{(R),(R^{-1})}\big(R_{(2^{\prime})}\ell\big(R^{-1}\big)_{(2)}\ell^{-1}\rhd\alpha\big)\star\big(R_{(1^{\prime})}\rhd\beta\lhd\big(R^{-1}\big)_{(1)}\big).

(4.7)

Therefore, if
\alpha={}_{V_{\lambda}}\phi{}^{e^{j}}_{e_{i}}
and
\beta={}_{V_{\mu}}\phi{}^{f^{n}}_{f_{m}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle{}_{V_{\lambda}}\phi{}^{e^{j}}_{e_{i}}{}_{V_{\mu}}\phi{}^{f^{n}}_{f_{m}}=\sum_{(R),(R^{-1}),j^{\prime},n^{\prime},n^{\prime\prime}}\big(\pi_{V_{\lambda}}\big(R_{(2^{\prime})}\ell\big(R^{-1}\big)_{(2)}\ell^{-1}\big)\big)^{e^{j^{\prime}}}_{e_{i}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{{}_{V_{\lambda}}\phi{}^{e^j}_{e_i}{}_{V_{\mu}}\phi{}^{f^n}_{f_m}= \sum_{(R),(R^{-1}), j', n',n'' }}{}\times\big(\pi_{V_{\mu}}\big(R_{(1^{\prime})}\big)\big)^{f^{n^{\prime}}}_{f_{m}}\big(\pi_{V_{\mu}}\big(R^{-1}\big)_{(1)}\big)^{f^{n}}_{f_{n^{\prime\prime}}}{}_{V_{\lambda}}\phi{}^{e^{j}}_{e_{j^{\prime}}}\star{}_{V_{\mu}}\phi{}^{f^{n^{\prime\prime}}}_{f_{n^{\prime}}}.

(4.8)

Recall that the matrix entries of the endomorphisms
R_{V_{\lambda},V_{\mu}}^{\pm 1}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})}(V_{\lambda}\otimes V_{\mu})
belong to
q^{\pm(\lambda,\mu)}\mathbb{C}(q)
(see Theorem
3.2
(2) for the stronger integral statement). Then we see that the factors
q^{+(\lambda,\mu)}
cancel the factors
q^{-(\lambda,\mu)}
in the last expression. Noting moreover that the matrix entries of
\ell
belong to
A
, we finally obtain that the coefficients in the sum belong to
\mathbb{C}(q)
.

Proposition 4.1
.

The subspace
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
endowed with the product (
4.4
) is a right module
\mathbb{C}(q)
-algebra over
U_{q}\otimes U_{q}^{\rm cop}\subset{\mathbb{A}}_{q}
. We denote it
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}(\mathfrak{g})
, or simply
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
.

The following result is due to Majid
[
69
]
(see
[
33
]
for a simpler proof):

Proposition 4.2
.

The coproduct
\Delta\!\colon\!\mathbb{U}_{q}\!\rightarrow\!\mathbb{U}_{q}{\otimes}\mathbb{U}_{q}
yields a morphism of Hopf algebras
\Delta\!\colon\!\mathbb{U}_{q}\!\to\!\mathbb{A}_{q}
.

By using the morphism of Hopf algebras
U_{q}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{U}_{q}\xrightarrow{\Delta}\mathbb{A}_{q}
we can pull-back the action of
\mathbb{A}_{q}
on
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
, which thus becomes a right
U_{q}
-module algebra. It is easily seen that the action of
U_{q}
is the right coadjoint action, defined by

\operatorname{coad}^{r}(x)(\alpha)=\sum_{(x)}{S}(x_{(2)})\rhd\alpha\lhd x_{(1)},\qquad\forall x\in U_{q},\quad\forall\alpha\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}.

Next we are going to recall a fundamental relation between
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
and
\tilde{U}_{q}
. Recall that
U_{q}
is a right
U_{q}
-module algebra for the right adjoint action, defined by

\operatorname{ad}^{r}(y)(x)=\sum_{(y)}{S}(y_{(1)})xy_{(2)},\qquad\forall x,y\in U_{q}.

This action extends to actions on the simply-connected quantum group
\tilde{U}_{q}
and
\mathbb{U}_{q}
, and thus defines on them structures of right
U_{q}
-module algebras. Denote by
\mathcal{Z}(\tilde{U}_{q})
the center of
\tilde{U}_{q}
. We have

\mathcal{Z}\big(\tilde{U}_{q}\big)=\big\{z\in\tilde{U}_{q},\,\forall x\in U_{q},\,\operatorname{ad}^{r}(x)(z)=\epsilon(x)z\big\}.

(4.9)

The set of
locally finite
elements of
\tilde{U}_{q}
is defined by

\tilde{U}_{q}^{\rm lf}:=\big\{x\in\tilde{U}_{q}\,|\,\operatorname{rk}_{\mathbb{C}(q)}(\operatorname{ad}^{r}(U_{q})(x))<\infty\big\}.

It is a
U_{q}
-module subalgebra of
\tilde{U}_{q}
.
Finally, the set of
\operatorname{coad}^{r}
-invariant elements of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
is defined by

{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{U_{q}}:=\big\{\alpha\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}\,|\,\forall y\in U_{q},\,\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)(\alpha)=\varepsilon(y)\alpha\big\}.

Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
is a
U_{q}
-module algebra,
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{U_{q}}
is a subalgebra of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Define
\Phi_{1}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}\rightarrow\mathbb{U}_{q}
,
\Phi_{1}(\alpha)=(\alpha\otimes{\rm id})(RR^{\prime})
. We have:

1.

\Phi_{1}
is a morphism of algebra, equivariant and injective.

2.

The image of
\Phi_{1}
is
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\rm lf}
.

3.

\Phi_{1}
induces an isomorphism from
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{U_{q}}
to
\mathcal{Z}\big(\tilde{U}_{q}\big)
.

We call
\Phi_{1}
the
RSD map
, after Reshetikhin and Semenov-Tian-Shansky
[
73
]
and Drinfeld
[
34
,
35
]
, who considered it first.

A proof of Theorem
4.3
can be found in
[
13
, Theorem 3]
. To make a complete correspondence with that statement, note that (
3.6
) implies that
\Phi_{1}
takes values in
\tilde{U}_{q}
. The difficult parts of Theorem
4.3
are the injectivity of
\Phi_{1}
and the claim (2). Note that the third claim follows from the first two and (
4.9
).

An alternative proof that
\Phi_{1}
is an equivariant morphism is given in
[
33
, Proposition 4.7]
, based on the construction of a left
U_{q}^{\rm op}
-comodule structure on
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
. The equivariance of
\Phi_{1}
also follows from a simple computation shown in Proposition
6.6
below.

Note that because the elements of the restricted dual are necessarily
\operatorname{coad}^{r}
finite, the equivariance of
\Phi_{1}
implies that their images by
\Phi_{1}
are necessarily
\operatorname{ad}^{r}
-finite.

Remark 4.4
.

We give a simple self-contained proof of Theorem
4.3
in the
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
case in Proposition
5.4
.

Remark 4.5
.

It is a result of
[
52
]
that the
\operatorname{ad}^{r}(U_{q})
-module
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\rm lf}
is generated by the elements
\ell_{-\lambda}
,
\lambda\in 2P_{+}
, where for every weight
\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{m}n_{i}\omega_{i}
we set
\ell_{\mu}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\ell_{i}^{n_{i}}
. Moreover, there is an Ore subset
S
of
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\rm lf}
such that
\tilde{U}_{q}
is a free module of finite rank over the skew fraction ring
S^{-1}\tilde{U}_{q}^{\rm lf}
(see
[
53
]
). The set
S
is the Abelian group generated by the elements
\ell_{-\lambda}
, where
\lambda\in 2P_{+}\cap Q_{+}
.

Finally, we provide a definition of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
by generators and relations. Though well-known, we include a proof for completeness. As
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
are isomorphic as linear spaces, the matrix coefficients
{}_{V}\phi{}^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}
generate
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
. Let
V
be an object of the category
{\mathcal{C}}
,
(e_{i})
a basis of
V
,
(e^{i})
the dual basis, and
E_{i}^{j}
the corresponding basis of
\operatorname{End}(V)
, defined by
E_{i}^{j}(e_{k})=\delta_{j,k}e_{i}
. Define

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}=\sum_{i,j}E_{i}^{j}\otimes{}_{V}\phi{}^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\in\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}.

(4.10)

This expression defines
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}
independently of the choice of basis
(e_{i})
, since we have the following naturality property: if
V
,
W
are objects of
{\mathcal{C}}
, and
f\colon V\to W
a morphism of
U_{q}
-modules, then

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle W}}{{M}}(f\otimes{\rm id})=(f\otimes{\rm id})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}\!.

(4.11)

Given two objects
V
and
W
of
\mathcal{C}
we write

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}_{1}=\sum_{i,j}E_{i}^{j}\otimes{\rm id}\otimes{}_{V}\phi{}^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\in\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}

and similarly
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle W}}{{M}}_{2}=\sum_{i,j}{\rm id}\otimes E_{i}^{j}\otimes{}_{W}\phi{}^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}
. We view
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V\otimes W}}{{M}}
as an element of
\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)
by using the standard isomorphism

\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)\cong\operatorname{End}(V\otimes W).

Finally we view
{R}_{V,W},{R^{\prime}}_{V,W}\in\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)
as elements of
\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)\otimes 1\subset\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
.

Proposition 4.6
.

The following fusion relation holds true in
\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}{:}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V\otimes W}}{{M}}=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}_{1}{R^{\prime}}_{V,W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle W}}{{M}}_{2}(R_{V,W}^{\prime})^{-1},

(4.12)

where the product of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
is used to multiply the matrix elements of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}
and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle W}}{{M}}
. It implies the reflection equation:

{R}_{V,W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}_{1}{R^{\prime}}_{V,W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle W}}{{M}}_{2}\,=\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle W}}{{M}}_{2}{R}_{V,W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}_{1}{R^{\prime}}_{V,W}.

(4.13)

Conversely, the naturality relations (
4.11
) and the fusion relations (
4.12
), for every objects
V
and
W
of
\mathcal{C}
, are a defining set of relations for
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
. That is,
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
can be viewed as the quotient of the algebra freely generated over
k=\mathbb{C}(q)
by the matrix coefficients
{}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}
, for all objects
V
of the category
\mathcal{C}
, by the ideal generated by the relations (
4.11
) and (
4.12
).

Proof.

Let us write
\textstyle R_{V,W}=\sum_{(R)}R_{(1)}\otimes R_{(2)}=\sum_{(R)}R_{(1^{\prime})}\otimes R_{(2^{\prime})}
. The product of
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
is defined by

{}_{(V\otimes W)}\phi{}^{e^{i}\otimes e^{k}}_{e_{j}\otimes e_{l}}={}_{V}\phi{}^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\star{}_{W}\phi{}^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}.

Then the relation (
4.12
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j,k,l}E_{i}^{j}\otimes E_{k}^{l}\otimes{}_{V}\phi{}^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\star{}_{W}\phi{}^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}=\sum_{(R),(R),i,j,k,l}E_{i}^{j}R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}\otimes R_{(1)}E_{k}^{l}S\big(R_{(1^{\prime})}\big)\otimes{}_{V}\phi{}^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}{}_{W}\phi{}^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}.

The isomorphism
V\otimes V^{*}\rightarrow\operatorname{End}(V)
,
v\otimes f\mapsto(w\mapsto f(w)v)
, maps
R_{(1)}e_{k}\otimes S\big(R_{(1^{\prime})}\big)^{*}e^{l}
to
R_{(1)}E_{k}^{l}S\big(R_{(1^{\prime})}\big)
and
e_{i}\otimes(R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})})^{*}e^{j}
to
E_{i}^{j}R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}
. Hence the above relation can be written as

\sum_{i,j,k,l}E_{i}^{j}\otimes E_{k}^{l}\otimes{}_{V}\phi{}^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\star{}_{W}\phi{}^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}=\sum_{(R),(R),i,j,k,l}E_{i}^{j}\otimes E_{k}^{l}\otimes\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}e_{j}}\big)\Big({}_{V}\phi^{R_{(1)}^{*}e^{k}}_{S(R_{(1^{\prime})})e_{l}}\Big).

Now we have

{}_{V}\phi^{R_{(1)}^{*}e^{k}}_{S(R_{(1^{\prime})})e_{l}}=S\big(R_{(1^{\prime})}\big)\rhd{}_{V}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\lhd R_{(1)},\qquad{}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}e_{j}}=R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}\rhd{}_{W}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}},

where we use the coregular actions
\rhd
,
\lhd
and we denote now by
R_{(1)},R_{(1^{\prime})},R_{(2)},R_{(2^{\prime})}\in\mathbb{U}_{q}
the components of the universal R-matrix, instead of
R_{V,W}
. Identifying the matrix coefficients in
i
,
j
,
k
,
l
we recover the relation (
4.6
). Hence it is equivalent to the fusion relation, which thus provides a defining set of relations for
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
.

Finally, note that by (
4.11
) we have

\sigma_{V,W}R_{V,W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V\otimes W}}{{M}}=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle W\otimes V}}{{M}}\sigma_{V,W}R_{V,W},

where
\sigma_{V,W}\colon V\otimes W\rightarrow W\otimes V
is the flip map. Then

\displaystyle\sigma_{V,W}R_{V,W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}_{1}{R^{\prime}}_{V,W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle W}}{{M}}_{2}\big(R_{V,W}^{\prime}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle W}}{{M}}_{1}{R^{\prime}}_{W,V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}_{2}\big(R_{W,V}^{\prime}\big)^{-1}\sigma_{V,W}R_{V,W}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{V,W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle W}}{{M}}_{2}{R}_{V,W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}_{1},

which implies the reflection equation.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

As usual, denote by
a_{V}
or
\pi_{V}(a)\in\operatorname{End}(V)
the component of an element
a=(a_{V})\in\mathbb{U}_{q}
associated to the object
V
of
\mathcal{C}
. The right coadjoint action of
U_{q}
on
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
can be written in matrix form as (see, e.g., the proof of (
6.21
) below for a similar computation)
