\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}\!\big)=\sum_{(y)}\big((y_{(1)_{V}}\otimes{\rm id})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}(S(y_{(2)})_{V}\otimes{\rm id})\big).

(4.14)

Let
\lambda\in P_{+}
,
V_{\lambda}
the type
1
simple
U_{q}
-module of highest weight
\lambda
, and
\pi_{V_{\lambda}}
the associated representation. Denote by
\operatorname{Tr}_{V_{\lambda}}\colon\operatorname{End}(V_{\lambda})\rightarrow k
the trace on
\operatorname{End}(V_{\lambda}).
Put

\operatorname{qTr}_{V_{\lambda}}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda}}}{{M}}\big):=(\operatorname{Tr}_{V_{\lambda}}\otimes{\rm id})\big(\big(\pi_{V_{\lambda}}(\ell)\otimes{\rm id}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda}}}{{M}}\big)\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}.

Proposition 4.8
.

1.

The elements
\operatorname{qTr}_{V_{\lambda}}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda}}}{{M}}\big)
,
\lambda\in P_{+}
, form a basis of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{U_{q}}
.

2.

The elements
(\operatorname{Tr}_{V_{\lambda}}\otimes{\rm id})\big((\pi_{V_{\lambda}}\otimes{\rm id})\big((\ell\otimes 1)(RR^{\prime})\big)\big)
,
\lambda\in P_{+}
, form a basis of
\mathcal{Z}\big(\tilde{U}_{q}\big)
.

Proof.

The first part is an immediate consequence of Proposition
6.22
below (namely, it is the case
n=1
, so that
a_{\lambda}(x)\in\operatorname{End}_{U_{q}}(V_{\lambda})
is a scalar for every
\lambda\in P_{+}
). The second part is a consequence of the first and Theorem
4.3
. ∎

Remark 4.9
.

Let
V_{\omega_{1}}
be the fundamental representation of
\tilde{U}_{q}
. The center
\mathcal{Z}\big(\tilde{U}_{q}\big)
contains the elements (see
[
12
,
74
]
)

(\operatorname{Tr}_{V_{\omega_{1}}}\otimes{\rm id})\big((\pi_{V_{\omega_{1}}}\otimes{\rm id})\big((\ell\otimes{\rm id})\big((RR^{\prime})^{k}\big)\big)\big),\qquad k\in\{1,\dots,m\}.

These elements generate
\mathcal{Z}\big(\tilde{U}_{q}\big)
when
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
A_{m}
or
C_{m}
; see
[
12
]
for a more precise description in the other cases. Using Theorem
4.3
and

({\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{1})\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}\!\big)=(\pi_{V}\otimes{\rm id})(RR^{\prime})

we deduce that the elements

{}_{k}\omega:=(\operatorname{Tr}_{V_{\omega_{1}}}\otimes{\rm id})\big((\pi_{V_{\omega_{1}}}(\ell)\otimes{\rm id})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\omega_{1}}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!k}\big),\qquad k\in\{1,\dots,m\},

belong to and generate
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{U_{q}}
when
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
A_{m}
or
C_{m}
. In particular, for
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
we have
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{U_{q}}=\mathbb{C}(q)[\omega]
, with

\omega=\operatorname{qTr}_{V_{2}}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}\big)=\operatorname{Tr}_{V_{2}}\big(K_{V_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}\big),\qquad K_{V_{2}}:=\begin{pmatrix}q&0\\
0&q^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\!,

(4.15)

where
V_{2}
is the
2
-dimensional type 1 simple
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
-module, and as usual
K_{V_{2}}
is the endomorphism of
V_{2}
given by the action of
K
.

Next we define the integral form
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
. Recall that
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
is a Hopf
A
-subalgebra, and that it is a free
A
-module, and a full
A
-sublattice of
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
.

Proposition 4.10
.

The space
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
endowed with the product (
4.4
) is an
A
-algebra. We denote it
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
. Moreover,
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
is the
A
-algebra generated by the matrix coefficients
{}_{X}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}
, for all objects
X
of the category
\mathcal{C}_{A}
, with defining relations (
4.11
) and (
4.12
).

Note that, since
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
coincides with
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
as an
A
-module, it is a free
A
-module and we have
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}(q)={\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
.

Proof.

We have to check that products of elements of an
A
-basis of
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
can be expressed as linear combinations in this basis with coefficients in
A
. Denote by
\{\alpha_{i}\}
a basis of
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
. By Proposition
4.1
we have

\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}=\sum_{k}m^{k}_{ij}\alpha_{k},

(4.16)

where each
m^{k}_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
. The basis elements
\alpha_{i}
are linear combinations over
A
of matrix coefficients of
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-modules of type
1
. Let
X
and
Y
be two such modules, and
\{e_{i}\}
,
\{e^{i}\}
and
\{f_{n}\}
,
\{f^{n}\}
dual basis of
X
and
Y
respectively. As in (
4.8
) we have

\displaystyle{}_{X}\phi{}^{e^{j}}_{e_{i}}{}_{Y}\phi{}^{f^{n}}_{f_{m}}=\sum_{(R),(R^{-1}),j^{\prime},n^{\prime},n^{\prime\prime}}\big(\pi_{X}\big(R_{(2^{\prime})}\ell(R^{-1})_{(2)}\ell^{-1}\big)\big)^{e^{j^{\prime}}}_{e_{i}}\big(\pi_{Y}\big(R_{(1^{\prime})}\big)\big)^{f^{n^{\prime}}}_{f_{m}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{{}_{X}\phi{}^{e^j}_{e_i}{}_{Y}\phi{}^{f^n}_{f_m}= \sum_{(R),(R^{-1}), j', n',n'' }}{}\times\big(\pi_{Y}(R^{-1})_{(1)}\big)^{f^{n}}_{f_{n^{\prime\prime}}}{}_{X}\phi{}^{e^{j}}_{e_{j^{\prime}}}\star{}_{Y}\phi{}^{f^{n^{\prime\prime}}}_{f_{n^{\prime}}}.

The elements
{}_{X}\phi{}^{e^{j}}_{e_{j^{\prime}}}\star{}_{Y}\phi{}^{f^{n^{\prime\prime}}}_{f_{n^{\prime}}}
being in
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
, they can expressed as linear combinations over
A
of the basis elements
\alpha_{i}
. Also, by Theorem
3.2
(1) the coefficients in this sum belong to
\mathbb{C}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]
. Therefore each coefficient
m^{k}_{ij}
in (
4.16
) belongs to
\mathbb{C}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]
. Since
\mathbb{C}(q)\cap\mathbb{C}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]=\mathbb{C}\big[q,q^{-1}\big]
, this proves the first claim. The arguments of Proposition
4.6
apply as well to
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
, which implies the second claim.∎

Lemma 4.11
.

The action
\operatorname{coad}^{r}
yields on
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
a structure of right
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-module algebra, whence of
U_{A}
-module algebra. Moreover,
\Phi_{1}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\big)
is a full
A
-sublattice of
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\rm lf}
, that we denote by
\tilde{U}_{A}^{\rm lf}
.

Proof.

The first claim is clear, for the action
\operatorname{coad}^{r}
endows the
A
-module
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
with a structure of right
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-module algebra, dual by the (non degenerate) pairing
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}\otimes_{A}U_{A}^{\rm res}\rightarrow A
to the structure defined by
\operatorname{ad}^{r}
on
U_{A}^{\rm res}
. The inclusion
U_{A}\subset U_{A}^{\rm res}
yields the second claim. The third follows from Theorem
4.3
and the fact that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
is a full
A
-sublattice of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
. ∎

5
The example of
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))}

In this section we provide a presentation by generators and relations of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, and using it we give an elementary proof of Theorem
4.3
in this case.

Let
V_{r}
be the
r
-dimensional type
1
simple
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
-module. Put on
V_{2}
the basis vectors
v_{+}
,
v_{-}
such that
K.v_{+}=qv_{+}
,
E.v_{+}=0
,
v_{-}=F.v_{+}
, and define in this basis

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{End}(V_{2})\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2)).

(5.1)

Lemma 5.1
.

The algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is generated by the matrix elements
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}
. These satisfy the relations

\displaystyle ad=da,\qquad

\displaystyle ab-ba=-\big(1-q^{-2}\big)bd,

\displaystyle db=q^{2}bd,\qquad

\displaystyle cb-bc=\big(1-q^{-2}\big)\big(da-d^{2}\big),

(5.2)

\displaystyle cd=q^{2}dc,\qquad

\displaystyle ac-ca=\big(1-q^{-2}\big)dc,

as well as
ad-q^{2}bc=1
. Moreover
\omega=qa+q^{-1}d
is central.

Note that
\omega
coincides with the element (
4.15
).

Proof.

The family of matrix elements of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}
for all type
1

U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
-modules
V
spans
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
over
\mathbb{C}(q)
, since as a vector space it is the same as
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
. Any finite dimensional
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
-module is completely reducible, and any simple one is a direct summand of some tensor power of
V_{2}
. Hence the fusion relation (
4.12
) implies that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is generated by the matrix elements
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
. The relations (
5.2
) follow easily from the reflection equation (
4.13
) associated to
V_{2}
, using the expression

R_{V_{2},V_{2}}=q^{-1/2}\begin{pmatrix}q&0&0&0\\
0&1&q-q^{-1}&0\\
0&0&1&0\\
0&0&0&q\end{pmatrix}\!,

and they imply that
qa+q^{-1}d
is central.
Because
V_{2}\otimes V_{2}
admits the trivial representations as a subrepresentation, there exist non zero intertwiners

\phi\colon\ V_{2}\otimes V_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(q),\qquad\psi\colon\ \mathbb{C}(q)\rightarrow V_{2}\otimes V_{2}.

As a consequence
\phi\ \circ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}\otimes V_{2}}}{{M}}\circ\psi
is proportional to the unit element of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
. Using the fusion relation, an easy computation provides the additional relation
ad-q^{2}bc=1
.
∎

Consider the RSD map
\Phi_{1}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\rightarrow\mathbb{U}_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
,
\alpha\mapsto(\alpha\otimes{\rm id})(RR^{\prime})
. A straightforward computation using the expression (
3.7
) shows that

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(a)=K+q^{-1}\big(q-q^{-1}\big)^{2}FE,\qquad

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(b)=q^{-1}\big(q-q^{-1}\big)F,

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(c)=\big(q-q^{-1}\big)K^{-1}E,\qquad

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(d)=K^{-1}.

(5.3)

Therefore
\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{1})
is contained in
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
. The image of the central element
\omega
is

\displaystyle\Omega=\Phi_{1}(\omega)=qK+q^{-1}K^{-1}+\big(q-q^{-1}\big)^{2}FE,

(5.4)

which is
(q-q^{-1})^{2}
times the standard Casimir element of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
.

Next we show that the relations (
5.2
) and
ad-q^{2}bc=1
yield a presentation of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
.
Let
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
be the algebra generated by elements
\tilde{a}
,
\tilde{b}
,
\tilde{c}
,
\tilde{d}
satisfying all these relations. Denote by

j\colon\ \tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}\to{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))

the unique morphism of algebra sending
\tilde{x}
to
x
for
x\in\{a,b,c,d\}.

Proposition 5.2
.

The monomials
\tilde{a}^{\alpha}\tilde{b}^{\beta}\tilde{c}^{\gamma}
and
\tilde{d}^{\delta}\tilde{b}^{\beta}\tilde{c}^{\gamma}
, where
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\geq 1
, form a basis of
\tilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0,1}
over
\mathbb{C}(q)
. Moreover,
j
is an isomorphism,
\Phi_{1}
is injective, and the center of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is the polynomial algebra
\mathbb{C}(q)[\omega]
.

Proof.

By inspection of the relations of Lemma
5.1
, it is easily seen that the given monomials form a generating set of
\tilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0,1}
. As for linear independence, consider the Verma
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
-module
M_{x}
,
x\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
, with basis vectors
v_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and action

Ev_{0}=0,\qquad Kv_{n}=xq^{-2n}v_{n},\qquad Fv_{n}=v_{n+1}.

Then

Ev_{n}=[n]_{q}\frac{xq^{1-n}-x^{-1}q^{n-1}}{q-q^{-1}}v_{n-1},\qquad\Omega v_{n}=\big(qx+q^{-1}x^{-1}\big)v_{n},

where
\Omega
is as above. By the formulas (
5.3
) we have

\Phi_{1}\big(a^{\alpha}b^{\beta}c^{\gamma}\big)=q^{-\alpha-\beta}\big(q-q^{-1}\big)^{\beta+\gamma}\big(\Omega-q^{-1}K^{-1}\big)^{\alpha}F^{\beta}\big(K^{-1}E\big)^{\gamma}

and

\Phi_{1}\big(d^{\delta}b^{\beta}c^{\gamma}\big)=q^{-\beta}\big(q-q^{-1}\big)^{\beta+\gamma}K^{-\delta}F^{\beta}\big(K^{-1}E\big)^{\gamma}.

Consider a linear relation with coefficients
A_{\alpha\beta\gamma},B_{\delta\beta^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
:

\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}A_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\tilde{a}^{\alpha}\tilde{b}^{\beta}\tilde{c}^{\gamma}+\sum_{\delta,\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}B_{\delta\beta^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}\tilde{d}^{\delta}\tilde{b}^{\beta^{\prime}}\tilde{c}^{\gamma^{\prime}}=0.

Applying
\Phi_{1}\circ j
we get (keeping the same names for the resulting coefficients in
\mathbb{C}(q)
):

\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}\big(A_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\big(\Omega-q^{-1}K^{-1}\big)^{\alpha}F^{\beta}\big(K^{-1}E\big)^{\gamma}+B_{\delta\beta^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}K^{-\delta}F^{\beta^{\prime}}\big(K^{-1}E\big)^{\gamma^{\prime}}\big)=0.

(5.5)

By acting on the highest weight vector
v_{0}
all terms on the left hand side vanish, but those with
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}=0
. Hence

\sum_{\alpha,\beta}A_{\alpha\beta 0}\big(\Omega-q^{-1}K^{-1}\big)^{\alpha}F^{\beta}v_{0}+\sum_{\delta\beta^{\prime}}B_{\delta\beta^{\prime}0}K^{-\delta}F^{\beta^{\prime}}v_{0}=0

implying for each
\beta
the relation

\sum_{\alpha}A_{\alpha\beta 0}\big(qx+q^{-1}x^{-1}\big(1-q^{2\beta}\big)\big)^{\alpha}+\sum_{\delta}B_{\delta\beta 0}x^{-\delta}q^{2\beta\delta}=0.

This is a Laurent polynomial in
x
. Since
\delta\geq 1
, the highest degree term in
x
has vanishing coefficient,
A_{\alpha\beta 0}=0
, and hence
B_{\delta\beta 0}=0
. So (
5.5
) has no terms with
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}=0
. Then, by acting on
v_{1}
it results again that (
5.5
) has no term with
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}=1
. Iterating this argument, an obvious recurrence implies that all the coefficients vanish,
A_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=B_{\delta\beta^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}=0
, which implies the linear independence of the monomials
\tilde{a}^{\alpha}\tilde{b}^{\beta}\tilde{c}^{\gamma}
and
\tilde{d}^{\delta}\tilde{b}^{\beta}\tilde{c}^{\gamma}
and therefore proves that they form a basis of
\tilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0,1}
.

As a by-product we see that
\Phi_{1}\circ j
is injective, and therefore
j
is injective too. It is also surjective because the monomials
{a}^{\alpha}{b}^{\beta}{c}^{\gamma}
and
{d}^{\delta}{b}^{\beta}c^{\gamma}
form a generating family of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
. It follows that
\Phi_{1}
is injective as well.

Finally, let
z
be in the center of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
. Then
\Phi_{1}(z)
commutes with
\Phi_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2)))
, and hence with
K
,
E
,
F
. Thus it belongs to the center of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, which is
\mathbb{C}(q)[\Omega]
. Therefore

\Phi_{1}(z)=P(\Omega)=P(\Phi_{1}(\omega))=\Phi_{1}(P(\omega)).
The result follows from the injectivity of
\Phi_{1}
.∎

Remark 5.3
.

A consequence of this proposition is that the algebras
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
and
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
are not isomorphic. Indeed the former has the family of one dimensional representations
\rho_{xy}
(
x\in\mathbb{C}
,
y\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
), defined by

\rho_{xy}(d)=0,\qquad\rho_{xy}(a)=x,\qquad\rho_{xy}(b)=-q^{-1}y,\qquad\rho_{xy}(c)=q^{-1}y^{-1}.

Then
K\notin\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{1})
, for otherwise
K=\Phi_{1}(\lambda)
, and injectivity of
\Phi_{1}
and
K^{-1}=\Phi_{1}(d)
would imply
\lambda d=d\lambda=1
. But this is not possible since
\rho_{xy}(d)=0
. In fact the family
\{\rho_{xy}\}_{x,y}
cannot be obtained by pull-back of representations of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
and the category of finite dimensional modules of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is not semisimple (see
[
21
, Proposition 9]
).

From the above results we can now derive an easy proof of the particular case of Theorem
4.3
for
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
. Note that
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))=U_{q}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
by the result of
[
52
]
recalled in Remark
4.5
. Also, the inclusion
U_{q}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\subset U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is strict, for
K\notin U_{q}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
.

Proposition 5.4
.

The RSD map yields an isomorphism of
U_{q}
-module algebras

\Phi_{1}\colon\ {\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\rightarrow U_{q}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2)).

Proof.

That
\Phi_{1}
is an equivariant morphism follows from the same arguments as for the Alekseev map (see Theorem
6.6
). Injectivity was shown in Proposition
5.2
. We prove that
\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{1})=U_{q}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
by following closely the arguments of Section 3.11 of Joseph–Letzter
[
52
]
. For every integer
m\geq 0
we have

\operatorname{ad}^{r}(K)\big(\big(EK^{-1}\big)^{m}\big)=q^{-2m}\big(EK^{-1}\big)^{m},\qquad\operatorname{ad}^{r}(E)\big(\big(EK^{-1}\big)^{m}\big)=0.

(5.6)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\operatorname{ad}^{r}(F)\big(EK^{-1}\big)=\frac{q}{q-q^{-1}}\big(\Omega-(q+q^{-1})K^{-1}\big),\qquad\operatorname{ad}^{r}\big(F^{2}\big)\big(EK^{-1}\big)=-\big(q+q^{-1}\big)F,

\displaystyle\operatorname{ad}^{r}(F^{3})\big(EK^{-1}\big)=0,\qquad\operatorname{ad}^{r}(E)\big(K^{-1}\big)=EK^{-1}\big(q^{-2}-1\big),

\displaystyle\operatorname{ad}^{r}(F)\big(K^{-1}\big)=F\big(1-q^{-2}\big),\qquad\operatorname{ad}^{r}(E)(F)=-\operatorname{ad}^{r}(F)\big(EK^{-1}\big),\qquad\operatorname{ad}^{r}(F)(F)=0.

These relations imply that
EK^{-1}
,
K^{-1}
and
F
belong to
U_{q}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
. Because
\Omega
is invariant under the action
\operatorname{ad}^{r}
,
the formulas (
5.3
) imply that
\Phi_{1}(a)
,
\Phi_{1}(b)
,
\Phi_{1}(c)
and
\Phi_{1}(d)
belong to
U_{q}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
. Therefore
\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{1})\subset U_{q}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
.

These above relations imply also that
\Phi_{1}(c)
is a highest weight vector for the action
\operatorname{ad}^{r}
, generating a copy of
V_{3}
, the
3
-dimensional simple
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
-module of type
1
.

Let us show by induction that
\operatorname{ad}^{r}(U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2)))\big(\Phi_{1}(c)^{m}\big)=V_{2m+1}
, for an arbitrary positive integer
m
. In view of (
5.6
), it remains to prove that
\operatorname{ad}^{r}\big(F^{k}\big)\big(\big(EK^{-1}\big)^{m}\big)\neq 0
for all integers
k\leq 2m
, and
\operatorname{ad}^{r}\big(F^{k}\big)\big(\big(EK^{-1}\big)^{m}\big)=0
for
k\geq 2m+1.
This holds true for
m=1
.

If this is true for a given
m
, then using the formula of
\Delta(F)
, the
q
-binomial identity, and the fact that
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is an
\operatorname{ad}^{r}
-module algebra, we get

\displaystyle\operatorname{ad}^{r}\big(F^{k}\big)\big(\big(EK^{-1}\big)^{m+1}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\left[\begin{matrix}k\\
i\end{matrix}\right]_{q^{-2}}\operatorname{ad}^{r}\big(K^{-i}F^{k-i}\big)\big(\big(EK^{-1}\big)^{m}\big)\operatorname{ad}^{r}\big(F^{i}\big)\big(EK^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{2}\left[\begin{matrix}k\\
i\end{matrix}\right]_{q^{-2}}\operatorname{ad}^{r}\big(K^{-i}F^{k-i}\big)\big(\big(EK^{-1}\big)^{m}\big)\operatorname{ad}^{r}\big(F^{i}\big)\big(EK^{-1}\big),

since
\operatorname{ad}^{r}\big(F^{i}\big)\big(EK^{-1}\big)=0
if and only if
i\geq 3
. By the induction hypothesis, for
k\geq 2m+3
each of the three terms of the sum vanishes. For
k=2m+2
, only the term for
i=2
is non zero, which is equal to

\left[\begin{matrix}k\\
2\end{matrix}\right]_{q^{-2}}\operatorname{ad}^{r}\big(K^{-2}F^{2m}\big)\big(\big(EK^{-1}\big)^{m}\big)\big({-}\big(q+q^{-1}\big)F\big).

By induction this term is non zero. Therefore
\operatorname{ad}^{r}(F^{k})\big(\big(EK^{-1}\big)^{m}\big)\neq 0
for all integers
k\leq 2m+2.
This proves our claim.

Finally the multiplication map
\left(\oplus_{m\geq 0}V_{2m+1}\right)\otimes\mathbb{C}(q)[\Omega]\longrightarrow U_{q}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is easily shown to be an isomorphism, as in
[
52
, Section 3.11]
. Since
\Omega=\Phi_{1}(\omega)
, and
V_{2m+1}
is generated by
\Phi_{1}(c^{2m})
, we deduce the inclusion
U_{q}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\subset\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{1})
.∎

Remark 5.5
.

If we denote
{\mathbb{H}}=\oplus_{m\geq 0}V_{2m+1},
the isomorphism of modules

{\mathbb{H}}\otimes\mathbb{C}(q)[\Omega]\longrightarrow U_{q}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))

is an explicit example of the theorem of separation of variables of
[
13
,
53
]
in the case of
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
(this case was first described in
[
52
]
). As shown in these works, the multiplicity
[{\mathbb{H}},V_{k}]
for
k\geq 1
is the dimension (
0
or
1
) of the set of zero weight vectors of
V_{k}
.

Let us make explicit the result of
[
53
]
mentionned in Remark
4.5
. By the relations (
5.2
) and Proposition
5.2
, it is immediate that for all
x\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
there exist elements
y
,
y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
such that
dy=xd
and
y^{\prime}d=dx
. The element
d
is regular because
\Phi_{1}(d)
is invertible and
\Phi_{1}
is injective, so
\{d^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a
left and right multiplicative Ore set
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
. The localization of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
over
\{d^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is well-defined (see
[
49
, Theorem 1.9 and Corollary 6.4]
). Let us denote it by
{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
.

Proposition 5.6
.

The map
\Phi_{1}\colon{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\to U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
defined by
\Phi_{1}\big(d^{-1}\big)=K
and the formulas (
5.3
) is an isomorphism of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
-module algebras.

Proof.

Since
\Phi_{1}(d)
is invertible,
\Phi_{1}
extends to a morphism
{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\to U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
uniquely (see
[
49
, Proposition 6.3]
). It is an isomorphism, because the monomials
d^{\delta}b^{\beta}c^{\gamma}
with
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta\in\mathbb{Z}
make a basis of
{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, and they are sent by
\Phi_{1}
to a PBW basis of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
.
∎

Finally consider the integral form
{\mathcal{L}}^{A}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
.

Lemma 5.7
.

The following holds:

1.

The
A
-algebra
{\mathcal{L}}^{A}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is generated by
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
with the defining relations (
5.2
).

2.

The localization of
{\mathcal{L}}^{A}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
over the set
\{d^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, that we denote by
{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}^{A}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, is generated by
a
,
b
,
c
,
d^{\pm 1}
with the defining relations (
5.2
).

3.

The RSD map yields embeddings of
U_{A}
-module algebras
\Phi_{1}\colon{\mathcal{L}}^{A}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\rightarrow U_{A}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
and
\Phi_{1}\colon{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}^{A}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\rightarrow U_{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, and
\Phi_{1}\big({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}^{A}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big)=U_{A}^{\prime}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is the
A
-subalgebra
U_{A}^{\prime}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\subset U_{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, generated by
\big(q-q^{-1}\big)E
,
\big(q-q^{-1}\big)F
and
K^{\pm 1}
.

Proof.

(1) It is shown in
[
29
]
, Proposition 1.3, that
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is the
A
-subalgebra generated by
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
. Since
{\mathcal{L}}^{A}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
coincides with
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
as an
A
-module, any element of
{\mathcal{L}}^{A}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is a linear combination over
A
of monomials in
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
with respect to the product
\star
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
. By the relation (
4.6
) and Proposition
4.10
, any such monomial is a linear combination over
A
of monomials in
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
with respect to the product (
4.4
) of
{\mathcal{L}}^{A}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
. This implies the first claim. The second follows from the fact that the relations (
5.2
) are defined over
A
. The assertion (2) is an immediate consequence of (1), and (3) follows from Lemma
4.11
. We use the formulas (
5.3
) to determine
\Phi_{1}\big({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}^{A}_{0,1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big)
.∎

6
The “daisy” graph algebra
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}(\mathfrak{g})}

6.1
Definition and first properties

We define
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
as a
twisted product
of
n
copies of the
U_{q}\otimes U_{q}^{\rm cop}
-module algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
(see Proposition
4.1
), following
[
32
]
(but using right modules instead of left ones). Since the twist uses
R
-matrices, as for
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
the construction uses as well categorical completions. Eventually we will see that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
is also the braided tensor product of
n
copies of the
U_{q}
-module algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
(see, e.g.,
[
69
]
for the notion of braided tensor product).

We need to recall a few notions. Consider Hopf algebras
A
and
B
, and a
bicharacter

F\in B\otimes A
. By definition,
F
is an invertible tensor and satisfies

(\Delta_{B}\otimes{\rm id}_{A})(F)=F_{23}F_{13},\qquad({\rm id}_{B}\otimes\Delta_{A})(F)=F_{12}F_{13}.

(6.1)

Viewing
F
as an element of
(1\otimes B)\otimes(A\otimes 1)\subset(A\otimes B)^{\otimes 2}
, it is readily checked that

F_{12}(\Delta_{A\otimes B}\otimes{\rm id})(F)=F_{23}({\rm id}\otimes\Delta_{A\otimes B})(F)

and

(\varepsilon_{A\otimes B}\otimes{\rm id})(F)=({\rm id}\otimes\varepsilon_{A\otimes B})(F)=1.

Therefore
F
is a twist of
A\otimes B
, endowed with the standard Hopf algebra product structure (see (
4.3
) for the similar operation applied to
{\mathbb{D}}_{q}
). Denote by
A\otimes^{F}B
the resulting structure of Hopf algebra. Given a right
A
-module algebra
M
, and a right
B
-module algebra
N
, one defines the twisted tensor product
M\otimes^{F}N
as the space
M\otimes N
endowed with the product

(\alpha\otimes\beta)(\alpha^{\prime}\otimes\beta^{\prime})=\sum_{(F)}\alpha(\alpha^{\prime}\cdot F_{(2)})\otimes(\beta\cdot F_{(1)})\beta^{\prime}

for every
\alpha,\beta\in M
,
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in N
. This product gives
M\otimes^{F}N
a structure of associative and unital right module algebra over
A\otimes^{F}B
, containing
M\otimes^{F}1
and
1\otimes^{F}N
as
A
- and
B
-module algebras respectively.

Remark 6.1
.

In the case where
A=B
is quasi-triangular with
R
-matrix
R
, and
F=R^{\prime}
, this construction gives the braided tensor product of
A
-module algebras.

These constructions generalizes straightforwardly to the categorical completions we consider. Taking
A=B=\mathbb{U}_{q}
, the identities (
2.2
) for the universal
R
-matrix imply that
R^{\prime}
is a bicharacter of
\mathbb{U}_{q}{\otimes}\mathbb{U}_{q}
, and that we have (compare with (
4.2
))

\mathbb{A}_{q}=\mathbb{U}_{q}{\otimes}^{R^{\prime}}\mathbb{U}_{q}.

We are going to iterate the above twist constructions. To this aim, observe that given homomorphisms of Hopf algebras
f_{A}\colon\mathbb{U}_{q}\rightarrow A
,
f_{B}\colon\mathbb{U}_{q}\rightarrow B
, the element
(f_{B}\otimes f_{A})(R^{\prime})
is a bicharacter of
A\otimes B
, and by Proposition
4.2
the map

f_{A}\odot f_{B}:=(f_{A}\otimes f_{B})\circ\Delta\colon\ \mathbb{U}_{q}\rightarrow A\otimes^{(f_{B}\otimes f_{A})(R^{\prime})}B

is a morphism of Hopf algebras. Because of (
6.1
), taking
H:=A=B
the operation
\odot
endows the set of all morphisms of Hopf algebras
\mathbb{U}_{q}\rightarrow H
with a structure of associative monoid. One checks easily that it has the counit as neutral element. Clearly,
\odot
is natural in the sense that
(g_{A}\circ f_{A})\odot(g_{B}\circ f_{B})=(g_{A}\otimes g_{B})\circ(f_{A}\odot f_{B})
for any morphisms of Hopf algebras
g_{A}\colon A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
,
g_{B}\colon B\rightarrow B^{\prime}
.

In particular, by using the family of morphisms
{\rm id}_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}^{\odot n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define Hopf algebras
\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\odot n}
as follows:

\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\odot 0}=\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D}),\qquad\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\odot 1}=\mathbb{U}_{q}

and for
n\geq 1
,

\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\odot(n+1)}:=\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\odot n}{\otimes}^{J_{n}}\mathbb{U}_{q},\qquad{\rm where}\quad J_{n}:=\big({\rm id}_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}\otimes{\rm id}_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}^{\odot n}\big)(R^{\prime}).

Therefore, an immediate induction shows that
\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\odot n}
is the twist of
\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
by
\prod_{k=n-1}^{1}J_{k}
. Because of (
6.1
), for every
k
,
l\in\mathbb{N}
we have an equality of Hopf algebras

\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\odot(k+l)}=\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\odot{k}}\odot\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\odot{l}}.

(6.2)

For instance, since
{\mathbb{A}}_{q}=\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\odot 2}
and
{\rm id}_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}^{\odot 2}=\Delta
, the Hopf algebra
{\mathbb{A}}_{q}^{\odot 2}=\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\odot 4}
is the twist of
{\mathbb{A}}_{q}{\otimes}\mathbb{A}_{q}
by

F_{2}:=\big({\rm id}_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}^{\odot 2}\odot{\rm id}_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}^{\odot 2}\big)(R^{\prime})=({\Delta}{\otimes}{\Delta})(R^{\prime})=R^{\prime}_{23}R^{\prime}_{13}R^{\prime}_{24}R^{\prime}_{14}\in\mathbb{A}_{q}{\otimes}\mathbb{A}_{q}.

(6.3)

We are mainly concerned with the Hopf algebras
\mathbb{A}_{q}^{\odot n}=\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\odot 2n}
. Denote by
F_{n}
the twist from
\mathbb{A}_{q}^{\otimes n}
to
\mathbb{A}_{q}^{\odot n}
.

We define
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
as the twist by
F_{n}
of the right
\mathbb{A}_{q}^{\otimes n}
-module algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}\big(q^{1/D}\big)^{\otimes n}
(endowed with the componentwise action and product). By construction
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
is a right
\mathbb{A}_{q}^{\odot n}
-module algebra, and it coincides with
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}\big(q^{1/D}\big)^{\otimes n}
as a
\mathbb{A}_{q}^{\odot n}
-module.

The above definition of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
by induction can be formulated by means of exchange relations between its factors
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
. Let us explain this. For every
k
,
l
the right
\mathbb{A}_{q}^{\odot n}
-module algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,k}\big(q^{1/D}\big)\odot{\mathcal{L}}_{0,l}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
is the twist by
\textstyle F(l,k)=({\Delta}^{\odot l}\otimes{\Delta}^{\odot k})(R^{\prime})
of the
\mathbb{A}_{q}^{\odot k}{\otimes}\mathbb{A}_{q}^{\odot l}
-module algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,k}\big(q^{1/D}\big)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,l}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
. By associativity of
{\odot}
(see (
6.2
)) we have

{\mathcal{L}}_{0,k}\big(q^{1/D}\big)\odot{\mathcal{L}}_{0,l}\big(q^{1/D}\big)={\mathcal{L}}_{0,k+l}\big(q^{1/D}\big).

Under this factorisation, by setting
k+l=n
, for every
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,k}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
and
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,l}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
the product of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
takes the form

(\alpha\otimes\beta)(\alpha^{\prime}\otimes\beta^{\prime})=\sum_{(F(l,k))}\alpha(\alpha^{\prime}\cdot F(l,k)_{(2)})\otimes(\beta\cdot F(l,k)_{(1)})\beta^{\prime},

(6.4)

where
the products
\alpha(\alpha^{\prime}\cdot F(l,k)_{(2)})
and
(\beta\cdot F(l,k)_{(1)})\beta^{\prime}
are taken in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,k}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,l}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
respectively. Moreover, for every
1\leq a\leq n
the map
\mathfrak{i}_{a}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}\big(q^{1/D}\big)\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
, identifying
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
with the
a
-th factor of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}\big(q^{1/D}\big)^{\otimes n}
by putting
1
’s elsewhere
(
1
being the unit of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
)
, is an embedding of module algebras. We will use the notations

{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\big(q^{1/D}\big)^{(a)}:=\operatorname{Im}(\mathfrak{i}_{a}),\qquad(\alpha)^{(a)}:=\mathfrak{i}_{a}(\alpha).

Taking
(\alpha)^{(a)}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\big(q^{1/D}\big)^{(a)}
and
(\beta)^{(b)}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\big(q^{1/D}\big)^{(b)}
with
a<b
, and
F=F(1,1)\in\mathbb{A}_{q}{\otimes}\mathbb{A}_{q}
as in (
6.3
), we deduce from (
6.4
) that
(
all products being taken in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
)

(\beta)^{(b)}(\alpha)^{(a)}=\sum_{(F)}\big(\alpha\cdot F_{(2)}\big)^{(a)}\big(\beta\cdot F_{(1)}\big)^{(b)}.

(6.5)

We have:

Proposition 6.2
.

The subspace
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\otimes n}\subset{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
endowed with the product defined by (
4.4
) on each component and by the exchange relation (
6.5
) between distinct components is a right module
\mathbb{C}(q)
-algebra over
\big(U_{q}\otimes U_{q}^{\rm cop}\big)^{\odot n}\subset\mathbb{A}_{q}^{\odot n}
. We denote it
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
. Moreover,
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
is a
U_{q}
-module algebra, namely the braided tensor product of
n
copies of the
U_{q}
-module algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
, with
U_{q}
-action, that we still denote by
\operatorname{coad}^{r}
, given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)\big(\alpha^{(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha^{(n)}\big)=\sum_{(y)}\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y_{(1)})\big(\alpha^{(1)}\big)\otimes\dots\otimes\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y_{(n)})\big(\alpha^{(n)}\big)

(6.6)

for all
y\in U_{q}
and
\alpha^{(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha^{(n)}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
.

Proof.

We have to show that the product (
6.5
) restricted to
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\otimes n}\subset{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
is defined over the subfield
\mathbb{C}(q)
. Indeed, the injection
\mathfrak{i}_{a}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
being a morphism of algebras, the product of elements
\alpha,\beta\in\mathfrak{i}_{a}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1})
is defined over
\mathbb{C}(q)
(see Proposition
4.1
). Therefore it is enough to check that the exchange relation (
6.5
) is defined over
\mathbb{C}(q)
as well. We proceed as in (
4.7
) and (
4.8
). For matrix coefficients
\big({}_{V_{\lambda}}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}
and
\big({}_{V_{\mu}}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}
, where
a<b
, (
6.5
) can be written as

\displaystyle\big({}_{V_{\mu}}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}\big({}_{V_{\lambda}}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(R^{1})\cdots(R^{4})}\big(\big({}_{V_{\lambda}}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}\cdot\big(R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(1)}\otimes R^{3}_{(1)}R^{4}_{(1)}\big)\big)\big(\big({}_{V_{\mu}}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}\cdot\big(R^{2}_{(2)}R^{4}_{(2)}\otimes R^{1}_{(2)}R^{3}_{(2)}\big)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(R^{1})\cdots(R^{4})}\!\!\!\big(S\big(R^{3}_{(1)}R^{4}_{(1)}\big)\!\rhd\big({}_{V_{\lambda}}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}\!\lhd R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(1)}\big)\big(S\big(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{3}_{(2)}\big)\!\rhd\big({}_{V_{\mu}}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}\!\lhd R^{2}_{(2)}R^{4}_{(2)}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(R^{1})\cdots(R^{4}),i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}\pi_{V_{\lambda}}\big(R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(1)}\big)^{e^{i}}_{e_{i^{\prime}}}\ \pi_{V_{\lambda}}\big(S\big(R^{3}_{(1)}R^{4}_{(1)}\big)\big)^{e^{j^{\prime}}}_{e_{j}}

\displaystyle\qquad\hphantom{=\sum_{(R^{1})\cdots(R^{4}),i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}}{}\times\pi_{V_{\mu}}\big(R^{2}_{(2)}R^{4}_{(2)}\big)^{e^{k}}_{e_{k^{\prime}}}\pi_{V_{\mu}}\big(S\big(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{3}_{(2)}\big)\big)^{e^{l^{\prime}}}_{e_{l}}\ \big({}_{V_{\lambda}}\phi^{e^{i^{\prime}}}_{e_{j^{\prime}}}\big)^{(a)}\ \big({}_{V_{\mu}}\phi^{e^{k^{\prime}}}_{e_{l^{\prime}}}\big)^{(b)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(R^{1})\cdots(R^{4}),i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}\pi_{V_{\lambda}}\big(R^{1}_{(1)}\!\!{}^{-1}R^{2}_{(1)}\big)^{e^{i}}_{e_{i^{\prime}}}\ \pi_{V_{\lambda}}\big(R^{4}_{(1)}\!\!{}^{-1}R^{3}_{(1)}\big)^{e^{j^{\prime}}}_{e_{j}}

\displaystyle\qquad\hphantom{=\sum_{(R^{1})\cdots(R^{4}),i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}}{}\times\pi_{V_{\mu}}\big(R^{2}_{(2)}R^{4}_{(2)}\!\!{}^{-1}\big)^{e^{k}}_{e_{k^{\prime}}}\pi_{V_{\mu}}\big(R^{3}_{(2)}\ell R^{1}_{(2)}\!\!{}^{-1}\ell^{-1}\big)^{e^{l^{\prime}}}_{e_{l}}\ \big({}_{V_{\lambda}}\phi^{e^{i^{\prime}}}_{e_{j^{\prime}}}\big)^{(a)}\big({}_{V_{\mu}}\phi^{e^{k^{\prime}}}_{e_{l^{\prime}}}\big)^{(b)},

where we denote by
R^{1},\dots,R^{4}
the four
R
-matrices in (
6.3
),
R^{i}=\sum_{(R)}R_{(1)}^{i}\otimes R_{(2)}^{i}
as usual, and
\ell
is the pivotal element.
Since the matrix entries of
R_{V_{\lambda},V_{\mu}}^{\pm 1}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})}(V_{\lambda}\otimes V_{\mu})
belong to
q^{\pm(\lambda,\mu)}\mathbb{C}(q)
(see Theorem
3.2
(2) for the stronger integral statement), the factors
q^{+(\lambda,\mu)}
cancel the factors
q^{-(\lambda,\mu)}
in the last expression. The matrix entries of
\ell
belong to
A
, so we finally obtain that the coefficients in the sum belong to
\mathbb{C}(q)
.

Finally, as for the action of
U_{q}
on
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
, consider the structure of
U_{q}
-module algebra on
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
obtained by pulling-back the action of
\big(U_{q}\otimes U_{q}^{\rm cop}\big)
via
\Delta
(see the comment after Proposition
4.2
). It follows from Remark
6.1
that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}={\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\odot n}
is the braided tensor of
n
copies of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
. The restriction of the action of
U_{q}
on each factor
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
being
\operatorname{coad}^{r}
, the formula (
6.6
) is a consequence of the properties of actions of module algebras.
∎

The commutation relation (
6.5
) yields a presentation of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
by generators and relations. This presentation is well-known (see
[
2
,
19
,
32
,
70
]
), but for completeness we prefer to give a proof. Let
V
be an object of the category
{\mathcal{C}}
. Similarly to (
4.10
), define

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}=\sum_{i,j}E_{i}^{j}\otimes\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}=\sum_{i,j}E_{i}^{j}\otimes\big(1^{\otimes(a-1)}\otimes({}_{V}\phi{}^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}})\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-a)}\big)\in\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}.

Note that the matrix coefficients of the set of matrices
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}
, for every object
V
of
\mathcal{C}
and
1\leq a\leq n
, generate the algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
.

Proposition 6.3
.

For every
a<b
the matrices
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}
satisfy the fusion equation (
4.12
), the naturality relations (
4.11
), and the following
exchange relation

(
in
\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})

{R}_{V,W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M_{1}}}{}^{\!\!\!(a)}{R}_{V,W}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle W}}{{M_{2}}}{}^{\!\!\!(b)}=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle W}}{{M_{2}}}{}^{\!\!(b)}{R}_{V,W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M_{1}}}{}^{\!\!\!(a)}R_{V,W}^{-1}.

(6.7)

Moreover, all these relations determine the product of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
. Hence the algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
can be viewed as the quotient of the algebra freely generated over
\mathbb{C}(q)
by the matrix coefficients
\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}
, for all objects
V
of
\mathcal{C}
and every
1\leq a\leq n
, by the ideal generated by the relations (
4.11
), (
4.12
) and (
6.7
).

Proof.

The fusion and naturality relations follow from the fact that
\mathfrak{i}_{a}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
is a morphism of algebras. The matrix coefficients
\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}
generate the subalgebras
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(a)}
, whence
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
too. Conversely, by Proposition
4.6
the fusion and naturality relations determine the product of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(a)}
. Hence it is enough to show that the exchange relations (
6.7
) and the commutation relation (
6.5
) are equivalent. Let us write
\textstyle R_{V,W}=\sum_{(R)}R_{(1)}\otimes R_{(2)}=\sum_{(R)}R_{(1^{\prime})}\otimes R_{(2^{\prime})}
. Then (
6.7
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\sum_{(R),(R),i,j,k,l}R_{(1)}E_{i}^{j}S\big(R_{(1^{\prime})}\big)\otimes R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}E_{k}^{l}\otimes\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}\big({}_{W}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(R),(R),i,j,k,l}R_{(1)}E_{i}^{j}S\big(R_{(1^{\prime})}\big)\otimes E_{k}^{l}R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}\otimes\big({}_{W}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}.

The isomorphism
V\otimes V^{*}\rightarrow\operatorname{End}(V)
,
v\otimes f\mapsto(w\mapsto f(w)v)
, maps
R_{(1)}e_{i}\otimes S\big(R_{(1^{\prime})}\big)^{*}e^{j}
to
R_{(1)}E_{i}^{j}S\big(R_{(1^{\prime})}\big)
,
R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}e_{k}\otimes e^{l}
to
R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}E_{k}^{l}
, and
e_{k}\otimes(R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})})^{*}e^{l}
to
E_{k}^{l}R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}
. Hence the above relation can be written as

\displaystyle\sum_{(R),(R),i,j,k,l}E_{i}^{j}\otimes E_{k}^{l}\otimes\Big({}_{V}\phi^{R_{(1)}^{*}e^{i}}_{S(R_{(1^{\prime})})e_{j}}\Big)^{(a)}\Big({}_{W}\phi^{(R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})})^{*}e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\Big)^{(b)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(R),(R),i,j,k,l}E_{i}^{j}\otimes E_{k}^{l}\otimes\big({}_{W}\phi^{e^{k}}_{R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}\Big({}_{V}\phi^{R_{(1)}^{*}e^{i}}_{S(R_{(1^{\prime})})e_{j}}\Big)^{(a)}.

Now we have

\displaystyle{}_{V}\phi^{R_{(1)}^{*}e^{i}}_{S(R_{(1^{\prime})})e_{j}}=S\big(R_{(1^{\prime})}\big)\rhd{}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\lhd R_{(1)}={}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\cdot\big(R_{(1)}\otimes R_{(1^{\prime})}\big),

\displaystyle{}_{W}\phi^{(R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})})^{*}e^{k}}_{e_{l}}={}_{W}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\lhd R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}={}_{W}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\cdot\big(R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}\otimes 1\big),

\displaystyle{}_{W}\phi^{e^{k}}_{R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}e_{l}}=R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}\rhd{}_{W}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}={}_{W}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\cdot\big(1\otimes S^{-1}\big(R_{(2)}R_{(2^{\prime})}\big)\big),

where we use the coregular actions
\rhd,\lhd
and on the right hand sides we denote by
R_{(1)}
,
R_{(1^{\prime})}
,
R_{(2)}
,
R_{(2^{\prime})}\in\mathbb{U}_{q}
the components of the universal R-matrix (instead of
R_{V,W}
). Using that
(S\otimes S)(R)=R
, and denoting by
m
the product map of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
, the above relation eventually becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j,k,l}E_{i}^{j}\otimes E_{k}^{l}\otimes m\big(\big(\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}\otimes\big({}_{W}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}\big)\cdot(R_{13}R_{23})\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{i,j,k,l}E_{i}^{j}\otimes E_{k}^{l}\otimes m\big(\big(\big({}_{W}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}\otimes\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}\big)\cdot(R_{24}^{-}R_{23}^{-})\big).

Identifying terms we get the commutation relation

\displaystyle\big({}_{W}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}

\displaystyle=m\big(\big(\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}\otimes\big({}_{W}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}\big)\cdot(R_{14}R_{24}R_{13}R_{23})\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(F)}\big(\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}\cdot F_{(2)}\big)\big(\big({}_{W}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}\cdot F_{(1)}\big),

(6.8)

where
\textstyle F=R^{\prime}_{23}R^{\prime}_{13}R^{\prime}_{24}R^{\prime}_{14}=\sigma_{12,34}(R_{14}R_{24}R_{13}R_{23})
. This is the same as (
6.5
), so it is equivalent to the exchange relation. ∎

Let us denote
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}_{ij}:={}_{V}\phi^{e^{j}}_{e_{i}}
. The following proposition gives a formulation of the action
\operatorname{coad}^{r}
in (
6.6
) on these matrix coefficients.

Proposition 6.4
.

For all
1\leq a\leq n
we have:

\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}_{ij}\!{}^{(a)}\big)=\sum_{(y),k,l}\pi_{V}(y_{(1)})_{ik}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}_{kl}\!{}^{(a)}\pi_{V}\big(S(y_{(2)})_{lj}\big)

(6.9)

also written as

\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}\!{}^{(a)}\big)=\sum_{(y)}\big(y_{(1)_{V}}\otimes{\rm id}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}\!{}^{(a)}\big(S(y_{(2)})_{V}\otimes{\rm id}\big).

Proof.

The formula (
6.9
) extends uniquely to any product of matrices
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}\!{}^{(a)}
by the defining property of actions of module algebras. That it is equivalent to (
6.6
) is immediate from the definition of the coregular actions. ∎

Remark 6.5
.

The fact that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
is a right
U_{q}
-module algebra (see Proposition
6.2
) follows also by verifying that the ideal generated by its defining relations (
4.11
), (
4.12
) and (
6.7
) of Proposition
6.3
is stable under
\operatorname{coad}^{r}
, using the formula (
6.9
). In the original papers on qLGFTs, these defining relations were imposed in order that this
U_{q}
-module algebra structure holds.

6.2
The Alekseev map

We have defined
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
as a twist of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\otimes n}
in Proposition
6.2
, and obtained a presentation by generators and relations in Proposition
6.3
. Although this presentation seems complicated, the Alekseev map, defined below, identifies
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
as a module subalgebra of
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
.

Let
V
be a
U_{q}
-module of type
1
. For every
1\leq a\leq n
set

R_{Va}=(\pi_{V}\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{a})(R)\in\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes\tilde{U}_{q}^{{\otimes}n},

where as usual
R\in\mathbb{U}_{q}{\otimes}\mathbb{U}_{q}
is the universal
R
-matrix,
\pi_{V}\colon\mathbb{U}_{q}\rightarrow\operatorname{End}(V)
the canonical projection, and
\mathfrak{i}_{a}\colon\tilde{U}_{q}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
the map defined by
\mathfrak{i}_{a}(x)=1^{\otimes(a-1)}\otimes x\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-a)}
. That
R_{Va}
is a matrix with entries in
\tilde{U}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}
follows from (
3.6
). Consider the linear map

\Phi_{n}\colon\ \operatorname{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\big\{\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}\,\big|\,V\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C}),\,1\leq a\leq n,\,1\leq i,j\leq\dim(V)\big\}\longrightarrow\tilde{U}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}

defined by

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{n})\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n)}\big)=R_{Vn}R_{Vn}^{\prime},

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{n})\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}\big)=(R_{Vn}\cdots R_{Va+1})R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}(R_{Vn}\cdots R_{Va+1})^{-1},\qquad 1\leq a<n.

(6.10)

Since
\textstyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}=\sum_{i,j}E_{i}^{j}\otimes\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}
, we have
({\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{n})\big(\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}\big)\in\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes\tilde{U}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}
. When
n=1
,
\Phi_{n}
coincides with the RSD map
\Phi_{1}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{q}
. We call
\Phi_{n}
the
Alekseev map
. It was first introduced by Alekseev in
[
1
]
.

We can represent
({\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{n})\big(\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}\big)
by the oriented colored braid shown in the figure below, where we use the standard graphical encoding of invariant operators of ribbon categories (see
[
78
]
). In this figure, the vertical strand with label
a
carries the
a
-th factor of
\tilde{U}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}
, and the one with label
V
carries
\operatorname{End}(V)
. Positive crossings carry the operator
\check{R}=\sigma\circ R
, where
\sigma(x\otimes y)=y\otimes x
as usual, and negative crossings carry the operator
\check{R}^{-1}
.

Figure 6.1:
The colored braid representing
({\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{n})\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}\big)
.

Let us endow
\tilde{U}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}
with the following action of
U_{q}

\operatorname{ad}^{r}(y)(x)=\sum_{(y)}\Delta^{(n)}({S}(y_{(1)}))x\Delta^{(n)}(y_{(2)})

(6.11)

for all
y\in U_{q}
,
x\in\tilde{U}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}
. Then
\tilde{U}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}
becomes a right
U_{q}
-module algebra.

The next result is due to Alekseev
[
1
]
.

Theorem 6.6
.

The Alekseev map yields an embedding of module algebras
\Phi_{n}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}
. Moreover it satisfies

({\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{n})\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(1)}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n)}\big)=\big(\pi_{V}\otimes\Delta^{(n-1)}\big)(RR^{\prime}).

(6.12)

Proof.

Let us extend
\Phi_{n}
(by keeping the same notation) in the natural way to the algebra freely generated by the matrix coefficients
\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}
.

By Proposition
6.3
,
\Phi_{n}
induces a well-defined algebra morphism
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}
if it preserves the fusion and exchange relations. Using the graphical encoding recalled in Figure
6.1
, this is shown in the next two figures in the case
n=2
, which generalizes immediately to any
n
. The symbol
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\centerdot}}{{=}}
means equality up to isotopy. Similarly, the relation (
6.12
) is proved by the third figure.

Figure 6.2:
The colored braid identities representing the exchange relation (
6.7
) (top), the fusion relation (
4.12
) (middle), and the relation (
6.12
) (bottom).

The linear map defined by
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}\mapsto R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}
on the
\mathbb{C}(q)
-vector space spanned by all the matrix coefficients
\big({}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}
, for every
1\leq a\leq n
and object
V
of
\mathcal{C}
, is injective by Theorem
4.3
. It differs from
\Phi_{n}
by a linear isomorphism (induced on the
a
-th tensorand by conjugating with
R_{Vn}\cdots R_{Va+1}
), so
\Phi_{n}
is injective.

Finally, let us show that
\Phi_{n}
is a morphism of
U_{q}
-modules. Since both multiplications in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
and
\tilde{U}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}
commute with the respective actions of
U_{q}
, it is enough to check this on generators. For those given by the matrix coefficients we then have to show that

({\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{n})\big(\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}\big)\big)=({\rm id}\otimes\operatorname{ad}^{r}(y))\big(({\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{n})\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}\big)\big)

(6.13)

for every object
V
in
\mathcal{C}
,
1\leq a\leq n
and
y\in U_{q}
. In the case
a=n
,
\Phi_{n}
has the same expression as the RSD map. Hence (
6.13
) follows from the statement of equivariance in Proposition
4.3
.
This can also be proved easily:

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\operatorname{ad}^{r}(y))\big(({\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{n})\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n)}\big)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=({\rm id}\otimes\operatorname{ad}^{r}(y))(R_{Vn}R_{Vn}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(RR^{\prime}),(y)}\pi_{V}\big((RR^{\prime})_{(1)}\big)S(y_{(1)})(RR^{\prime})_{(2)}y_{(2)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(RR^{\prime}),(y)}\pi_{V}\big(y_{(1)}S(y_{(2)})\big)\pi_{V}\big((RR^{\prime})_{(1)}\big)S(y_{(3)})(RR^{\prime})_{(2)}y_{(4)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(RR^{\prime}),(y)}\pi_{V}(y_{(1)})\pi_{V}\big((RR^{\prime})_{(1)}\big)\pi_{V}\big(S(y_{(2)})\big)(RR^{\prime})_{(2)}S(y_{(3)})y_{(4)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(RR^{\prime}),(y)}\pi_{V}(y_{(1)})\pi_{V}((RR^{\prime})_{(1)})\pi_{V}\big(S(y_{(2)})\big)(RR^{\prime})_{(2)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=({\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{n})\big(\!\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n)}\big)\big),

where we have used
[RR^{\prime},(S\otimes S)(\Delta(y))]=0.

More generally, by writing the actions explicitly, that result implies as well the relation

\displaystyle\sum_{(y)}\big(\big(\pi_{V}(y_{(1)})\otimes 1\big)R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}\big(\pi_{V}(S(y_{(2)}))\otimes 1\big)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(y)}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{a}\big(S(y_{(1)})\big)\big)R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{a}(y_{(2)})\big).

(6.14)

Now set

R^{(a)}=R_{Vn}\cdots R_{Va+1}

and denote by
\mathfrak{i}_{an}\colon\tilde{U}_{q}^{{\otimes}n-a}\rightarrow 1^{\otimes a}\otimes\tilde{U}_{q}^{{\otimes}n-a}\subset\tilde{U}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}
the identification map with the final
n-a
tensorands. Then

\displaystyle(1\otimes\Phi_{n})\big(\!\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}\big)\big)=\sum_{(y)}\big(\pi_{V}(y_{(1)})\otimes 1\big)R^{(a)}R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}R^{(a)}{}^{\!-1}\big(\pi_{V}(S(y_{(2)}))\otimes 1\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{ (1 \otimes\Phi_n) \big(\! \operatorname{coad}^r(y) \big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\! (a)}\big) \big)}{}=\sum_{(y)}\big(\pi_{V}(y_{(1)})\otimes 1\big)R^{(a)}R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hphantom{ (1 \otimes\Phi_n) \big(\! \operatorname{coad}^r(y) \big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\! (a)}\big) \big)=}{}\times\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{an}\big(\Delta^{(n-a-1)}\big(\underbrace{S(y_{(2)})y_{(3)}}_{=\varepsilon(y_{(2)})}\big)\big)\big)R^{(a)}{}^{\!-1}\big(\pi_{V}(S(y_{(4)}))\otimes 1\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{ (1 \otimes\Phi_n) \big(\! \operatorname{coad}^r(y) \big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\! (a)}\big) \big)}{}=\sum_{(y)}\big(\pi_{V}(y_{(1)})\otimes 1\big)R^{(a)}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{an}\big(\Delta^{(n-a-1)}\big(S(y_{(2)})\big)\big)\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{ (1 \otimes\Phi_n) \big(\! \operatorname{coad}^r(y) \big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\! (a)}\big) \big)=}{}\times R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}\big(1\!\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{an}\big(\Delta^{(n-a-1)}(y_{(3)})\big)\big)R^{(a)}{}^{\!-1}\big(\pi_{V}\big(S(y_{(4)})\big)\!\otimes 1\big).

Since
R\Delta=\Delta^{\prime}R
in
\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\otimes 2}
, we have
\textstyle\sum_{(y)}R^{-1}(y_{(2)}\otimes y_{(1)})=\sum_{(y)}(y_{(1)}\otimes y_{(2)})R^{-1}
and then

\displaystyle\sum_{(y)}\big(1\otimes S(y_{(1)})\big)R(y_{(2)}\otimes 1)=\sum_{(y)}(y_{(1)}\otimes 1)R\big(1\otimes S(y_{(2)})\big),

\displaystyle\sum_{(y)}\big(S(y_{(1)})\otimes 1\big)R^{-1}(1\otimes y_{(2)})=\sum_{(y)}(1\otimes y_{(1)})R^{-1}\big(S(y_{(2)})\otimes 1\big)

by applying
{\rm id}\otimes S
, and using
({\rm id}\otimes S)(R^{-1})=R
. We deduce recursively

\displaystyle\sum_{(y)}\big(\pi_{V}(y_{(1)})\otimes 1\big)R^{(a)}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{an}\big(\Delta^{(n-a-1)}\big(S(y_{(2)})\big)\big)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{an}\big(\Delta^{(n-a-1)}\big(S(y_{(1)})\big)\big)\big)R^{(a)}\big(\pi_{V}(y_{(2)})\otimes 1\big)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{(y)}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{an}\big(\Delta^{(n-a-1)}(y_{(1)})\big)\big)R^{(a)}{}^{\!-1}\big(\pi_{V}\big(S(y_{(2)})\big)\otimes 1\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\big(\pi_{V}\big(S(y_{(1)})\big)\otimes 1\big)R^{(a)}{}^{\!-1}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{an}\big(\Delta^{(n-a-1)}(y_{(2)})\big)\big).

So

\displaystyle(1\otimes\Phi_{n})\big(\!\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}\big)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(y)}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{an}\big(\Delta^{(n-a-1)}\big(S(y_{(1)})\big)\big)\big)R^{(a)}\big(\pi_{V}(y_{(2)})\otimes 1\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\hphantom{=\sum_{(y)}}{}\times R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}\big(\pi_{V}\big(S(y_{(3)})\big)\otimes 1\big)R^{(a)}{}^{\!-1}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{an}\big(\Delta^{(n-a-1)}(y_{(4)})\big)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(y)}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{an}\big(\Delta^{(n-a-1)}\big(S(y_{(1)})\big)\big)\big)R^{(a)}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{a}\big(S(y_{(2)})\big)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\hphantom{=\sum_{(y)}}{}\times R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{a}(y_{(3)})\big)R^{(a)}{}^{\!-1}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{an}\big(\Delta^{(n-a-1)}(y_{(4)})\big)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(y)}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{an}\big(\Delta^{(n-a-1)}\big(S(y_{(1)})\big)\big)\big)\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{a}\big(S(y_{(2)})\big)\big)R^{(a)}

\displaystyle\qquad\hphantom{=\sum_{(y)}}{}\times R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}R^{(a)}{}^{\!-1}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{a}(y_{(3)})\big)\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{an}\big(\Delta^{(n-a-1)}(y_{(4)})\big)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(y)}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{a-1n}\big(\Delta^{(n-a)}\big(S(y_{(1)})\big)\big)\big)R^{(a)}R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}R^{(a)}{}^{\!-1}\big(1\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{a-1n}\big(\Delta^{(n-a)}(y_{(2)})\big)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{(y)}\big(1\otimes\Delta^{(n-1)}\big(S(y_{(1)})\big)\big)R^{(a)}R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}R^{(a)}{}^{\!-1}\big(1\otimes\Delta^{(n-1)}(y_{(2)})\big),

where we use (
6.14
) in the second equality, and the others follow from trivial commutations between elements lying on different tensorands, and the property
(S\otimes S)\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta S
. This proves (
6.13
).
The relation (
6.12
) has been proved with a picture which encapsulates the following simple computation:

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{n})\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(1)}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n)}\big)

\displaystyle=\prod_{a=1}^{n}\big((R_{Vn}\cdots R_{Va+1})R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}(R_{Vn}\cdots R_{Va+1})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=R_{Vn}\cdots R_{V1}R_{V1}^{\prime}\cdots R_{Vn}^{\prime}=\big(\pi_{V}\otimes\Delta^{(n-1)}\big)(RR^{\prime}).

This concludes the proof.
∎

Denote by
\big({\tilde{U}}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}\big)^{\rm lf}
the set of locally finite elements of
{\tilde{U}}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}
with respect to the action (
6.11
). Consider the subalgebras
({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})^{U_{q}}
and
\big(\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}\big)^{U_{q}}
of invariant elements of the
U_{q}
-module algebras
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
and
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
respectively. We have:

Proposition 6.7
.

The Alekseev map sends
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
isomorphically onto
\big({\tilde{U}}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}\big)^{\rm lf}
, and its restriction to invariant elements affords an isomorphism
\Phi_{n}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\rightarrow\big({\tilde{U}}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}\big)^{U_{q}}
.

Proof.

Consider the first claim. By (
6.9
) the action
\operatorname{coad}^{r}
on
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
preserves the spaces of matrix coefficients of each object
V
of
\mathcal{C}
. Since it gives
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
a structure of module algebra, it follows that
\operatorname{coad}^{r}
is locally finite. The equivariance of
\Phi_{n}
then implies that its image is contained in
\big({\tilde{U}}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}\big)^{\rm lf}
. As
\Phi_{n}
is injective, it remains to prove that it maps
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
surjectively onto
\big({\tilde{U}}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}\big)^{\rm lf}
. Let us denote by
{\rm ad}_{n}^{r}
the action (
6.11
). We can consider another action of
U_{q}
on
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
defined as the
n
-fold tensor product of the adjoint action of
U_{q}
on
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
, which we denote
(\operatorname{ad}^{r})^{\otimes n}
. It is thus defined as follows:

(\operatorname{ad}^{r})^{\otimes n}(y)\big(a^{(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes a^{(n)}\big)=\sum_{(y)}\operatorname{ad}^{r}(y_{(1)})\big(a^{(1)}\big)\otimes\dots\otimes\operatorname{ad}^{r}(y_{(n)})\big(a^{(n)}\big).

We first show that these two actions on
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
define isomorphic modules. For simplicity of notations let us consider the case
n=2
. Consider the map

\psi\colon\quad{\mathbb{U}}_{q}^{\otimes 2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{U}}_{q}^{\otimes 2},\qquad a\otimes b\mapsto R^{-1}_{12}(a\otimes 1)R_{12}(1\otimes b).

We claim that
\psi
intertwines
(\operatorname{ad}^{r})^{\otimes 2}
and
{\rm ad}_{2}^{r}
. This follows from a small variation of the previous proof. Indeed we have

\displaystyle{\rm ad}_{2}^{r}(y)(\psi(a\otimes b))

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y),(R),(R^{-1})}S(y_{(2)})(R^{-1})_{(1)}aR_{(1)}y_{(3)}\otimes S(y_{(1)})(R^{-1})_{(2)}R_{(2)}by_{(4)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y),(R),(R^{-1})}(R^{-1})_{(1)}S(y_{(1)})aR_{(1)}y_{(3)}\otimes(R^{-1})_{(2)}S(y_{(2)})R_{(2)}by_{(4)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y),(R),(R^{-1})}(R^{-1})_{(1)}S(y_{(1)})ay_{(2)}R_{(1)}\otimes(R^{-1})_{(2)}R_{(2)}S(y_{(3)})by_{(4)}

\displaystyle=\psi((\operatorname{ad}^{r})^{\otimes 2}(a\otimes b))).

It is easy to show that
\psi
is an isomorphism, it descends to a map
\psi\colon\big(\tilde{U}_{q}^{\rm lf}\big)^{\otimes 2}\rightarrow\big({\tilde{U}}_{q}^{\otimes 2}\big)^{\rm lf}
, and that
\textstyle\psi(a\otimes b)=\sum_{(R)}\operatorname{ad}^{r}(R_{(1)})(a)\otimes R_{(2)}b
. Now, let
z\in\big({\tilde{U}}_{q}^{{\otimes}2}\big)^{\rm lf}
. Then
\psi^{-1}(z)
is locally finite for the action
(\operatorname{ad}^{r})^{\otimes 2}
. By the main theorem of
[
58
]
, the set of locally finite elements of
{\tilde{U}}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}
for
(\operatorname{ad}^{r})^{\otimes n}
is equal to
\big({\tilde{U}}_{q}^{\rm lf}\big)^{\otimes n}
. Therefore
\psi^{-1}(z)\in{\tilde{U}}_{q}^{\rm lf}\otimes{\tilde{U}}_{q}^{\rm lf}
, and from the surjectivity of the map
\Phi_{1}
onto
{\tilde{U}}_{q}^{\rm lf}
, we deduce that
z=\psi\circ\Phi_{1}^{\otimes 2}(t)
for some
t\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\otimes 2}
. But because of the identity
R_{12}^{-1}R_{01}R^{\prime}_{01}R_{12}=R_{02}R_{01}R^{\prime}_{01}R_{02}^{-1}
, which is a consequence of the Yang–Baxter equation, we obtain that
z=\psi\circ\Phi_{1}^{\otimes 2}(t)=\Phi_{2}(t)
, where we have identified the two vector spaces
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\otimes 2}
. This shows
\Phi_{2}
is a surjection onto
\big({\tilde{U}}_{q}^{{\otimes}2}\big)^{\rm lf}
. The generalisation to any
n
is straightforward.

By the previous theorem we have an inclusion
\Phi_{n}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\big)\subset\big({\tilde{U}}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}\big)^{U_{q}}
. The above argument applies in particular to
{\rm ad}_{n}^{r}
-invariant elements
z\in\big({\tilde{U}}_{q}^{{\otimes}n}\big)^{U_{q}}
. Together with the injectivity of
\Phi_{n}
it implies that this inclusion is an equality. Therefore the second claim follows.∎

Remark 6.8
.

This last result is a generalisation to
n\geq 1
of Theorem
4.3
(3).

6.3
Integral form

Let
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a basis of the free
A
-module
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
. Put
(\alpha_{i})^{(a)}:=\mathfrak{i}_{a}(\alpha_{i})
, where as usual
\mathfrak{i}_{a}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\rightarrow\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\big)^{\otimes n}
is the inclusion map,
1\leq a\leq n
. A basis of the free
A
-module
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\big)^{\otimes n}
is given by the elements
\alpha_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}=(\alpha_{i_{1}})^{(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes(\alpha_{i_{n}})^{(n)}
, the products in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
of the elements
(\alpha_{i_{1}})^{(1)},\dots,(\alpha_{i_{n}})^{(n)}
(in this order from
1
to
n
), indexed by the tuples
(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})\in I^{n}
. For every basis elements
\alpha_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}
,
\alpha_{j_{1}\cdots j_{n}}
we have

\alpha_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}\alpha_{j_{1}\cdots j_{n}}=\sum_{k_{1}\cdots k_{n}}m_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n},j_{1}\cdots j_{n}}^{k_{1}\cdots k_{n}}\alpha_{k_{1}\cdots k_{n}}

for some coefficients
m_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n},j_{1}\cdots j_{n}}^{k_{1}\cdots k_{n}}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
. By Proposition
4.10
we know that
(\alpha_{i})^{(a)}(\alpha_{j})^{(a)}
is a linear combination over
A
of basis elements
(\alpha_{k})^{(a)}
, for every
i
,
j
and
a
. Therefore, it remains to show that any product
(\alpha_{i})^{(b)}(\alpha_{j})^{(a)}
, for
a<b
, is a linear combination over
A
of basis elements
(\alpha_{k})^{(a)}(\alpha_{l})^{(b)}
. Any
(\alpha_{k})^{(a)}
is a linear combination over
A
of matrix cooefficients of
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-modules of type
1
. Let
X
and
Y
be two such modules. By (
6.8
) the exchange relation between matrix coefficients
\big({}_{X}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}
and
\big({}_{Y}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}
, for
a<b
, can be written as

\displaystyle\big({}_{Y}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}\big({}_{X}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}=\sum_{(F)}\big(\big({}_{X}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}\cdot F_{(2)}\big)\big(\big({}_{Y}\phi^{e^{k}}_{e_{l}}\big)^{(b)}\cdot F_{(1)}\big),

where
\textstyle F=R^{\prime}_{23}R^{\prime}_{13}R^{\prime}_{24}R^{\prime}_{14}
. By using Theorem
3.2
as in the proof of Proposition
4.10
, we see that the sum is a linear combination over
\mathbb{C}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]
of basis elements
(\alpha_{k})^{(a)}(\alpha_{l})^{(b)}
. Therefore each coefficient
m_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n},j_{1}\cdots j_{n}}^{k_{1}\cdots k_{n}}\in\mathbb{C}(q)\cap\mathbb{C}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]=\mathbb{C}\big[q,q^{-1}\big]
.

This proves the first claim of the following statement. The second follows from the arguments of Proposition
6.3
, which apply as well to
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
.

Proposition 6.9
.

The
A
-submodule
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\big)^{\otimes n}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
is an
A
-subalgebra. We denote it
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
. Moreover,
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
is the
A
-algebra generated by the matrix coefficients
\big({}_{X}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}\big)^{(a)}
, for all objects
X
of the category
\mathcal{C}_{A}
and
1\leq a\leq n
, with defining relations given by the naturality relations (
4.11
), the fusion relations (
4.12
), and the exchange relations (
6.7
).

Note that, by the properties of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
is a free
A
-module and we have
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}(q)={\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
.

Lemma 6.10
.

The action
\operatorname{coad}^{r}
on
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
yields on
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
a structure of right
U_{A}
-module algebra, and the Alekseev map restricts to an embedding of
U_{A}
-module algebras
\Phi_{n}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{A}^{\otimes n}
.

Proof.

The first claim follows immediately from the case of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
(see Lemma
4.11
), since
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}=\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\big)^{\otimes n}
as an
A
-module. For the second claim, one simply note that Theorem
3.2
implies that
R_{Xn}\cdots R_{Xa+1}\in\operatorname{End}_{A}(X)\otimes\tilde{U}_{A}^{\otimes n}
, for every
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-module
X
of type
1
, and use that
{\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{n}
is defined on generators of
\operatorname{End}_{A}(X)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
as
{\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}
followed by conjugations with matrices of the form
R_{Xn}\cdots R_{Xa+1}
.∎

Proposition 6.11
.

{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
, and therefore its subalgebras
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
and
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}
, does not have non trivial zero divisors.

Proof.

Because of the injectivity of
\Phi_{n}
it is sufficient to show that
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
and
\tilde{U}_{A}^{\otimes n}
have no non trivial zero divisors. We note that
U_{A}(\mathfrak{g})^{\otimes n}=U_{A}(\mathfrak{g}^{\oplus n}).
Then the result for
{U}_{A}^{\otimes n}
is a consequence of
[
27
, Corollary 1.8]
applied to
\mathfrak{g}^{\oplus n}.
Note that in that paper it is assumed that the Cartan matrix is indecomposable but their method, which consists in proving that an associated graded algebra is quasipolynomial, does not use this assumption. It applies as well for
\tilde{U}_{A}^{\otimes n}
,
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
. ∎

In the case of
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
we can restrict the target:

Proposition 6.12
.

\Phi_{n}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{{\otimes}n}
takes values in
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{{\otimes}n}
, and yields an embedding of
U_{A}
-module algebras
\Phi_{n}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\rightarrow U_{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{{\otimes}n}
.

Proof.

The second claim follows from the first and Lemma
6.10
. Consider the first claim. For every
1\leq a\leq n
and object
V
of
\mathcal{C}
we have
R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes U_{q}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{\otimes n}
by Proposition
5.4
. We have to show that conjugating by
R_{Vn}\cdots R_{Va+1}\in\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes\tilde{U}_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{{\otimes}n}
maps
\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes U_{q}^{\rm lf}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{\otimes n}
to
\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{\otimes n}
. It is enough to prove it for
V=V_{2}
, and for the conjugation by
R_{Va+1}
only (the general case follows from this one by an easy induction). Recall the expression of
R
in (
3.7
). Using that
E
,
F
act nilpotently on
V_{2}
with order
2
, and
R^{-1}=(S\otimes{\rm id})(R)
, we get

\displaystyle R_{Va+1}=(\pi_{V_{2}}\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{a+1})(R)=\begin{pmatrix}1\otimes q^{H/2}&(q-q^{-1})1\otimes q^{H/2}F\\[2.15277pt]
0&1\otimes q^{-H/2}\end{pmatrix}\!,

(6.15)

\displaystyle R_{Va+1}^{-1}=(\pi_{V_{2}}\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{a+1})(R^{-1})=\begin{pmatrix}1\otimes q^{-H/2}&-q(q-q^{-1})1\otimes q^{H/2}F\\[2.15277pt]
0&1\otimes q^{H/2}\end{pmatrix}\!,

(6.16)

where
q^{H/2}\in\tilde{U}_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is defined in Section
3
, and for each matrix entry we write only the components in the
a
-th and
a+1
-th tensorands of
\tilde{U}_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{{\otimes}n}
(the others being
1
’s). With this convention, let

N:=\begin{pmatrix}u\otimes 1&v\otimes 1\\
w\otimes 1&x\otimes 1\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes\tilde{U}_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{{\otimes}n}.

(6.17)

Then

R_{Va+1}NR_{Va+1}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}u\otimes 1&-(q^{2}-1)u\otimes KF-q^{2}(q-q^{-1})w\otimes KF^{2}\\
{}+q^{-1}(q-q^{-1})w\otimes F&+v\otimes K+q^{-1}(q-q^{-1})x\otimes KF\\[6.45831pt]
w\otimes K^{-1}&-q(q-q^{-1})w\otimes F+x\otimes 1\end{pmatrix}\!.

This matrix has entries in
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{\otimes n}
if the matrix entries of
N
belong to
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{\otimes n}
. Conjugating recursively
R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}
with
R_{Vi}
, for
i
from
a+1
to
n
, by the same computation we deduce that
\Phi_{n}
takes values in
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{\otimes n}
. ∎

6.4
Localization when
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}

We are now going to define a localization of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, which will satisfy a generalization of Proposition
5.6
. We need the following lemma. For every
u\in U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, denote by
u^{(i)}
the element of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{\otimes n}
with
u
in the
i
-th tensorand and
1
’s elsewhere. Analogously to (
5.1
), for every
1\leq i\leq n
, put

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i)}=\begin{pmatrix}a^{(i)}&b^{(i)}\\
c^{(i)}&d^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{(i)},

(6.18)

where
V=V_{2}
.

Lemma 6.13
.

For every
1\leq i\leq n
,
\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(i)}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big)
is contained in the subalgebra of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{\otimes n}
generated by
\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{n})
and the elements
K^{(i+1)},\dots,K^{(n)}
.

Proof.

The case
i=n
is clear, as
\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(n)}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big)=\Phi_{n}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(n)}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big)
by definition. We argue by decreasing induction on
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Take

\tilde{M}=({\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n})\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n-1)}\big)

in (
6.17
). The entries of
\tilde{M}
generate the algebra
\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(n-1)}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big)
. By the formula of
R_{Vn}\tilde{M}R_{0n}^{-1}
we have
\Phi_{n}\big(c^{(n-1)}\big)=w\otimes K^{-1}
. Hence
w\otimes 1=\big(w\otimes K^{-1}\big)(1\otimes K)
belongs to the algebra generated by
\Phi_{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2)))
and
K^{(n)}
. Since
q^{-1}\big(q-q^{-1}\big)(1\otimes F)=\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}\big(b^{(n)}\big)=\Phi_{n}\big(b^{(n)}\big)
, the same is true of
-q\big(q-q^{-1}\big)w\otimes F
, and hence eventually also of
x\otimes 1
,
u\otimes 1
and
v\otimes 1
by using again the formula of
R_{Vn}\tilde{M}R_{Vn}^{-1}
. This proves the statement for
i=n-1
. Inducting on
i
, using the matrix
\tilde{M}
of generators of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(i)}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, the same reasoning proves the result for all values of
i
. ∎

Lemma 6.14
.

Define elements
\xi^{(i)}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
,
i=1,\dots,n
by

\xi^{(i)}=\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i)}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n)}\big)_{22},

where
22
denotes the lower right matrix element. The elements
\xi^{(i)}
are commuting and satisfy:

\Phi_{n}\big(\xi^{(i)}\big)=\big(K^{-1}\big)^{(i)}\cdots\big(K^{-1}\big)^{(n)}.

Proof.

As in (
6.12
) we have

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{n})\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i)}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n)}\big)=R_{Vn}\cdots R_{Vi}R_{Vi}^{\prime}\cdots R_{Vn}=\big(\pi_{V}\otimes 1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes\Delta^{(n-i)}\big)(RR^{\prime}).

The lower right matrix element of
(\pi_{V}\otimes{\rm id})(RR^{\prime})
is equal to
K^{-1}
. As a result, by applying
\Delta^{(n-i)}
we obtain

\Phi_{n}\big(\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i)}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n)}\big)_{22}\big)=1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes\Delta^{(n-i)}\big(K^{-1}\big)=\big(K^{-1}\big)^{(i)}\cdots\big(K^{-1}\big)^{(n)}.

By injectivity of
\Phi_{n}
this proves that the elements
\xi^{(i)}
are commuting. ∎

The elements
\xi^{(i)}
commute, and
\big\{\big(\xi^{(1)}{}^{k}\big)\big\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is an Ore set of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
. In fact, it is easy to see that for every
i=1,\dots,n
the element
\xi^{(i)}
is an Ore element of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(i\leq)}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, where
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(i\leq)}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is the subalgebra of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
generated by the subalgebras
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(a)}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
,
a\geq i
. Indeed, because
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
has no non trivial zero divisors,
\xi^{(i)}
is a regular element. The set
\big\{\big(\xi^{(i)}{}^{k}\big)\big\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is multiplicatively closed, and it is an Ore subset of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(i\leq)}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, since for all
x\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(i\leq)}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
there exist elements
y,y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(i\leq)}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
such that

x\xi^{(i)}=\xi^{(i)}y
and
\xi^{(i)}x=y^{\prime}\xi^{(i)}
. This is shown as follows:
\Phi_{n}(x)\Phi_{n}\big(\xi^{(i)}\big)=\Phi_{n}(x)\big(K^{-1}\big)^{(i)}\cdots\big(K^{-1}\big)^{(n)}=\big(K^{-1}\big)^{(i)}\cdots\big(K^{-1}\big)^{(n)}\operatorname{ad}^{r}(K)(\Phi_{n}(x))
, where we have used the fact that
\Phi_{n}(x)\in 1^{\otimes i-1}\otimes U_{q}^{\otimes(n-i+1)}
. But
\operatorname{ad}^{r}(K)(\Phi_{n}(x))=\Phi_{1}(\operatorname{coad}^{r}(K)(x)),
and therefore the Ore conditions are satisfied with
y=\operatorname{coad}^{r}(K)(x)
.

This argument does not permit to conclude that the multiplicative sets
\big\{\big(\xi^{(i)}{}^{k}\big)\big\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
are Ore sets of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
. We therefore cannot localize with respect to the elements
\xi^{(i)}
as easily as for
\xi^{(1)}
. We proceed in a different way.

We first explain the case
n=2
. The element
\xi^{(1)}=\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(2)}\big)_{22}=d^{(1)}d^{(2)}+c^{(1)}b^{(2)}
is a regular Ore element of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}
, so we can define the localisation

{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}\big[\xi^{(1)}{}^{-1}\big].
We want to define the inverse of the element
\xi^{(2)}
, and a new algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}\big[\xi^{(1)}{}^{-1}\big]\big[\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}\big]
such that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}\big[\xi^{(1)}{}^{-1}\big]\subset{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}\big[\xi^{(1)}{}^{-1}\big]\big[\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}\big]
and
\Phi_{2}
extends naturally to an homomorphism of algebras

\Phi_{2}\colon\ {\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}\big[\xi^{(1)}{}^{-1}\big]\big[\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}\big]\rightarrow U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{\otimes 2}.

This can be done by writing explicitely the exchange relations between
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(1)}
and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(2)}
involving
d^{(2)}=\xi^{(2)}
:

\displaystyle c^{(1)}d^{(2)}=d^{(2)}c^{(1)},

\displaystyle d^{(2)}a^{(1)}=a^{(1)}d^{(2)}+\big(1-q^{-2}\big)c^{(1)}b^{(2)},

\displaystyle d^{(2)}d^{(1)}=d^{(1)}d^{(2)}+\big(1-q^{2}\big)c^{(1)}b^{(2)},

\displaystyle d^{(2)}b^{(1)}=b^{(1)}d^{(2)}+\big(1-q^{2}\big)\big(a^{(1)}-d^{(1)}\big)b^{(2)}.

We define
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}\big[\xi^{(1)}{}^{-1}\big]\big[\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}\big]
to be the algebra generated by the elements

a^{(1)}
,
b^{(1)}
,
c^{(1)}
,
d^{(1)}
,

a^{(2)}
,
b^{(2)}
,
c^{(2)}
,
d^{(2)}
,
\xi^{(1)}{}^{-1}
,
\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}
,
where
a^{(1)},\dots,d^{(2)}
satisfy the exchange relation (
5.2
) and (
6.7
),
\xi^{(1)}{}^{-1}
is the inverse in the sense of Ore of the element

\xi^{(1)}=d^{(1)}d^{(2)}+c^{(1)}b^{(2)}
and the following relations for
\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}
, the inverse of
\xi^{(2)}=d^{(2)},
hold true:

\displaystyle\xi^{(2)}\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}=\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}\xi^{(2)}=1,

\displaystyle\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}c^{(1)}=c^{(1)}\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1},

\displaystyle\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}a^{(1)}=a^{(1)}\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}-q^{-2}\big(1-q^{-2}\big)c^{(1)}b^{(2)}\xi^{(2)}{}^{-2},

\displaystyle\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}d^{(1)}=d^{(1)}\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}-q^{-2}\big(1-q^{2}\big)c^{(1)}b^{(2)}\xi^{(2)}{}^{-2},

\displaystyle\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}b^{(1)}=b^{(1)}\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}+q^{-1}\big(1-q^{-2}\big)\big(a^{(1)}-d^{(1)}\big)b^{(2)}\xi^{(2)}{}^{-2}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}b^{(1)}=}{}+q^{-5}\big(1-q^{2}\big)\big(1-q^{-4}\big)c^{(1)}\big(b^{(2)}\big)^{2}\xi^{(2)}{}^{-3}.

The last relations are chosen in order that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}\big[\xi^{(1)}{}^{-1}\big]\subset{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}\big[\xi^{(1)}{}^{-1}\big]\big[\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}\big]
as an algebra and are a direct consequence of the exchange relations (
6.7
) and the invertibility of
\xi^{(2)}.

As a result
\Phi_{2}
extends to a morphism of algebra
\Phi_{2}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}\big[\xi^{(1)}{}^{-1}\big]\big[\xi^{(2)}{}^{-1}\big]\rightarrow U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{\otimes 2}.
This morphism of algebra will be shown to be an isomorphism in Proposition
6.16
.

The construction of the localisation for
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
,
n>2
, is defined by the same procedure.
We want to define a localisation
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big[\xi^{(n)}{}^{-1},\dots,\xi^{(1)}{}^{-1}\big]
. The set of elements
\big\{\xi^{(n)k}\big\}_{k}
being an Ore subset of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, we can define the standard localisation
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big[\xi^{(n)}{}^{-1}\big]
. Note that the matrix
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(2)}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n)}
has the same reflection equation with
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(1)}
as

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(2)}.
Therefore we can define
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big[\xi^{(n)}{}^{-1}\big]\big[\xi^{(n-1)}{}^{-1}\big]
by the same method as when
n=2
, and so on.

Definition 6.15
.

By iterating the preceeding construction we define:

{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))={\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big[\xi^{(n)}{}^{-1}\big]\big[\xi^{(n-1)}{}^{-1}\big]\cdots\big[\xi^{(1)}{}^{-1}\big].

In the sequel it will be convenient to define invertible elements
\delta^{(i)}\in{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
,
i=1,\dots,n,
satisfying

\xi^{(i)}=\delta^{(i)}\cdots\delta^{(n)}
, i.e.,
\delta^{(i)}=\xi^{(i)}\xi^{(i+1)}{}^{-1}
.

The elements
\delta^{(i)}
,
i=1,\dots,n,
are invertible, commute and satisfy
\Phi_{n}\big(\delta^{(i)}\big)=\big(K^{-1}\big)^{(i)}
.

We can define the localization
{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
of the integral form
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
in the very same way (recall Lemma
5.7
for the case
n=1
). Let

\Phi_{n}\colon\quad{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\rightarrow U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{{\otimes}n}

be the unique morphism of module algebras extending the Alekseev map. Recall the subalgebra
U_{A}^{\prime}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
of
U_{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, defined in Lemma
5.7
.

Proposition 6.16
.

The extended Alekseev map
\Phi_{n}\colon{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\rightarrow U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{{\otimes}n}
is an isomorphism of
U_{q}
-module algebras, and it restricts to an isomorphism of
U_{A}
-module algebras
\Phi_{n}\colon{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\rightarrow U_{A}^{\prime}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{{\otimes}n}
.

Proof.

All claims are clear by the previous results, except surjectivity. For the first claim, it follows from Lemma
6.13
, since the algebra generated by
\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(i)}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big)
and
K^{(i)}
is
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{(i)}
(the
i
-th tensorand). For the second claim, it follows from Lemma
5.7
(3).∎

Remark 6.17
.

This proposition (first claim) justifies the localization of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
by the elements
\xi^{(1)},\dots,\xi^{(n)}
. The result of localization, if any, by the elements
d^{(1)},\dots,d^{(n)}
is not clear to us; note that they do not commute.

6.5
Invariant elements

We go back to the general situation of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}={\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}(\mathfrak{g})
for an arbitrary
\mathfrak{g}
. We need the following fact, proved by Majid
[
69
]
for
{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
. For completeness we recall the main ideas of the proof given in
[
32
]
, which extends immediately to
\mathcal{O}_{A}
.

Proposition 6.18
.

The iterated coproduct
\Delta^{(n-1)}\colon\mathcal{O}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{A}^{\otimes n}
, considered as a linear map
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
, is an algebra morphism and satisfies the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
