\scriptstyle{\Delta^{(n-1)}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{n}}
\textstyle{\tilde{U}_{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta^{(n-1)}}
\textstyle{\tilde{U}_{A}^{\otimes n}.}

Proof.

The main point of the proof is that, for every
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-module
X
of type
1
, we have

\big({\rm id}\otimes\Delta^{(n-1)}\big)\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle X}}{{M}}\!\big)=\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle X}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(1)}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle X}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n)}.

(6.19)

This follows by a direct application of the definitions. But Proposition
4.6
implies that the map
\Delta^{(n-1)}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
is an algebra morphism if and only if the matrix on the left hand side of (
6.19
) satisfies the fusion relation. This is easily shown to be true for the right hand side by using the exchange relation (
6.7
) recursively. This proves the proposition. The commutativity diagram is a reformulation of (
6.12
).∎

Consider now the algebras of invariant elements,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}:=\big\{\alpha\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}\mid\forall y\in U_{q},\ \operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)(\alpha)=\varepsilon(y)\alpha\big\},

\displaystyle\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}:=\big\{\alpha\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\mid\forall y\in U_{A},\ \operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)(\alpha)=\varepsilon(y)\alpha\big\}.

Note that
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}={\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\cap{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
, and therefore
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}=\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}(q)
. Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
is a free
A
-module, and
A
is a principal ideal domain, the
A
-submodule
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}
is free.

Proposition 6.19
.

The algebra
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}
is the centralizer of
\Delta^{(n-1)}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\big)
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
. As a corollary we have

\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}=\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)
.

Proof.

Clearing denominators it is enough to give the details for
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
. First note that an element
z
of
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
is invariant under the right adjoint action of
U_{q}
if and only if it commutes with
\Delta^{(n-1)}(x)
for every
x\in U_{q}
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle z\Delta^{(n-1)}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x)}z\varepsilon(x_{(1)})\Delta^{(n-1)}(x_{(2)})=\sum_{(x)}\varepsilon(x_{(1)})z\Delta^{(n-1)}(x_{(2)})

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{(x)}\Delta^{(n-1)}(x_{(1)})\Delta^{(n-1)}(S(x_{(2)}))z\Delta^{(n-1)}(x_{(3)})=\!\sum_{(x)}\!\Delta^{(n-1)}(x_{(1)})\operatorname{ad}^{r}(x_{(2)})(z).

Hence
\textstyle z\Delta^{(n-1)}(x)=\sum_{(x)}\Delta^{(n-1)}(x_{1})\varepsilon(x_{(2)})z=\Delta^{(n-1)}(x)z
if
z
is an invariant element. Conversely, if
z
commutes with
\Delta^{(n-1)}(x)
for every
x\in U_{q}
, then

\operatorname{ad}^{r}(x)(z)=\sum_{(x)}\Delta^{(n-1)}(S(x_{(1)}))\Delta^{(n-1)}(x_{(2)})z=\Delta^{(n-1)}(\varepsilon(x))z=\varepsilon(x)z.

This proves our claim. Now, let
\alpha\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
. Then
\Phi_{n}(\alpha)
is an
\operatorname{ad}^{r}(U_{q})
-invariant element of
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
, by Theorem
6.6
. By the claim above, and the fact that commuting with elements of
\tilde{U}_{q}
or
U_{q}
is the same,
\Phi_{n}(\alpha)
commutes with the matrix coefficients of
\big(\pi_{V}\otimes\Delta^{(n-1)}\big)(RR^{\prime})
, for every object
V
of
\mathcal{C}
. By the injectivity of
\Phi_{n}
and the relation (
6.12
),
\alpha
commutes with the matrix coefficients of (
6.19
) for all objects
V
of
\mathcal{C}
. These generate the algebra
\Delta^{(n-1)}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1})
, so
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
lies in the centralizer of the latter.

Conversely, the same reasoning shows that if
\alpha
lies in the centralizer of
\Delta^{(n-1)}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1})
, then
\Phi_{n}(\alpha)
commutes with the matrix coefficients of
\big(\pi_{V}\otimes\Delta^{(n-1)}\big)(RR^{\prime})
, for every object
V
of
\mathcal{C}
. From Theorem
4.3
we deduce that
\Phi_{n}(\alpha)
lies in the centralizer of
\Delta^{(n-1)}\big(\tilde{U}_{q}^{\rm lf}\big)
. This is the same as the centralizer of
\Delta^{(n-1)}(\tilde{U}_{q})
by Remark
4.5
(1), so as above we deduce that
\Phi_{n}(\alpha)
is an invariant element of
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
, and by injectivity and equivariance of
\Phi_{n}
that
\alpha
is an invariant element of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
.

The corollary is immediate, because a central element of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
necessarily commutes with
\Delta^{(n-1)}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1})
, so it is invariant.∎

We now give an explicit basis of the algebra of invariant elements
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
. Let
n
be an integer greater than
1
. For every
2\leq k\leq n
we denote by
S(k)\in{\mathbb{U}_{q}}^{{\otimes}n}
the element defined by

S(k)={\rm id}^{\otimes(k-2)}\otimes\big({\rm id}\otimes\Delta^{(n-k)}\big)(R).

Let
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in P_{+}
, and
V_{\lambda_{1}},\dots,V_{\lambda_{n}}
the type
1
simple
U_{q}
-modules of highest weights
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
respectively. Put
[\lambda]=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})
, and consider the
U_{q}
-module

V_{[\lambda]}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}V_{\lambda_{j}}.

Define
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}\,\in\operatorname{End}\big(V_{[\lambda]}\big)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
by
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}\,=\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda}}}{{M}}
if
n=1
, and if
n\geq 2
by

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}\,=\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{1}}}}{{M}}_{1}{}^{\!\!\!(1)}\prod_{k=2}^{n}\big(S(k)^{-1}_{V_{\lambda_{1}},\dots,V_{\lambda_{n}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{k}}}}{{M}}_{k}{}^{\!\!\!(k)}\big)\prod_{k=n}^{2}S(k)_{V_{\lambda_{1}},\dots,V_{\lambda_{n}}}.

(6.20)

For example for
n=2
we have

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}\,=\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{1}}}}{{M}}_{1}{}^{\!\!\!(1)}R_{12}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{2}}}}{{M}}_{2}{}^{\!\!\!(2)}R_{12}

and for
n=3
,

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}\,=\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{1}}}}{{M}}_{1}{}^{\!\!\!(1)}({\rm id}\otimes\Delta)(R^{-1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{2}}}}{{M}}_{2}{}^{\!\!\!(2)}(R^{-1})_{23}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{3}}}}{{M}}_{3}{}^{(3)}R_{23}({\rm id}\otimes\Delta)(R).

The reason for considering the matrices
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}
comes from the two following propositions. The first generalizes the identity (
4.14
).

Proposition 6.20
.

The right coadjoint action of
U_{q}
on the matrix elements of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}
can be written in matrix form as

\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}\!\big)=\sum_{(y)}\big((\pi_{V_{[\lambda]}}(y_{(1)})\otimes{\rm id})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{{\mathbb{M}}}}(\pi_{V_{[\lambda]}}(S(y_{(2)}))\otimes{\rm id})\big).

(6.21)

Proof.

We show it for
n=2
, since the general proof follows from it by an easy induction on
n
. We have

\displaystyle\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}\!\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)\big(\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{1}}}}{{M}}_{1}{}^{\!\!\!(1)}R_{12}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{2}}}}{{M}}_{2}{}^{\!\!\!(2)}R_{12}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y)}(y_{(1)})_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{1}}}}{{M}}_{1}{}^{\!\!\!(1)}S(y_{(2)})_{1}R_{12}^{-1}(y_{(3)})_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{2}}}}{{M}}_{2}{}^{\!\!\!(2)}S(y_{(4)})_{2}R_{12}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y)}(y_{(1)})_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{1}}}}{{M}}_{1}{}^{\!\!\!(1)}(y_{(2)})_{2}R_{12}^{-1}(S(y_{(3)}))_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{2}}}}{{M}}_{2}{}^{\!\!\!(2)}S(y_{(4)})_{2}R_{12}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y)}(y_{(1)}\otimes y_{(2)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{1}}}}{{M}}_{1}{}^{\!\!\!(1)}R_{12}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{2}}}}{{M}}_{2}{}^{\!\!\!(2)}(S(y_{(3)})\otimes S(y_{(4)}))R_{12}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y)}(y_{(1)}\otimes y_{(2)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{1}}}}{{M}}_{1}{}^{\!\!\!(1)}R_{12}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{2}}}}{{M}}_{2}{}^{\!\!\!(2)}R_{12}\big(S(y_{(4)})\otimes S(y_{(3)})\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y)}\big(\big(\pi_{V_{[\lambda]}}(y_{(1)})\otimes{\rm id}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{{\mathbb{M}}}}\big(\pi_{V_{[\lambda]}}(S(y_{(2)}))\otimes{\rm id}\big)\big).

All equalities are clear but the third and fifth, which follow from
\big({\rm id}\otimes S^{-1}\big)(R)=R^{-1}
,
R\Delta R^{-1}=\Delta^{\rm cop}
,
\Delta\circ S=(S\otimes S)\circ\Delta
, and the fact that
S
is an algebra antimorphism. The result follows. ∎

Denote by
\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{e}}_{i}\!\big)
a basis of
V_{\lambda}
, by
\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{e}}{}^{\!\!i}\big)
the dual basis, and put
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{e}}_{[i]}:=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{1}}}{{e}}_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{n}}}{{e}}_{i_{n}}
. We thus get a basis
\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{e}}{}^{\!\![i]}\big)
of
V_{[\lambda]}
. Denote by
\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{e}}{}^{\!\![i]}\big)
the dual basis.

Proposition 6.21
.

The elements
\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{e}}{}^{\!\!\![j]}\otimes{\rm id}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{e}}{}_{\!\!\![i]}\otimes{\rm id}\big)
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
, where
[\lambda]\in P_{+}^{n}
,
[i]
labels the basis of
V_{[\lambda]}
, and
[j]
the dual basis, form a basis of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
. Equivalently, for every
x\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
there exists a unique family of endomorphisms
a_{[\lambda]}(x)\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\big(V_{[\lambda]}\big)
,
[\lambda]\in P_{+}^{n}
, which are zero except possibly for a finite number of terms, such that

x=\sum_{[\lambda]}\big(\!\operatorname{Tr}_{{V_{[\lambda]}}}\otimes{\rm id}\big)\big(\big(\pi_{V_{\lambda}}(\ell)a_{[\lambda]}(x)\otimes{\rm id}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}\!\big)=\sum_{[\lambda]}\operatorname{qTr}_{{V_{[\lambda]}}}\big(\big(a_{[\lambda]}(x)\otimes{\rm id}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}\!\big),

(6.22)

where as usual
\ell
is the pivotal element and
\pi_{V_{\lambda}}(\ell)
is the endomorphism of
V_{[\lambda]}
given by the action of
\ell
.

Note that
\pi_{V_{\lambda}}(\ell)
is introduced in (
6.22
) in order to simplify the statement of the next proposition.

Proof.

We show the first claim of the proposition for
n=2
; the general proof is similar. In this case it is sufficient to prove that the set of matrix elements of the tensors

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{1}}}}{{M}}_{1}{}^{\!\!\!(1)}R_{12}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{2}}}}{{M}}_{2}{}^{\!\!\!(2)}=\sum_{(R)}(R_{(2)})_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{1}}}}{{M}}_{1}{}^{\!\!\!(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{2}}}}{{M}}_{2}{}^{\!\!\!(2)}S(R_{(1)})_{1},\qquad[\lambda]\in P_{+}^{n},

is a basis of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}
. Note that we use
\textstyle R^{-1}=(S\otimes{\rm id})(R)=\sum_{(R)}S(R_{(1)})\otimes R_{(2)}
in the above equality. Let
T=(S\otimes S^{-1})\big(R^{-1}\big)
, that we denote as usual by
\textstyle T=\sum_{(T)}T_{(1)}\otimes T_{(2)}
. From the identity
1\otimes 1=(S\otimes{\rm id})\big(R^{-1}R\big)=\sum_{(R),(T)}S(R_{(1)})T_{(1^{\prime})}\otimes S(T_{(2^{\prime})})R_{(2)}
, we obtain

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{1}}}}{{M}}_{1}{}^{\!\!\!(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{2}}}}{{M}}_{2}{}^{\!\!\!(2)}=\sum_{(T)}S(T_{(2)})_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{1}}}}{{M}}_{1}{}^{\!\!\!(1)}R_{12}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{2}}}}{{M}}_{2}{}^{\!\!\!(2)}(T_{(1)})_{1}.

The conclusion follows from this, since the set of matrix elements of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{1}}}}{{M}}_{1}{}^{\!\!\!(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda_{2}}}}{{M}}_{2}{}^{\!\!\!(2)}
,
[\lambda]\in P_{+}^{2}
, is a basis of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,2}
. The second claim is a trivial consequence of the first one. ∎

By combining the two previous propositions we obtain the following characterisation of the invariants elements.

Proposition 6.22
.

An element
x\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
belongs to
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
if and only if for every
[\lambda]\in P_{+}^{n}
we have
a_{[\lambda]}(x)\in\operatorname{End}_{U_{q}}\big(V_{[\lambda]}\big)
. Therefore, the elements

v_{[\lambda]}\big(a_{[\lambda]}^{(k)}\big):=\operatorname{qTr}_{V_{[\lambda]}}\big(a_{[\lambda]}^{(k)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}\!\big),

(6.23)

where
\big\{a_{[\lambda]}^{(k)}\big\}_{k}
is any basis of
\operatorname{End}_{U_{q}}\big(V_{[\lambda]}\big)
, make a basis of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
.

Proof.

We have
x\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
if and only if
\operatorname{coad}^{r}(y)(x)=\epsilon(y)x
for every
y\in U_{q}
, or equivalently for every
y\in U_{q}
. From Proposition
6.20
, the basis provided by Proposition
6.21
, and the fact that
S^{2}(y)=\ell y\ell^{-1}
, this is equivalent to
\textstyle\sum_{(y)}S^{-1}(y_{(2)})_{V_{[\lambda]}}a_{[\lambda]}(x)(y_{(1)})_{V_{[\lambda]}}=\epsilon(y)a_{[\lambda]}(x)
for every
[\lambda]\in P_{+}^{n}
, which is also equivalent to
y_{V_{[\lambda]}}a_{[\lambda]}(x)=a_{[\lambda]}(x)y_{V_{[\lambda]}}
.∎

Finally, let us consider the integral form
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}
. Matrices
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[X]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}
can be defined by the formula (
6.20
), replacing
V_{\lambda_{1}},\dots,V_{\lambda_{n}}
with arbitrary
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-modules
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
of type
1
. Clearly these matrices still satisfy the equivariance property of Proposition
6.20
, and the arguments of Proposition
6.21
imply that their matrix elements form a generating family of the
A
-module
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
. When the
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-modules
X_{i}
span the set of full
A
-sublattices
{}_{A}V_{\lambda_{i}}
of the
U_{q}
-modules
V_{\lambda_{i}}
, such elements form a free family. One can still define invariant elements
v_{X}(a_{X})\in\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}
by

v_{X}(a_{X}):=\operatorname{qTr}_{X}\big(a_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle X}}{{\mathbb{M}}}\!\big),

where
X:=X_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes X_{n}
for arbitrary
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-modules
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
of type
1
, and
a_{X}\in\operatorname{End}_{U_{A}^{\rm res}}(X)
. The
A
-module
\operatorname{End}_{U_{A}^{\rm res}}(X)
is free, as it is a submodule of the free
A
-module
\operatorname{End}_{U_{A}^{\rm res}}(X)
and
A
is a principal ideal domain. Then, taking
X_{i}:={}_{A}V_{\lambda_{i}}
and
a_{X}^{(k)}
basis elements of
\operatorname{End}_{U_{A}^{\rm res}}(X)
, the elements
v_{X}\big(a_{X}^{(k)}\big)
form a free family over
A
, and by Proposition
6.22
a basis of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
.

In the case of
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
, let us state the following result. It is a direct consequence of Theorem
8.6
and the fact that the skein algebra
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
is finitely generated and Noetherian, which is proved in
[
72
, Theorem 3]
, by topological means. The method relies on the fact that
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
has a natural filtration, and consists in proving that the associated graded algebra is finitely generated and Noetherian.

Theorem 6.23
.

The algebra
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big)^{U_{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))}
is finitely generated and Noetherian.

6.6
Centers

Next we turn to the center
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
and
\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\big)
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
.

Proposition 6.24
.

The Alekseev map affords an isomorphism from
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})
to
\mathcal{Z}\big(\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}\big)
. In particular this implies
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})=\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1})^{\otimes n}
. Moreover, the elements

{}_{\lambda}\omega^{(i)}:=\operatorname{qTr}_{V_{\lambda}}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i)}\big),\qquad\lambda\in P_{+},i\in\{1,\dots,n\},

belong to the center
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})
and the family of elements

\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}{}_{\lambda_{i}}\omega^{(i)}
, where
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in P_{+}
, form a basis of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})
. The elements
{}_{\lambda}\omega^{(i)}
belong to
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
, and they form a free family.

Proof.

We have
\Phi_{n}(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}))=\mathcal{Z}(\Phi_{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}))
by Theorem
6.6
. We claim that if
x\in\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
commutes with
\Phi_{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})
, it is central. Indeed,
x
commutes with
\Phi_{n}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(n)}\big)=1^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes\tilde{U}_{q}^{\rm lf}
, and hence with
1^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes\tilde{U}_{q}
, and the matrix coefficients of
R_{Vn}^{\pm 1}
by Remark
4.5
. (For instance, when
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
,
K=q^{H}
and
q^{H/2}
commute and are diagonalizable on objects of
\mathcal{C}
, and since
x
commutes with
1^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes K
, it commutes with
1^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes q^{H/2}
too, and the conclusion follows from (
6.15
) and (
6.16
)). Using that
x
commutes also with
\Phi_{n}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(n-1)}\big)
, and hence with the matrix coefficients of
R_{Vn}R_{Vn-1}R_{Vn-1}^{\prime}R_{Vn}^{-1}
, we deduce that it commutes with the matrix coefficients of
R_{Vn-1}R_{Vn-1}^{\prime}
, and hence with every element of
1^{\otimes(n-2)}\otimes\tilde{U}_{q}\otimes 1
. Continuing in this way recursively, we get that
x\in\mathcal{Z}\big(\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}\big)
, which proves our claim. It implies that
\Phi_{n}(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}))\subset\mathcal{Z}\big(\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}\big)
.

Let us prove the converse inclusion. Put
R^{(a)}:=R_{Vn}\cdots R_{Va+1}
. For every
1\leq a\leq n
we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}\big(\!\operatorname{qTr}_{V_{\lambda}}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{Tr}_{V_{\lambda}}\otimes{\rm id})\big(\big(\pi_{V_{\lambda}}(\ell)\otimes{\rm id}\big)R^{(a)}R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}R^{(a)}{}^{\!-1}\big)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{Tr}_{V_{\lambda}}\otimes{\rm id})\big(\big(\pi_{V_{\lambda}}(\ell)\otimes{\rm id}\big)R_{Va}R_{Va}^{\prime}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{i}_{a}\big(\Phi_{1}\big(\!\operatorname{qTr}_{V_{\lambda}}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda}}}{{M}}\!\big)\big)\big),

(6.24)

where
\Phi_{1}
in the last equality is the RSD map, and the second equality follows from
R^{-1}=(S\otimes{\rm id})(R)
and
S^{2}(x)=\ell x\ell^{-1}
for every
x\in\mathbb{U}_{q}
. By Theorem
4.3
and Proposition
4.8
, the family of these elements, when
\lambda
spans
P_{+}
, forms a basis of the center of the
a
-th tensorand of
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
. Therefore
\Phi_{n}(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}))=\mathcal{Z}\big(\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}\big)
. By injectivity of
\Phi_{n}
this proves the first claim. The computation (
6.24
) concludes the description of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})
. The last claim is clear.∎

In particular, recall from (
6.18
) that for
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
and every
1\leq i\leq n
we put

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i)}=\begin{pmatrix}a^{(i)}&b^{(i)}\\
c^{(i)}&d^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{End}(V_{2})\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{(i)}.

Set

\omega^{(i)}=qa^{(i)}+q^{-1}d^{(i)}=\operatorname{qTr}_{V_{2}}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i)}\big).

By (
6.24
) we have

\Phi_{n}\big(\omega^{(i)}\big)=\Omega^{(i)},

that is,
(q-q^{-1})^{2}
times the Casimir element of the
i
-th tensorand of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{\otimes n}
, and then
\mathcal{Z}(U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{\otimes n})=\mathbb{C}(q)\big[\Omega^{(1)},\dots,\Omega^{(n)}\big]
. The proposition implies

\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2)))=\mathbb{C}(q)\big[\omega^{(1)},\dots,\omega^{(n)}\big].

More generally, by the same arguments and the results recalled in Remark
4.9
, there is an analogous description of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}(\mathfrak{g}))
for an arbitrary finite dimensional complex simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of type
A
,
B
,
C
or
D
.

Remark 6.25
.

When
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
, by using the
A
-basis of the
A
-module
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))={\mathcal{O}}_{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
given in
[
29
, Proposition 1.3]
, the arguments of Proposition
5.2
show that
\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big)=A[\omega]
. Then it is easy to see as above that the family of elements
\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}{}_{\lambda_{i}}\omega^{(i)}
form an
A
-basis of
\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big)
, and that
\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big)=A\big[\omega^{(1)},\dots,\omega^{(n)}\big]
.

For every
\lambda\in P_{+}
denote

{}_{\lambda}\eta=\operatorname{qTr}_{V_{\lambda}}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(1)}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\lambda}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n)}\big).

In the specific case of
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
, let us put

\eta:=\operatorname{qTr}_{V_{2}}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(1)}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n)}\big).

(6.25)

Lemma 6.26
.

\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\big)
contains the commutative algebra generated over
\mathbb{C}(q)
by the elements
{}_{\lambda_{1}}\omega^{(1)},\dots,{}_{\lambda_{n}}\omega^{(n)}
and
{}_{\lambda}\eta
for all
\lambda,\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in P_{+}
.

Proof.

Clearly
\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\big)
contains
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n})^{U_{q}}
, whence the elements
{}_{\lambda_{1}}\omega^{(1)},\dots,{}_{\lambda_{n}}\omega^{(n)}
for every
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in P_{+}
, by Propositions
6.24
and
6.19
. Moreover, by this result
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
is the centralizer of
\Delta^{(n-1)}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1})
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
. Therefore
\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\big)\supset\Delta^{(n-1)}(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}))
. By Proposition
4.8
and the relation (
6.19
), the elements
{}_{\lambda}\eta
,
\lambda\in P_{+}
, form a basis of
\Delta^{(n-1)}(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}))
. The result follows. ∎

Note that the
\mathbb{C}(q)
-algebra generated by the elements
{}_{\lambda_{1}}\omega^{(1)},\dots,{}_{\lambda_{n}}\omega^{(n)}
and
{}_{\lambda}\eta
for all weights
\lambda,\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in P_{+}
is the polynomial algebra generated by these elements when
\lambda,\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
are fundamental weights. We have even a much better result:

Theorem 6.27
.

We have an isomorphism

\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\big)\cong\Delta^{(n-1)}(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}))\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1})^{\otimes n}.

Therefore
\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\big)
is the polynomial algebra generated over
\mathbb{C}(q)
by the elements
{}_{\lambda_{1}}\omega^{(1)}
,
\dots
,
{}_{\lambda_{n}}\omega^{(n)}
and
{}_{\lambda}\eta
for all fundamental weights
\lambda,\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
. In particular, when
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
we have

\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))}\big)=\mathbb{C}(q)\big[\omega^{(1)},\dots,\omega^{(n)},\eta\big]

and

\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{U_{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))}\big)=A\big[\omega^{(1)},\dots,\omega^{(n)},\eta\big].

This is a consequence of the following lemmas, which are interesting by themselves. Denote by
U_{h}:=U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
the Hopf algebra over
{\mathbb{C}}[[h]]
generated topologically (in the
h
-adic sense) by
E_{i}
,
F_{i}
,
H_{i}
, where
i=1,\dots,m
, satisfying the relations
[H_{i},H_{j}]=0
,
[H_{i},E_{j}]=a_{ij}E_{j}
,
[H_{i},F_{j}]=-a_{ij}F_{j}
and (
3.1
)–(
3.3
), where
K_{i}
is replaced by
q_{i}^{H_{i}}
and
q_{i}
by
e^{d_{i}h}
. By the same formula as (
6.11
) with
U_{h}
in place of
U_{q}
,
U_{h}
acts on
U_{h}^{\otimes n}
by the right adjoint action. Let
\mathcal{Z}(U_{h})
be the center of
U_{h}
.

Lemma 6.28
.

The
h
-adic completion of
\Delta^{(n-1)}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{h}))\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}[[h]]}\mathcal{Z}(U_{h})^{\otimes n}
, considered as a subalgebra of
U_{h}^{\otimes n}
, is the center of
\big(U_{h}^{\otimes n}\big)^{U_{h}}
.

Proof.

Denote by
U=U(\mathfrak{g})
the envelopping algebra of
{\mathfrak{g}}
, and by
\Delta_{0}
its canonical coproduct. We have
U_{h}/hU_{h}=U
.
As shown in
[
76
, Lemma 3.10]
, a direct application of Theorem 10.1 of
[
57
]
proves the result for
h=0
, i.e., for
U
. Because
{\mathfrak{g}}
is finite dimensional and semisimple, Drinfeld’s results in
[
34
,
35
]
show that there exists an isomorphism of algebras
\phi\colon U_{h}\rightarrow U[[h]]
, equal to the identity on the quotient
U_{h}/hU_{h}
, and there exists an invertible element
J\in U^{\otimes 2}[[h]]
such that
\forall x\in U_{h},(\phi\otimes\phi)(\Delta(x))=J\Delta_{0}(\phi(x))J^{-1}
. By using
J
one can easily define an invertible element
J_{n}\in U^{\otimes n}[[h]]
such that
\forall x\in U_{h}
,
(\phi^{\otimes n})(\Delta^{(n-1)}(x))=J_{n}\Delta_{0}^{(n-1)}(\phi(x))J_{n}^{-1}
. As a result the map
\psi\colon\big(U_{h}^{\otimes n}\big)^{U_{h}}\rightarrow(U^{\otimes n})^{U}[[h]]
,
\psi(x)=J_{n}^{-1}(\phi^{\otimes n}(x))J_{n}
, is an isomorphism of algebras. We obviously have
\phi(\mathcal{Z}(U_{h}))=\mathcal{Z}(U)[[h]]
. The lemma follows after having checked that

\psi^{-1}\big(\mathcal{Z}(U)^{\otimes n}[[h]]\big)=\mathcal{Z}(U_{h})^{\otimes n}
and
\psi^{-1}\big(\Delta_{0}^{(n-1)}(\mathcal{Z}(U)[[h]])\big)=\Delta^{(n-1)}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{h}))
.
∎

Lemma 6.29
.

The center of
(U_{q}^{\otimes n})^{U_{q}}
is
\mathcal{Z}\big((U_{q}^{\otimes n})^{U_{q}}\big)=\Delta^{(n-1)}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{q}))\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}(q)}\mathcal{Z}(U_{q})^{\otimes n}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.26
it only remains to prove the inclusion “
\subset
”. Let
\varphi\colon U_{A}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}[[h]]\rightarrow U_{h}
be the morphism of algebras defined by
\varphi(K_{i})=e^{hd_{i}H_{i}}
,
\varphi(E_{i})=E_{i}
,
\varphi(F_{i})=F_{i}
. Let
z\in\mathcal{Z}\big((U_{q}^{\otimes n})^{U_{q}}\big)
. Up to multiplication by an element of
A
we can assume that
z\in\mathcal{Z}\big((U_{A}^{\otimes n})^{U_{A}}\big)
. Because
\varphi^{\otimes n}(z)
commutes with
\varphi^{\otimes n}\big((U_{A}^{\otimes n})^{U_{A}}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}[[h]]\big)
, it commutes with its
h
-adic completion. Consider the family of elements
v_{[\lambda]}(a_{[\lambda]})
defined by (
6.23
) with
{}_{A}V_{[\lambda]}:={}_{A}V_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes{}_{A}V_{\lambda_{1}}
and
a_{[\lambda]}
in
\operatorname{End}_{U_{A}^{\rm res}}\big({}_{A}V_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes{}_{A}V_{\lambda_{n}}\big)
. By Proposition
6.22
(see the comments therefater) they form a
\mathbb{C}(q)
-basis of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
. Proposition
6.7
then implies that the elements
\Phi_{n}(v_{[\lambda]}\big(a_{[\lambda]}^{(k)}\big)
form a basis of
\big(U_{q}^{\otimes n}\big)^{U_{q}}
. These results still hold by working with
U_{h}
and the topological version of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
defined over
\mathbb{C}[[h]]
, so the elements
\varphi^{\otimes n}\big(\Phi_{n}\big(v_{[\lambda]}\big(a_{[\lambda]}^{(k)}\big)\big)\big)
form a
{\mathbb{C}}[[h]]
-topological basis of
\big(U_{h}^{\otimes n}\big)^{U_{h}}
. Therefore
\varphi^{\otimes n}(z)
centralizes
\big(U_{h}^{\otimes n}\big)^{U_{h}}
, whence
\varphi^{\otimes n}(z)\in\Delta^{(n-1)}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{h}))\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}[[h]]}\mathcal{Z}(U_{h})^{\otimes n}
by Lemma
6.28
. This proves
z\in\Delta^{(n-1)}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{q}))\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}(q)}\mathcal{Z}(U_{q})^{\otimes n}
. ∎

Proof of Theorem
6.27
.

We have an isomorphism
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\cong\big(\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}\big)^{U_{q}}
by Proposition
6.7
, and
\mathcal{Z}\big(\big(\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}\big)^{U_{q}}\big)=\Delta^{(n-1)}\big(\mathcal{Z}(\tilde{U}_{q})\big)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}(q)}\mathcal{Z}(\tilde{U}_{q})^{\otimes n}
by the last lemma applied to
\tilde{U}_{q}
instead of
U_{q}
. But
\Phi_{n}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{Z}(\tilde{U}_{q})^{\otimes n}\big)=\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1})^{\otimes n}
by Proposition
6.24
, which is the vector space generated by
{}_{\lambda_{1}}\omega^{(1)},\dots,{}_{\lambda_{n}}\omega^{(n)}
for
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in P_{+}
(by Proposition
4.8
), and
\Phi_{n}^{-1}\big(\Delta^{(n-1)}\big(\mathcal{Z}(\tilde{U}_{q})\big)\big)
is the vector space generated by
{}_{\lambda}\eta
for all
\lambda\in P_{+}
(by Proposition
4.8
and the relation (
6.19
)). This proves all claims but the last one, which in turn follows from
\mathcal{Z}\big(\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\big)=\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\big)\cap{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
, and the fact that
\omega^{(1)},\dots,\omega^{(n)}
,
\eta\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
.
∎

6.7
Specializations

Let
\epsilon\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
. We defined the unrestricted specialization
U_{\epsilon}:=U_{A}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon}
of
U_{q}
in (
3.8
). Recall that
{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}
is the
A
-module
{\mathbb{C}}
, where
q
acts by multiplication by
\epsilon
. Similarly, the unrestricted specialisation of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
at
\epsilon
is the
U_{\epsilon}
-module algebra

{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}={\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}.

We need to consider the specialization at
q=\epsilon
of the Alekseev map. By Lemma
6.10
and Proposition
6.12
we know that
\Phi_{n}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{A}^{\otimes n}
is an embedding of
U_{A}
-module algebras, and in the case
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
it maps into
U_{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{{\otimes}n}
. Moreover, by Proposition
6.16
the latter embedding extends to an isomorphism
\Phi_{n}\colon{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\rightarrow U_{A}^{\prime}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{{\otimes}n}
.

When
q=\epsilon
the formulas (
5.3
) show that
\Phi_{1}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\rightarrow U_{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is an embedding, and
\Phi_{1}\colon{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\rightarrow U_{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
an isomorphism. For an arbitrary
\mathfrak{g}
,
\Phi_{1}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}
is also an embedding; this follows from the following facts. In
[
29
, Sections 4 and 6]
, De Concini–Lyubashenko introduced an embedding of algebras
\mu^{\prime\prime}\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{A}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{A}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})\otimes\tilde{U}_{A}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})
(where
\tilde{U}_{A}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm})
is the subalgebra of
\tilde{U}_{A}
associated to the Borel subalgebra
\mathfrak{b}_{\pm}
of
\mathfrak{g}
), and they proved that it affords an embedding
\mu^{\prime\prime}_{\epsilon}\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})\otimes\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})
. We have
\Phi_{1}=m\circ({\rm id}\otimes S^{-1})\circ\mu^{\prime\prime}
. Moreover,
\operatorname{Im}(\mu^{\prime\prime})
is contained in the subalgebra of
\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})\otimes\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})
with basis elements

\bar{F}_{\beta_{1}}^{n_{1}}\cdots\bar{F}_{\beta_{N}}^{n_{N}}K_{n_{1}\beta_{1}+\dots+n_{N}\beta_{N}}K_{\lambda}\otimes K_{-\lambda}K_{-p_{1}\beta_{1}-\dots-p_{N}\beta_{N}}\bar{E}_{\beta_{1}}^{p_{1}}\cdots\bar{E}_{\beta_{N}}^{p_{N}},

where
\lambda\in P
and
n_{1},\dots,n_{N},p_{1},\dots,p_{N}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. The map
m\circ\big({\rm id}\otimes S^{-1}\big)
sends this basis to a free family of
\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}
. As a result
\Phi_{1}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}
is injective. Since
\Phi_{n}
differs from
\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}
by a linear isomorphism (see (
6.10
)), it follows that

\Phi_{n}\colon\ {\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\to\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}

is an embedding of
U_{\epsilon}
-module algebras.

Moreover, in the
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
case, by Proposition
6.16
and the fact that
\Phi_{1}\colon{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\rightarrow U_{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
is an isomorphism, it follows that

\Phi_{n}\colon\ {}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\rightarrow U_{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))^{\otimes n}

(6.26)

is an isomorphism. Note that, by Lemma
4.11
, when
n=1
we have an isomorphism

\Phi_{1}\colon\ {\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\to\tilde{U}_{A}^{\rm lf}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}.

It is important to note that taking the specialization at a root of unity
q=\epsilon
and taking locally finite elements are non commuting operations. For instance
\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}
is a free module of finite rank over its center (see, e.g.,
[
25
, Section 9.2]
). Hence it coincides with its subalgebra of locally finite elements
(\tilde{U}_{\epsilon})^{\rm lf}
. On another hand,
\tilde{U}_{A}^{\rm lf}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}
is strictly contained in
\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}
; for instance it does not contain the elements
K_{i}
.

The algebra
\tilde{U}_{q}^{\rm lf}
is very complicated
[
51
]
, we know neither generators nor basis for arbitrary
\mathfrak{g}
. This hurdle prevents us to give a precise description of
\tilde{U}_{A}^{\rm lf}
,
\tilde{U}_{A}^{\rm lf}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}
, and
\big(\tilde{U}_{A}^{\otimes n}\big)^{\rm lf}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}
for
n>1
.

Finally, recall that
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}
is the centralizer of
\Delta^{(n-1)}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\big)
, see Proposition
6.19
. By the same arguments the algebra
({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{U_{\epsilon}}
of
U_{\epsilon}
-invariant elements of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
is the centralizer of
\Delta^{(n-1)}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
. Therefore we have an inclusion
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})\subset({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{U_{\epsilon}}
, and since clearly
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\allowbreak\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon}
is a subset of
({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{U_{\epsilon}}
, multiplication defines a morphism of algebras

\big(\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon}\big)\otimes\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})\rightarrow({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{U_{\epsilon}}.

In the rest of this paper we will simplify notations by setting

\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}:=\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon}.

(6.27)

The arguments of Proposition
6.11
apply as well to the specialization
q=\epsilon
(using that
\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}
has no non trivial zero divisors), so we have:

Proposition 6.30
.

The algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
does not have non trivial zero divisors, and therefore the subalgebras
({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{U_{\epsilon}}
and
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}
too.

7
Center of
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))}
and quantum coadjoint action

7.1
Center of
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))}

From now on
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
and
\epsilon
is a primitive
l
-th root of unity. We assume that
l\geq 3
and
l
is odd. We make this latter choice to simplify the exposition; the case of
l
even can be treated in a similar way, as all our constructions below rely on the description of the center of
U_{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, which is done for all primitive roots of unity
\epsilon
in
[
27
, Section 3]
. We omit
\mathfrak{g}
from the notations of the various algebras, and denote
U_{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
by
U_{\epsilon}
, etc.

By the relations (
5.3
) and (
5.4
),
\Phi_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})=\tilde{U}_{A}^{\rm lf}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}=U_{A}^{\rm lf}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}
is the algebra generated over
\mathbb{C}
by
\Omega
,
EK^{-1}
,
F
and
K^{-1}
. Adding the generator
K
gives
U_{\epsilon}
. By results of
[
27
]
, the center
\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
of
U_{\epsilon}
is the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra generated by
E^{l}
,
F^{l}
,
K^{\pm l}
and
\Omega
satisfying the relation

\prod_{j=1}^{l}(\Omega-c_{j})=\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{2l}E^{l}F^{l}+K^{l}+K^{-l}-2,

(7.1)

where
c_{j}=\epsilon^{j}+\epsilon^{-j}
. Let
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
be the subalgebra of
\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
generated by
E^{l}
,
F^{l}
and
K^{\pm l}
. It is a sub-Hopf algebra of
U_{\epsilon}
, with

\displaystyle\Delta\big(K^{\pm l}\big)=K^{\pm l}\otimes K^{\pm l},\qquad\Delta\big(E^{l}\big)=E^{l}\otimes K^{l}+1\otimes E^{l},\qquad\Delta\big(F^{l}\big)=K^{-l}\otimes F^{l}+F^{l}\otimes 1,

\displaystyle S\big(E^{l}\big)=-E^{l}K^{-l},\qquad S\big(F^{l}\big)=-K^{l}F^{l},\qquad S\big(K^{\pm l}\big)=K^{\mp l},

\displaystyle\varepsilon\big(E^{l}\big)=\varepsilon\big(F^{l}\big)=0,\qquad\varepsilon\big(K^{l}\big)=1.

Consider the sequence of polynomials
T_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, defined recursively by

T_{0}(x)=2,\qquad T_{1}(x)=x,\qquad T_{k}(x)=xT_{k-1}(x)-T_{k-2}(x)\qquad{\rm for}\quad k\geq 2.

(7.2)

Note that
T_{k}(x)/2
is the
k
-th Chebyshev polynomial of the first type in the variable
x/2
. One has
T_{k}\big(u+u^{-1}\big)=u^{k}+u^{-k}
, from which one derives easily that
\textstyle T_{l}(x)-2=\prod_{j=1}^{l}(x-c_{j})
.
Therefore, the relation (
7.1
) can be written as:

T_{l}(\Omega)=\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{2l}E^{l}F^{l}+K^{l}+K^{-l}.

(7.3)

Since
l
is odd, it is also equivalent to

\prod_{j=1}^{l}(\Omega+c_{j})=\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{2l}E^{l}F^{l}+K^{l}+K^{-l}+2.

(7.4)

By (
5.3
) we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(\omega)=\Omega,\qquad\Phi_{1}\big(b^{l}\big)=\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l}F^{l},

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}\big(c^{l}\big)=\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l}(EK^{-1})^{l},\qquad\Phi_{1}\big(d^{l}\big)=K^{-l}.

(7.5)

Hence

T_{l}(\omega)=b^{l}c^{l}d^{-l}+d^{-l}+d^{l}.

(7.6)

Using that
\Phi_{1}
is equivariant, injective, and surjective when extended to the localization, we deduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})=\mathbb{C}\big[\omega,b^{l},c^{l},d^{\pm l}\big]/\mathcal{I},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})=\mathbb{C}\big[\omega,b^{l},c^{l},d^{l}\big]/\mathcal{I},

where
\mathcal{I}
is the ideal of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
generated by
\big(T_{l}(\omega)-d^{l}\big)d^{l}-b^{l}c^{l}-1
. By the presentation of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
in Lemma
5.7
, we have

\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})=({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{U_{\epsilon}}.

Alternatively, we have
\big(U_{A}^{\rm lf}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}\big)^{U_{\epsilon}}=\mathcal{Z}\big(U_{A}^{\rm lf}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}\big)
by the arguments of Corollary
6.19
for
n=1
. Therefore
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})=\Phi_{1}^{-1}\big(\big(U_{A}^{\rm lf}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}\big)^{U_{\epsilon}}\big)=({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{U_{\epsilon}}
.

We can now define a notion of quantum Frobenius homomorphism for
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon},
similar in spirit to the one defined for
\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
in
[
71
]
, which is a map
\mathcal{O}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
. Consider the specialization
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{1}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
at
q=1
. We have
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{1}=\mathcal{O}_{1}=\mathcal{O}(G)
as commutative algebras (with
G={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C}))
). Denote by
\underline{a}
,
\underline{b}
,
\underline{c}
,
\underline{d}
the images of the generators
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
under the specialization map
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{1}
. They satisfy
\underline{a}\underline{d}-\underline{b}\underline{c}=1.

Let us define
Q_{l}\in{\mathbb{C}}[X,Y]
by
Q_{l}(X,Y)=T_{l}\big(\epsilon X+\epsilon^{-1}Y\big)-X^{l}-Y^{l}
.
Recall that
a
and
d
commute. So we put:

Definition 7.1
.

The Frobenius map
\operatorname{Fr}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
is the homomorphism of algebras given by

\operatorname{Fr}(\underline{a})=a^{l}+Q_{l}(a,d)=T_{l}(\omega)-d^{l},\qquad\operatorname{Fr}(\underline{b})=b^{l},\qquad\operatorname{Fr}(\underline{c})=c^{l},\qquad\operatorname{Fr}(\underline{d})=d^{l}.

We shall denote

\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}=\begin{pmatrix}\underline{a}&\underline{b}\\
\underline{c}&\underline{d}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}=\begin{pmatrix}a^{l}+Q_{l}(a,d)&b^{l}\\
c^{l}&d^{l}\end{pmatrix}\!.

Note that:

•

\det\big(\!\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}\big)-1=(T_{l}(\omega)-d^{l})d^{l}-b^{l}c^{l}-1
, i.e., the generator of the ideal
\mathcal{I}
.

•

T_{l}\big(\!\operatorname{qTr}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}\big)\big)=\operatorname{Tr}\big(\!\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}\big)
.

The notions above can be developed similarly for every
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
,
n\geq 1
. First, recall the additional generators
\delta^{(i)-1}
of the localization
{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
introduced in Definition
6.15
.

Proposition 7.2
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})=\mathbb{C}\big[\omega^{(i)},b^{(i)l},c^{(i)l},d^{(i)l},\,i=1,\dots,n\big]/\big(\mathcal{I}^{(i)},\,i=1,\dots,n\big),

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})=\mathbb{C}\big[\omega^{(i)},b^{(i)l},c^{(i)l},d^{(i)l},\big(\delta^{(i)l}\big)^{-1},\,i=1,\dots,n\big]/\big(\mathcal{I}^{(i)},\,i=1,\dots,n\big),

where
\mathcal{I}^{(i)}
is the ideal generated by the element
\big(T_{l}\big(\omega^{(i)}\big)-d^{(i)l}\big)d^{(i)l}-b^{(i)l}c^{(i)l}-1
.

Proof.

First we prove that
\Phi_{n}\big(b^{(i)l}\big),\Phi_{n}\big(c^{(i)l}\big),\Phi_{n}\big(d^{(i)l}\big)\in\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n})
for every
i=1,\dots,n
. By injectivity of
\Phi_{n}
it will follow that
b^{(i)l},c^{(i)l},d^{(i)l}\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
. By (
7.5
) the claim is true for
i=n
. Let
1\leq i\leq n-1
, and denote
R^{(i)}:=R_{V_{2}n}\cdots R_{V_{2}i+1}\in\operatorname{End}(V_{2})\otimes\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
, with
q
an indeterminate, as in the previous sections. Define
r_{11}^{(i)},r_{12}^{(i)},r_{21}^{(i)},r_{22}^{(i)}\in\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
by

R^{(i)}=\begin{pmatrix}r_{11}^{(i)}&r_{12}^{(i)}\\[4.30554pt]
r_{21}^{(i)}&r_{22}^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}

and
m_{11}^{(i)},m_{12}^{(i)},m_{21}^{(i)},m_{22}^{(i)}\in 1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes U_{q}^{\rm lf}\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-i)}
by

{\rm id}_{V_{2}}\otimes\big(1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes\Phi_{1}\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-i)}\big)\begin{pmatrix}a^{(i)}&b^{(i)}\\
c^{(i)}&d^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}m_{11}^{(i)}&m_{12}^{(i)}\\
m_{21}^{(i)}&m_{22}^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}\!,

where as in (
6.18
) we put

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i)}=\begin{pmatrix}a^{(i)}&b^{(i)}\\
c^{(i)}&d^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{End}(V_{2})\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(i)}.

By Proposition
5.4
,
m_{11}^{(i)}
,
m_{12}^{(i)}
,
m_{21}^{(i)}
,
m_{22}^{(i)}
generate the subalgebra
1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes U_{q}^{\rm lf}\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-i)}
of
U_{q}^{\otimes n}
. These elements satisfy the relations (
5.2
), with
m_{11}^{(i)}
,
m_{12}^{(i)}
,
m_{21}^{(i)}
,
m_{22}^{(i)}
replacing
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
respectively, and they commute with
r_{11}^{(i)}
,
r_{12}^{(i)}
,
r_{21}^{(i)}
,
r_{22}^{(i)}
, since the non trivial tensor components of the latters do not lie on the
i
-th tensorand of
U_{q}^{\otimes n}
. Denote
\mathfrak{i}_{in}\colon\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes(n-i)}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{q}^{\otimes n}
the identification map with the last
n-i
tensorands. We have
R^{(i)}=\big(\pi_{V_{2}}\otimes\mathfrak{i}_{in}\circ\Delta^{(n-i-1)}\big)(R)
by the relation (
2.2
). So the formulas (
6.15
) and (
6.16
) yield

r_{21}^{(i)}=0,\qquad r_{22}^{(i)}=r_{11}^{(i)-1},\qquad r_{12}^{(i)}r_{11}^{(i)}=qr_{11}^{(i)}r_{12}^{(i)},\qquad r_{12}^{(i)}=r_{11}^{(i)}f_{12}^{(i)}

and

R^{(i)-1}=\begin{pmatrix}r_{11}^{(i)-1}&-qr_{12}^{(i)}\\[2.15277pt]
0&r_{11}^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}\!,

where

r_{11}^{(i)}=\mathfrak{i}_{in}\circ\Delta^{(n-i-1)}\big(q^{H/2}\big),\qquad f_{12}^{(i)}=\mathfrak{i}_{in}\circ\Delta^{(n-i-1)}\big(\big(q-q^{-1}\big)F\big).

Therefore

\displaystyle R^{(i)}\big({\rm id}_{V_{2}}\otimes\big(1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes\Phi_{1}\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-i)}\big)\big)\begin{pmatrix}a^{(i)}&b^{(i)}\\
c^{(i)}&d^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}R^{(i)-1}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\begin{pmatrix}m_{11}^{(i)}+r_{12}^{(i)}r_{11}^{(i)-1}m_{21}^{(i)}&*\\[2.15277pt]
r_{11}^{(i)-2}m_{21}^{(i)}&-qr_{11}^{(i)-1}r_{12}^{(i)}m_{21}^{(i)}+m_{22}^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}\!.

Note also that, by definition,

\big({\rm id}_{V_{2}}\otimes\Phi_{n}\big)\begin{pmatrix}a^{(i)}&b^{(i)}\\
c^{(i)}&d^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}=R^{(i)}\big({\rm id}_{V_{2}}\otimes\big(1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes\Phi_{1}\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-i)}\big)\big)\begin{pmatrix}a^{(i)}&b^{(i)}\\
c^{(i)}&d^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}R^{(i)-1}.

Hence

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}\big(c^{(i)}\big)=r_{11}^{(i)-2}m_{21}^{(i)},

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}\big(d^{(i)}\big)=-qr_{11}^{(i)-1}r_{12}^{(i)}m_{21}^{(i)}+m_{22}^{(i)}.

Let now take the specialization
q=\epsilon
as above, a primitive
l
-th root of unity where
l\geq 3
is odd. We have

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}\big(c^{(i)l}\big)=\big(r_{11}^{(i)-2}m_{21}^{(i)}\big)^{l}=r_{11}^{(i)-2l}m_{21}^{(i)l},

(7.7)

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}\big(d^{(i)l}\big)=\big({-}\epsilon r_{11}^{(i)-1}r_{12}^{(i)}m_{21}^{(i)}+m_{22}^{(i)}\big)^{l}=-r_{11}^{(i)-l}r_{12}^{(i)l}m_{21}^{(i)l}+m_{22}^{(i)l},

(7.8)

by using
m_{21}^{(i)}m_{22}^{(i)}=\epsilon^{2}m_{22}^{(i)}m_{21}^{(i)}
, the
q
-binomial formula (see, e.g.,
[
54
, Proposition IV.2.2]
), and the vanishing at
q=\epsilon
of the
q
-Gauss binomial coefficients
[l]_{q}/[k]_{q}[l-k]_{q}
,
0<k<l
. Now

m_{21}^{(i)l}=\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l}1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes\big(K^{-1}E\big)^{l}\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-i)},\qquad r_{11}^{(i)-2l}=\mathfrak{i}_{in}\circ\Delta^{(n-i-1)}\big(K^{-l}\big).

These are central elements of
U_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
, so
\Phi_{n}\big(c^{(i)l}\big)
is central. As

r_{11}^{(i)-l}r_{12}^{(i)l}=f_{12}^{(i)l}=\mathfrak{i}_{in}\circ\Delta^{(n-i-1)}\big(\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l}F^{l}\big)

and
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
is a Hopf algebra,
r_{11}^{(i)-l}r_{12}^{(i)l}
is a central element of

U_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
. Again,
m_{21}^{(i)l}
and
m_{22}^{(i)l}
being central in
U_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
,
\Phi_{n}\big(d^{(i)l}\big)
is central.

Finally, recalling that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{(i)}
is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}
, by specializing
q
to
\epsilon
we get

d^{(i)l}T_{l}\big(\omega^{(i)}\big)-d^{(i)2l}-1=b^{(i)l}c^{(i)l}.

(7.9)

We know that
\omega^{(i)}\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
and we just proved that
d^{(i)l},c^{(i)l}\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
. Therefore, for every
x\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
we get
b^{(i)l}c^{(i)l}x=xb^{(i)l}c^{(i)l}=b^{(i)l}xc^{(i)l}
, i.e.,
\big(b^{(i)l}x-xb^{(i)l}\big)c^{(i)l}=0,

c^{(i)l}
is not a zero divisor, we deduce
b^{(i)l}x-xb^{(i)l}=0
. Hence
b^{(i)l}
is central in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
.

A formula of
\Phi_{n}\big(b^{(i)l}\big)
can be obtained as follows. Recall that (
6.24
) implies

\Phi_{n}\big(\omega^{(i)}\big)=\Omega^{(i)}.

(7.10)

This can also be checked by using the above formulas:

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}\big(\omega^{(i)}\big)=q\Phi_{n}\big(a^{(i)}\big)+q^{-1}\Phi_{n}\big(d^{(i)}\big)=q\big(m_{11}^{(i)}+q^{-1}f_{12}^{(i)}m_{21}^{(i)}\big)+q^{-1}\big(m_{22}^{(i)}-qf_{12}^{(i)}m_{21}^{(i)}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Phi_n\big(\omega^{(i)}\big)}{}=qm_{11}^{(i)}+q^{-1}m_{22}^{(i)}.

Then, by applying
\Phi_{n}
to the relation (
7.9
) and using (
7.7
), (
7.8
) and (
7.10
), one finds

\Phi_{n}\big(b^{(i)l}\big)=-r_{11}^{(i)l}r_{12}^{(i)l}\big(T_{l}\big(\Omega^{(i)}\big)-2m_{22}^{(i)l}\big)+r_{11}^{(i)2l}m_{12}^{(i)l}-r_{12}^{(i)2l}m_{21}^{(i)l}.

(7.11)

We can now achieve the proof. Note that
\mathbb{C}\big[\omega^{(1)},\dots,\omega^{(n)}\big]=\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}
by Remark
6.25
. The inclusion
\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}\subset\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
is clear, and the natural embedding
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
maps
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
into
\mathcal{Z}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
. By the case
n=1
the elements
\omega^{(i)}
,
b^{(i)l}
,
c^{(i)l}
,
d^{(i)l}
generate
\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}{}^{(i)}\big)
, with ideal of relations
\mathcal{I}^{(i)}
. The set of these ideals for
i=1,\dots,n
provide all the relations in
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
, for there are no others in
\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{n})
(as shows, e.g., Corollary
7.4
below). Therefore one has inclusions

\mathbb{C}\big[\omega^{(i)},b^{(i)l},c^{(i)l},d^{(i)l},\,i=1,\dots,n\big]/\big(\mathcal{I}^{(i)},\,i=1,\dots,n\big)\subset\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\big)\subset\mathcal{Z}\big({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\big).

The conclusion follows at once, since by their very definition the elements
\delta^{(i)\pm l}
are central in
{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}{}
, and
\Phi_{n}
maps the algebra generated by them and the left-hand side isomorphically to
U_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
.∎

Analogously to the case
n=1
, recalling that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{1}=\mathcal{O}(G)
we put:

Definition 7.3
.

The Frobenius map
{\rm Fr}\colon\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{1}\big)^{\otimes n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\big)
is the homomorphism of algebras given by

\operatorname{Fr}\big(\underline{a}^{(i)}\big)=a^{(i)l}+Q_{l}\big(a^{(i)},d^{(i)}\big),\qquad\operatorname{Fr}\big(\underline{b}^{(i)}\big)=b^{(i)l},\qquad\operatorname{Fr}\big(\underline{c}^{(i)}\big)=c^{(i)l},\qquad\operatorname{Fr}\big(\underline{d}^{(i)}\big)=d^{(i)l}.

We shall denote

\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(i)}=\begin{pmatrix}\underline{a}^{(i)}&\underline{b}^{(i)}\\
\underline{c}^{(i)}&\underline{d}^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(i)}=\begin{pmatrix}a^{(i)l}+Q_{l}\big(a^{(i)},d^{(i)}\big)&b^{(i)l}\\[2.15277pt]
c^{(i)l}&d^{(i)l}\end{pmatrix}\!,

(7.12)

where we recall that
a^{(i)l}+Q_{l}\big(a^{(i)},d^{(i)}\big)=T_{l}\big(\omega^{(i)}\big)-d^{(i)l}
. We can express
\Phi_{n}\circ\big(\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}\big)^{-1}
on the center as follows. Set

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(i)}=\begin{pmatrix}r_{11}^{(i)l}&r_{12}^{(i)l}\\[2.15277pt]
0&r_{11}^{(i)-l}\end{pmatrix}\!,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(i)}=\big(1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes\Phi_{1}\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-i)}\big)\big(\!\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(i)}\big)=\begin{pmatrix}T_{l}(\Omega^{(i)})-m_{22}^{(i)l}&m_{12}^{(i)l}\\[2.15277pt]
m_{21}^{(i)l}&m_{22}^{(i)l}\end{pmatrix}\!,

where we use the notations in the proof of Proposition
7.2
. These matrices belong to
\operatorname{End}(V_{2})\otimes\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\tilde{U}_{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}
and
\operatorname{End}(V_{2})\otimes\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}
respectively. Here we note that
\mathcal{Z}(\tilde{U}_{\epsilon})
is generated by
\Omega
,
E^{l}
,
F^{l}
and
K^{\pm\frac{l}{2}}
satisfying the relation (
7.1
), and we define
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\tilde{U}_{\epsilon})
as the subalgebra generated by
E^{l}
,
F^{l}
and
K^{\pm\frac{l}{2}}
.

By using (
7.7
), (
7.8
), (
7.10
) and (
7.11
) it is easy to check that:

Corollary 7.4
.

The map
\Phi_{n}\circ\big(\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}\big)^{-1}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}
is given by

\big({\rm id}_{V_{2}}\otimes\big(\Phi_{n}\circ(\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n})^{-1}\big)\big)\big(\mathcal{M}^{(i)}\big)=\mathcal{R}^{(i)}\mathcal{M}^{(i)}\mathcal{R}^{(i)-1}.

We will find useful later to have explicit formulas. Let us introduce the following generators of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
:

x=-\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l}E^{l}K^{-l},\qquad y=\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l}F^{l},\qquad z^{\pm 1}=K^{\pm l}.

(7.13)

Similarly, denote by
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\tilde{U}_{\epsilon})\subset\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}
the subalgebra generated by
x
,
y
,
z
and

z^{\prime}{}^{\pm 1}=K^{\pm\frac{l}{2}}.

For every
a\in\big\{x,y,z^{\pm 1}\big\}
put
a^{(i)}=1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes a\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-i)}
. We can view
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}
as a polynomial algebra in the variables
x^{(i)}
,
y^{(i)}
,
\big(z^{\pm 1}\big)^{(i)}
, and
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\tilde{U}_{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}
as a polynomial algebra in
x^{(i)}
,
y^{(i)}
,
\big(z^{\prime}{}^{\pm 1}\big)^{(i)}
.
Then

\mathcal{M}^{(i)}=\begin{pmatrix}T_{l}\big(\Omega^{(i)}\big)-(z^{-1})^{(i)}&y^{(i)}\\[2.15277pt]
-x^{(i)}&(z^{-1})^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}z^{(i)}\big(1-x^{(i)}y^{(i)}\big)&y^{(i)}\\[2.15277pt]
-x^{(i)}&(z^{-1})^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}\!.

(7.14)

Also,

\displaystyle r_{11}^{(i)l}=\big(z^{\prime}{}^{-1}\big)^{(i+1)}\cdots\big(z^{\prime}{}^{-1}\big)^{(n)},

(7.15)

\displaystyle r_{12}^{(i)l}=\big(z^{\prime}{}^{-1}\big)^{(i+1)}\cdots\big(z^{\prime}{}^{-1}\big)^{(n)}\bigg(y^{(i+1)}+\sum_{j=1}^{n-i-1}\big(z^{-1}\big)^{(i+1)}\cdots\big(z^{-1}\big)^{(i+j)}y^{(i+j+1)}\bigg)

(7.16)

yields

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}\big(c^{(i)l}\big)=r_{11}^{(i)-2l}m_{21}^{(i)l}=-x^{(i)}\big(z^{-1}\big)^{(i+1)}\cdots\big(z^{-1}\big)^{(n)},

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}\big(d^{(i)l}\big)=m_{22}^{(i)l}-m_{21}^{(i)l}r_{11}^{(i)-l}r_{12}^{(i)l}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Phi_n\big(d^{(i)l}\big)}{}=\big(z^{-1}\big)^{(i)}+x^{(i)}\bigg(y^{(i+1)}+\sum_{j=1}^{n-i-1}\big(z^{-1}\big)^{(i+1)}\cdots\big(z^{-1}\big)^{(i+j)}y^{(i+j+1)}\bigg).

One can readily express
\Phi_{n}\big(b^{(i)l}\big)=-r_{11}^{(i)l}r_{12}^{(i)l}\big((T_{l}(\Omega^{(i)})-2m_{22}^{(i)l}\big)+r_{11}^{(i)2l}m_{12}^{(i)l}-r_{12}^{(i)2l}m_{21}^{(i)l}
as well as a polynomial in the variables
x^{(j)}
,
y^{(j)}
,
\big(z^{\pm 1}\big)^{(j)}
.

In
[
71
]
, Parshall–Wang showed that the quantum Frobenius homomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
they defined is a morphism of coalgebra. As we now explain, our quantum Frobenius homomorphism
{\rm Fr}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
satisfies a similar property.

Recall the algebra morphism
\Delta^{(n-1)}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
(see Proposition
6.18
). Denote again by
\Delta^{(n-1)}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
its evaluation at
\epsilon
. For every
1\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leq n
we define
\iota_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,k}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
to be the identification map of the
j
-th tensorand of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,k}^{\epsilon}
with the
i_{j}
-th tensorand of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
,
1\leq j\leq k
.

Lemma 7.5
.

For every
1\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leq n
we have

\iota_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\circ\Delta^{(k-1)}\big(\!\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}\big)=\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(i_{1})}\cdots\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(i_{k})}.

(7.17)

Proof.

First we show (
7.17
) in the case
k=n=2
, that is

\Delta\big(\!\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}\big)=\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(1)}\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(2)}.

(7.18)

The commutation relations of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!\!(1)}
and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!\!(2)}
being complicated, we cannot compute the matrix components directly (which is the way used in
[
71
]
for their quantum Frobenius homomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
, where such computations reduce to the
q
-binomial identity). Instead, we first use the Alekseev map. Indeed, because
\Phi_{2}
is an algebra embedding, it is sufficient to show that (
7.18
) holds after having been composed with
\Phi_{2}
. We have

\Phi_{1}\big(\!\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}\big)=\begin{pmatrix}T_{l}(\Omega)-K^{-l}&\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l}F^{l}\\[2.15277pt]
\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l}(EK^{-1})^{l}&K^{-l}\end{pmatrix}\!.

Therefore (
7.18
) is a consequence of the following four equations in
U_{\epsilon}^{\otimes 2}
:

\displaystyle\Delta\big(K^{-l}\big)=\Phi_{2}\big(c^{(1)l}b^{(2)l}+d^{(1)l}d^{(2)l}\big),

\displaystyle\Delta\big(\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l}\big(EK^{-1}\big)^{l}\big)=\Phi_{2}\big(c^{(1)l}\big(a^{(2)l}+Q_{l}\big(a^{(2)},d^{(2)}\big)\big)+d^{(1)l}c^{(2)l}\big),

\displaystyle\Delta\big(\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l}F^{l}\big)=\Phi_{2}\big(\big(a^{(1)l}+Q_{l}\big(a^{(1)},d^{(1)}\big)\big)b^{(2)l}+b^{(1)l}d^{(2)l}\big),

\displaystyle\Delta(T_{l}(\Omega)-K^{-l})=\Phi_{2}\big(\big(a^{(1)l}+Q_{l}\big(a^{(1)},d^{(1)}\big)\big)\big(a^{(2)l}+Q_{l}\big(a^{(2)},d^{(2)}\big)\big)+b^{(1)l}c^{(2)l}\big).

We listed them in order of complexity. The first equation, using the explicit expression of
\Phi_{2}
on components, is rewritten as

\displaystyle\Delta\big(K^{-l}\big)=\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{2l}\big(K^{-1}E\otimes K^{-1}\big)^{l}\big(1\otimes F^{l}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Delta\big(K^{-l}\big)=}{}+\big(K^{-1}\otimes 1-\epsilon\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{2}K^{-1}E\otimes F\big)^{l}\big(1\otimes K^{-l}\big).

This relation holds thanks to the
q
-binomial identity. The other three relations, although more complicated, are shown similarly by a direct computation and using the expressions (
7.7
), (
7.8
), (
7.10
) and (
7.11
).

Because the relative commutations relations of
{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(1)},\dots,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(k)}
and
{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(i_{1})},\dots,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(i_{k})}
for general sequences
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}
are the same, the proof of (
7.17
) follows immediately from the case where
i_{j}=j
, for
1\leq j\leq k
. This, in turn, follows from (
7.18
) by induction on
k
. This concludes the proof.
∎

The following consequence of the Lemma will be a key tool in Section
9
.

Proposition 7.6
.

For every
1\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leq n
we have

T_{l}\big(\!\operatorname{qTr}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i_{1})}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i_{k})}\big)\big)=\operatorname{Tr}\big(\!\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(i_{1})}\cdots\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(i_{k})}\big).

In particular, this element is central in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

Let
\iota_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,k}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
be the identification map of the
j
-th tensorand of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,k}^{\epsilon}
with the
i_{j}
-th tensorand of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
,
1\leq j\leq k
. We have

\displaystyle T_{l}\big(\!\operatorname{qTr}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i_{1})}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i_{k})}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=T_{l}\big(\!\operatorname{qTr}\big(\big({\rm id}\otimes\big(\iota_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\circ\Delta^{(k-1)}\big)\big)\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}\big)\big)\big)

\displaystyle=T_{l}\big(\big(\iota_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\circ\Delta^{(k-1)}\big)\big(\!\operatorname{qTr}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}\big)\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\iota_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\circ\Delta^{(k-1)}\big)\big(T_{l}\big(\!\operatorname{qTr}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}\big)\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\iota_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\circ\Delta^{(k-1)}\big)\big(T_{l}(\omega)\big).

In the first equality we used the formula (
6.19
), the second and fourth equalities follow from definitions, and the third comes from the fact that
\Delta^{(k-1)}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,k}^{A}
is a homomorphism of algebras. By the identity (
7.6
) and the fact that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})\cong\mathbb{C}\big[b^{l},c^{l},d^{\pm l}\big]
is a Hopf subalgebra of
U_{\epsilon}
, we have
\Delta^{(k-1)}(T_{l}(\omega))\in\mathcal{Z}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{\otimes k}
. This and Proposition
7.2
imply that the above element is central in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
. Moreover

\displaystyle\iota_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\circ\Delta^{(k-1)}(T_{l}(\omega))

\displaystyle=\iota_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\circ\Delta^{(k-1)}\big(\!\operatorname{Tr}\big(\!\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}\big(\big({\rm id}\otimes\iota_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\circ\Delta^{(k-1)}\big)\big(\!\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}\big(\!\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(i_{1})}\cdots\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(i_{k})}\big),

where the first equality follows from the observations we made before Proposition
7.2
, and the others from the previous lemma.
∎

7.2
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}
-invariant central elements and
\boldsymbol{{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})}
-characters

We are going to relate
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
with the algebra of regular functions on the variety of
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
-characters of the sphere with
n+1
punctures, endowed with the Atiyah–Bott–Goldman Poisson structure. This is achieved in Section
7.2.2
. To this aim we recall a few preliminary results in the next section.

7.2.1
The quantum coadjoint action for
\boldsymbol{U_{\epsilon}}

We refer to
[
27
,
28
,
30
]
for details about the material discussed in this section. It can be formulated for any of the quantum groups
U_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{g})
, but we restrict to
U_{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
as we shall need Proposition
7.2
in Section
7.2.2
.

Consider the sets
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
and
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
of algebra homomorphisms from
\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
and
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
to
\mathbb{C}
, respectively. They are affine algebraic sets. An element of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
is called a
central character
of
U_{\epsilon}
. The inclusion
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})\subset\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
induces a regular (restriction) map

\tau\colon\ \operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})).

(7.19)

Since
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
is a polynomial algebra, any
\chi\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
is entirely defined by its values
(x_{\chi},y_{\chi},z_{\chi})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
on the tuple
(x,y,z)
of generators of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
defined in (
7.13
). By (
7.1
)–(
7.4
), any
\chi\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
is entirely defined by its values
(x_{\chi},y_{\chi},z_{\chi},\Omega_{\chi})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}
on the tuple
(x,y,z,\Omega)
of generators of
\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
, solutions to one of the equivalent equations

\prod_{j=1}^{l}(\Omega_{\chi}\mp c_{j})=-x_{\chi}y_{\chi}z_{\chi}+z_{\chi}+z_{\chi}^{-1}\mp 2,

where
c_{j}=\epsilon^{j}+\epsilon^{-j}
. Hence
\tau
has degree
l
, and
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
is a branched covering of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))=\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
of degree
l
, a hypersurface in
\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}
with quadratic singularities at the points
(0,0,\pm 1,\pm c_{j})
,
j=1,\dots,(l-1)/2
.

Because
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
is a commutative Hopf algebra,
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
has a canonical group structure defined dually by

\chi_{1}\chi_{2}(u):=(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2})\Delta(u),\qquad\chi_{1}^{-1}(u):=\chi_{1}(S(u)),\qquad e(u):=\varepsilon(u)

(7.20)

for any
u\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
and
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
, where
e\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
is the identity element. In formulas:

\displaystyle x_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}=x_{\chi_{1}}+z_{\chi_{1}}^{-1}x_{\chi_{2}},\qquad y_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}=y_{\chi_{1}}+y_{\chi_{2}}z_{\chi_{1}}^{-1},\qquad z_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}=z_{\chi_{1}}z_{\chi_{2}},

\displaystyle x_{\chi^{-1}}=-z_{\chi}x_{\chi},\qquad y_{\chi^{-1}}=-y_{\chi}z_{\chi},\qquad z_{\chi^{-1}}=z_{\chi}^{-1},

\displaystyle x_{e}=0,\qquad y_{e}=0,\qquad z_{e}=1.

This can be formulated as follows. Put
G={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
, and let
G^{*}
be the group formed by the pairs of matrices

\left(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\!,\begin{pmatrix}a^{-1}&0\\
c&a\end{pmatrix}\right)\in{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})^{\rm op}\times{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})^{\rm op},

where
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})^{\rm op}
is
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
endowed with the opposite multiplication. Set

\psi\left(\begin{pmatrix}z^{\prime}&z^{\prime}y\\
0&z^{\prime}{}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\!,\begin{pmatrix}z^{\prime}{}^{-1}&0\\
z^{\prime}x&z^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\right)=\big(x,y,z^{\prime}{}^{2}\big).

Identifying
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})))
with
\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
by mapping
\chi
to
(x_{\chi},y_{\chi},z_{\chi})
defined as above, it is readily checked that this defines a surjective morphism of algebraic groups

\psi\colon\ G^{*}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})))

with kernel the subgroup generated by
-(I,I)
, where
I
is the
2
-by-
2
identity matrix. Put

\bar{G}^{*}=G^{*}/\{\pm(I,I)\}.

We will denote the quotient isomorphism by

\bar{\psi}\colon\ \bar{G}^{*}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))).

Let us endow the ring of regular functions
\mathcal{O}(G)
with the Sklyanin–Drinfeld Poisson bracket
\{\,,\,\}
, associated to the classical
r
-matrix

\mathfrak{r}=\frac{1}{4}H\otimes H+E\otimes F\in\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}.

Recall that it can be given the following expression (see, e.g.,
[
77
]
, or
[
7
,
25
]
for a setup close to ours). First note that it is entirely determined by its values on the matrix coefficients (coordinate functions)
l_{11}
,
l_{12}
,
l_{21}
,
l_{22}
of the fundamental representation of
G
on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Put

L=\begin{pmatrix}l_{11}&l_{12}\\
l_{21}&l_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{r,s=1}^{2}E_{r}^{s}\otimes l_{rs}\in\operatorname{End}\big(\mathbb{C}^{2}\big)\otimes\mathcal{O}(G).

(7.21)

Denote by
\big\{\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!\big\}
and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}
the tensors in
\operatorname{End}\big(\mathbb{C}^{2}\big)\otimes\operatorname{End}\big(\mathbb{C}^{2}\big)\otimes\mathcal{O}(G)
defined by

\big\{\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!\big\}=\sum_{r,s,t,u=1}^{2}E_{r}^{s}\otimes E_{t}^{u}\otimes\{l_{rs},l_{tu}\},\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}=\sum_{r,s,t,u=1}^{2}E_{r}^{s}\otimes E_{t}^{u}\otimes l_{rs}l_{tu}

and as usual put
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}=\sum_{r,s=1}^{2}E_{r}^{s}\otimes 1\otimes l_{rs}
,
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}=\sum_{t,u=1}^{2}1\otimes E_{t}^{u}\otimes l_{tu}
. The
r
-matrix in the fundamental representation is

\mathfrak{r}=\frac{1}{4}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\otimes\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\otimes\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{End}\big(\mathbb{C}^{2}\big)\otimes\operatorname{End}\big(\mathbb{C}^{2}\big),

which we identify with
\mathfrak{r}\otimes 1\in\operatorname{End}\big(\mathbb{C}^{2}\big)\otimes\operatorname{End}\big(\mathbb{C}^{2}\big)\otimes\mathcal{O}(G)
. Then the bracket
\{\,,\,\}
on
\mathcal{O}(G)
is defined by the identity

\big\{\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!\big\}=\big[\mathfrak{r},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!\big]=\mathfrak{r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\mathfrak{r}.

The group
G
with the bracket
\{\,,\,\}
is a Poisson–Lie group. As such, it admits a dual Poisson–Lie group, which is precisely the group
G^{*}
defined as above. Its Poisson bracket
\{\,,\,\}_{G^{*}}
is defined by

\displaystyle\big\{\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}_{\pm},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}_{\pm}\big\}_{G^{*}}=\big[\mathfrak{r},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}_{\pm}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}_{\pm}\!\big],\qquad\big\{\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}_{+},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}_{-}\big\}_{G^{*}}=\big[\mathfrak{r},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}_{+}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}_{-}\!\big],

(7.22)

where
(L_{+},L_{-})
is the pair of matrices of coordinate functions on
G^{*}\subset{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})^{\rm op}\times{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})^{\rm op}
. We will denote by
\{\,,\,\}_{\bar{G}^{*}}
the induced bracket on
\bar{G}^{*}
. The push-forward
\psi_{*}\{\,,\,\}_{G^{*}}
is a Poisson bracket on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
that can be defined directly via the quantum coadjoint action, that we now recall.

The specialization morphism
ev_{\epsilon}\colon U_{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big[q,q^{-1}\big]\rightarrow U_{\epsilon}
,
q\mapsto\epsilon
, is surjective, with kernel the subalgebra
\big(q^{l}-q^{-l}\big)U_{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big[q,q^{-1}\big]
. Given an element
x\in U_{\epsilon}
, let us denote by
\tilde{x}\in ev_{\epsilon}^{-1}(x)
any preimage of
x
. For every
a\in\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
,
u\in U_{\epsilon}
we have
[\tilde{a},\tilde{u}]=\tilde{a}\tilde{u}-\tilde{u}\tilde{a}\in\big(q^{l}-q^{-l}\big)U_{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big[q,q^{-1}\big]
. So, let us put

D_{a}(u)=-\lim_{q\rightarrow\epsilon}\frac{\big[\tilde{a},\tilde{u}\big]}{l\big(q^{l}-q^{-l}\big)},\qquad a\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}).

(7.23)

It is easy to check that the maps
D_{a}\colon U_{\epsilon}\rightarrow U_{\epsilon}
are well-defined (they do not depend on the choices of
\tilde{a}
and
\tilde{u}
), and that they are derivations of
U_{\epsilon}
preserving
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
and
\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
. Hence they define algebraic vector fields on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
. Since
\Omega
is central in
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
,
D_{a}
is the zero map when
a\in\mathbb{C}[\Omega]
.

Let us introduce the elements
e,f
defined in
[
27
]
by

e=-xz=\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l}E^{l},\qquad f=-yz=-\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l}F^{l}K^{l}.

(7.24)

Direct computations from the definition and the fact that
D_{e}\Omega=0
imply the formulas:

\displaystyle D_{z}(K)=0,\qquad D_{z}(E)=-\frac{1}{l}zE,\qquad D_{z}(F)=\frac{1}{l}zF,

(7.25)

\displaystyle D_{e}(K)=\frac{1}{l}eK,\qquad D_{e}(E)=0,D_{e}(F)=-\frac{1}{l}\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l-1}[K;1]E^{l-1},

(7.26)

\displaystyle D_{y}(K)=-\frac{1}{l}yK,\qquad D_{y}(F)=0,D_{y}(E)=\frac{1}{l}\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l-1}[K;-1]F^{l-1},

(7.27)

where
[K;r]=\frac{K\epsilon^{r}-K^{-1}\epsilon^{-r}}{\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}}
,
r\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

A Poisson bracket
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}
is defined on
\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
by

\{a,b\}_{\rm QCA}=D_{a}(b).

With this Poisson bracket
\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
is a Poisson algebra,
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
being a Poisson ideal.
The Poisson structure on
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
is completely defined by the formulas

\{y,x\}_{\rm QCA}=-1+xy+z^{-2},\qquad\{z,x\}_{\rm QCA}=-zx,\qquad\{z,y\}_{\rm QCA}=yz

(7.28)

which can be straithforwardy checked using the definition. From them it is easy to show that the Poisson center of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
is the commutative algebra generated by
-xyz+z+z^{-1}.

Let us introduce the family of automorphisms of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
defined as follows. Let
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\tau_{r}
,
T_{r}
the automorphisms of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\tau_{r}(K)=K,\qquad\tau_{r}(E)=K^{r}E,\qquad\tau_{r}(F)=FK^{-r},\\
&T_{r}(K)=K^{-1},\qquad T_{r}(E)=-FK^{-r},\qquad T_{r}(F)=-K^{r}E.\end{split}

Note that
T_{r}=\tau_{r}\circ T_{0}
and
T_{1}
is the braid group automorphism. We have

T_{1}(x)=y,\qquad T_{1}(y)=z^{2}x.

These automorphisms of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
define automorphisms of the specialisation
U_{\epsilon}
and we will keep the same notation for them. If
T
is any automorphism of the type
T_{r}
or
\tau_{r}
, it is easy to show from the definition of
D_{a}
for
a\in{\mathcal{Z}}(U_{\epsilon})
that

D_{T(a)}\circ T=T\circ D_{a}.

Denote by
G^{0}
the
big cell
of
G={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
. It consists of the matrices with non vanishing lower right entry, and satisfies
G^{0}=B_{+}B_{-}
, where
B_{+}
and
B_{-}
are the subgroups of
G
of upper and lower triangular matrices. We have an unramified
2
-fold covering

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\sigma:&G^{*}&\longrightarrow&G^{0},\\
&(b_{+},b_{-})&\longmapsto&b_{+}b_{-}^{-1}.\end{array}

It induces a diffeomorphism

\bar{\sigma}\colon\ \bar{G}^{*}\rightarrow G^{0}.

Setting
z:=z^{\prime}{}^{2}
and

\mathcal{M}=\sigma\left(\begin{pmatrix}z^{\prime}&z^{\prime}y\\
0&z^{\prime}{}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\!,\begin{pmatrix}z^{\prime}{}^{-1}&0\\
z^{\prime}x&z^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\right)

(7.29)

we have

\mathcal{M}=\begin{pmatrix}z-zxy&y\\
-x&z^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\!.

Consider the Poisson bracket
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}
on
\mathcal{O}(G)
defined by

\big\{\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!\big\}_{\rm FR}=\mathfrak{r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}+\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\mathfrak{r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}},

where
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
is
\mathfrak{r}
post-composed with the flip map
a\otimes b\mapsto b\otimes a
. The bracket
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}
has been introduced in
[
77
]
and generalized in the work of Fock–Rosly
[
43
]
(which explains our notation).
Note that
\mathcal{O}\big(G^{0}\big)
is the localization of the algebra
\mathcal{O}(G)
with respect to the matrix coefficient
l_{22}
in (
7.21
). As a consequence, the Poisson bracket
\{,\}_{\rm FR}
being quadratic, it can be extended to
\mathcal{O}(G^{0})
.

The next result sums up the relationships between the brackets
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}
,
\{\,,\,\}_{G^{*}}
, and
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}
:

Theorem 7.7
.

1.

The map
\bar{\psi}\colon(\bar{G}^{*},\{\,,\,\}_{\bar{G}^{*}})\rightarrow(\operatorname{Spec}((\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})),\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA})
is an isomorphism of complex Poisson–Lie groups
[
28
,
30
]
.

2.

The map
\bar{\sigma}\colon(\bar{G}^{*},\{\,,\,\}_{\bar{G}^{*}})\rightarrow(G^{0},\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR})
is a diffeomorphism of complex Poisson manifolds
[
77
]
.

The first claim is proved in
[
28
,
30
]
for the simply-connected quantum groups
\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{g})
, where
\mathfrak{g}
is a complex finite dimensional simple Lie algebra (see Remark
7.9
below). We state it rather in the case of
U_{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, the adjoint quantum group for
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
, where it follows from straighforward computations using the formulas (
7.22
) and (
7.28
). The second statement follows as well from straighforward computations. Namely, by using the identities (
7.22
) and
\sigma^{*}(L)=L_{+}L_{-}^{-1}
, and the fact that
\mathfrak{r}+\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
is
{\rm ad}
-invariant, one can identify the formulas of
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}
and

\sigma_{*}(\{\,,\,\}_{G^{*}})(L\otimes L)=\big\{\sigma^{*}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\!\big),\sigma^{*}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!\big)\big\}_{G^{*}}.

We leave the verifications to the reader.

The next statement summarizes the results of De Concini–Kac–Procesi on the quantum coadjoint action. Recall the elements
e=-xz,f=-yz
of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
(see (
7.24
)). Let us identify
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
with
G^{0}
using the map
\bar{\sigma}\circ\bar{\psi}^{-1}
of Theorem
7.7
, and hence the derivations
D_{e}
,
D_{f}
,
D_{z}
of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
with algebraic vector fields on
G^{0}
(and hence on
G
). Denote by
\underline{H}
,
\underline{X}
,
\underline{Y}
the left-invariant vector fields on
G
associated to the generators
H
,
X
,
Y
of
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
, where

H=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\!,\qquad X=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\!,\qquad Y=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\!.

Denote by
\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
the vector space of power series in the generators
x
,
y
, and
z^{\pm 1}
of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
whose sums converge when evaluated on any
\chi\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
\chi=(x_{\chi},y_{\chi},z_{\chi})
(thus defining holomorphic functions). Set

\hat{U}_{\epsilon}=U_{\epsilon}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})}\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}),\qquad\hat{\mathcal{Z}}(U_{\epsilon})=\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})}\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}).

Theorem 7.8

(
[
28
,
30
]
)
.

(a)

We have
D_{z}=z\underline{H}/2
,
D_{e}=-z\underline{Y}
,
D_{f}=z\underline{X}
.

(b)

For every
t\in\mathbb{C}
the power series
\exp(tD_{e})
,
\exp(tD_{f})
converge to automorphisms of
\hat{U}_{\epsilon}
preserving
\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
and
\hat{\mathcal{Z}}(U_{\epsilon})
, and fixing
\mathbb{C}[\Omega]
.

Denote by
\mathcal{G}_{\rm DCK}
the subgroup of
{\rm Aut}(\hat{U}_{\epsilon})
generated by the
1
-parameter groups
(\exp(tD_{e}))_{t\in\mathbb{C}}
and
(\exp(tD_{f}))_{t\in\mathbb{C}}
. It acts dually by holomorphic transformations on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
and
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
by defining
g.\chi
on
u\in\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
, for every
g\in{\mathcal{G}}
and
\chi\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
, by
u(g.\chi)=(g^{-1}.u)(\chi)
. Then:

(c)

The diffeomorphism
\bar{\sigma}\circ\bar{\psi}^{-1}\colon\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))\rightarrow G^{0}
maps the action of
{\mathcal{G}}_{\rm DCK}
on the tangent spaces of
(0,0,\pm 1)\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
to the coadjoint action of
G
on
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2,\mathbb{C})^{*}
, the tangent spaces of
{\pm I}\in G^{0}
.

(d)

For any conjugacy class
\Gamma
in
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
,
\big(\bar{\psi}\circ\bar{\sigma}^{-1}\big)(\Gamma\cap G^{0})
is a
(
non empty
)

{\mathcal{G}}_{\rm DCK}
-orbit in
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
, and
\tau^{-1}
of this orbit has
l
connected components, all of whose are
{\mathcal{G}}_{\rm DCK}
-orbits in
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
.

(e)

An element
a\in{\mathcal{Z}}(U_{\epsilon})
is
\mathcal{G}_{\rm DCK}
-invariant if and only if
a\in\mathbb{C}[\Omega]
. Dually, the sets of fixed points of the action of
\mathcal{G}_{\rm DCK}
on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
and
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
are respectively
