(0,0,\pm 1):=\big(\bar{\psi}\circ\bar{\sigma}^{-1}\big)(\{{\rm\pm I}\})
and

\mathcal{D}:=\tau^{-1}((0,0,\pm 1))=\{(0,0,\pm 1,\pm c_{j}),\,j=1,\dots,(l-1)/2\}.

(f)

The
{\mathcal{G}}_{\rm DCK}
-orbits on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
and
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
are the symplectic leaves of
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}
.

Remark 7.9
.

In the statements of Theorems
7.7
and
7.8
we use our conventions, which differ from those in
[
28
,
30
]
in the following ways:

(i)

Theorem
7.7
(1) for the simply connected quantum group
\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
instead of
U_{\epsilon}\big({\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\big)
, as in
[
28
,
30
]
, states an isomorphism of
(G^{*},\{\,,\,\}_{G^{*}})
with
\big(\!\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\tilde{U}_{\epsilon})),\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}\big)
; as we take opposite comultiplications on
\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
, we get opposite multiplications of
G^{*}

(
whence
\bar{G}^{*}
for
U_{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\big)
.

(ii)

Our derivations
D_{a}
differ by a sign, which we introduce in order to get the equality of
\bar{\sigma}_{*}\{\,,\,\}_{\bar{G}^{*}}
with
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}
in Theorem
7.7
(2)
.

(iii)

In
[
27
,
28
,
30
]
, the matrix
{\mathcal{M}}
in (
7.29
) is different because they use the opposite coproduct on
\tilde{U}_{\epsilon}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))
; their braid group automorphism
T
is in our notation
T_{-1}
, which satisfies
T_{-1}(e)=f
.

It is easily checked that the identities (
7.28
) imply

D_{z}\mathcal{M}=\bigg[\frac{z}{2}\underline{H},\mathcal{M}\bigg],\qquad D_{x}\mathcal{M}=\big[{-}z^{-1}\underline{X},\mathcal{M}\big],\qquad D_{y}\mathcal{M}=\big[z^{-1}\underline{Y},\mathcal{M}\big],

(7.30)

where
\mathcal{M}
is defined by (
7.29
). Hence we get

D_{z}=\frac{z}{2}\underline{H},\qquad D_{x}=-z^{-1}\underline{X},\qquad D_{y}=z^{-1}\underline{Y}.

This is the content of Theorem
7.8
(a)
expressed in terms of the derivations
D_{z}
,
D_{x}
,
D_{y}
. In Theorem
7.8
(b)
,
U_{\epsilon}
is enlarged to
\hat{U}_{\epsilon}
in order to define the automorphisms
\exp(tD_{e})
,
\exp(tD_{f})
because the derivations
D_{e}
,
D_{f}
are not nilpotent.

For reasons that will be explained in Remark
7.15
, we will need a result analogous to Theorem
7.8
but based on different derivations. This leads to substantial differences in the details, so we give all proofs.

Consider the following derivations on
U_{\epsilon}
:

\mathcal{E}=zD_{x},\qquad\mathcal{F}=-zD_{y},\qquad\mathcal{H}=-2z^{-1}D_{z}.

(7.31)

Lemma 7.10
.

The derivations
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{H}
satisfy the following commutation relations, generating therefore the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
:

[\mathcal{H},\mathcal{E}]=2\mathcal{E},\qquad[\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F}]=-2\mathcal{F},\qquad[\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}]=\mathcal{H}.

(7.32)

Proof.

Straightforward computation using (
7.28
).
∎

For any
{\mathbb{C}}
-algebra
A
and endomorphism
{\mathcal{V}}\in{\operatorname{End}}(A)
we can define

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\exp(t{\mathcal{V}}):&A&\longrightarrow&A[[t]],\\
&a&\longmapsto&\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}{\mathcal{V}}^{n}(a).\end{array}

If
{\mathcal{V}}
is a derivation,
\exp(t{\mathcal{V}})
is a morphism of algebras. It admits a unique extension (by continuity for the
t
-adic topology)

\exp(t{\mathcal{V}})\colon A[[t]]\rightarrow A[[t]]
which is an automorphism with inverse
\exp(-t{\mathcal{V}}).

We now give expressions of
\exp(t{\mathcal{F}})
,
\exp(t{\mathcal{E}})\colon U_{\epsilon}\rightarrow U_{\epsilon}[[t]]
. For every
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}
consider the following elements of
{\mathbb{C}}[[t]]
:

\displaystyle(1+t)^{\alpha}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{n!}t^{n}\alpha(\alpha-1)\cdots(\alpha-n+1),

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}(t)=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}t^{n-1}\alpha(\alpha-1)\cdots(\alpha-n+1)=\frac{(1-t)^{\alpha}-1}{t}.

For any
a\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
, we can similarly define elements
(1+ta)^{\alpha}
,
\psi_{\alpha}(ta)\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})[[t]]
.

Lemma 7.11
.

The action of
\exp(t{\mathcal{F}})
is given by:

\displaystyle\exp(t{\mathcal{F}})(K)=(1-tyz)^{-1/l}K,\qquad\exp(t{\mathcal{F}})\big(K^{-1}\big)=(1-tyz)^{1/l}K^{-1},

\displaystyle\exp(t{\mathcal{F}})(F)=F,

\displaystyle\exp(t{\mathcal{F}})(E)=E-\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l-2}\big(K\epsilon^{-1}tz\psi_{-1/l}(tyz)+K^{-1}\epsilon tz\psi_{1/l}(tyz)\big)F^{l-1}.

Proof.

From (
7.27
) we have

{\mathcal{F}}(K)=\frac{zy}{l}K,\qquad{\mathcal{F}}(F)=0,{\qquad\mathcal{F}}(E)=-\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l-1}\frac{z}{l}[K,-1]F^{l-1}.

(7.33)

Iterating this, a straightforward computation proves the lemma.∎

Lemma 7.12
.

The action of
\exp(t{\mathcal{E}})
is given by

\displaystyle\exp(t{\mathcal{E}})(K)=(1-txz)^{-1/l}K,\qquad\exp(t{\mathcal{E}})\big(K^{-1}\big)=(1-txz)^{1/l}K^{-1},

\displaystyle\exp(t{\mathcal{E}})\big(K^{-1}E\big)=K^{-1}E,

\displaystyle\exp(t{\mathcal{E}})(FK)=FK+\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l-2}\big(K\epsilon tz\psi_{-1/l}(txz)+K^{-1}\epsilon^{-1}tz\psi_{1/l}(txz)\big)\big(K^{-1}E\big)^{l-1}.

Proof.

This is a little trickier than the previous proof, because it is
D_{y}
which appears in (
7.27
) and
{\mathcal{E}}
involves
D_{x}.

We therefore use the automorphism
T_{-1}
which is such that
T_{-1}(y)=-x
. As a result, by applying it to (
7.33
), we obtain

D_{x}\big(K^{-1}\big)=-\frac{x}{l}K^{-1},\qquad D_{x}\big(K^{-1}E\big)=0,\qquad D_{x}(FK)=\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l-1}\frac{1}{l}[K,1]\big(K^{-1}E\big)^{l-1}

which imply

{\mathcal{E}}(K)=\frac{zx}{l}K,\qquad{\mathcal{E}}\big(K^{-1}E\big)=0,\qquad{\mathcal{E}}(FK)=\big(\epsilon-\epsilon^{-1}\big)^{l-1}\frac{z}{l}[K,1]\big(K^{-1}E\big)^{l-1}.

Iterating this, a straightforward computation proves the lemma.
∎

Because
{\mathcal{E}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}
leave invariant
\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
and
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
they define maps
\exp(t{\mathcal{E}})
,
\exp(t{\mathcal{F}})
:
{\mathcal{Z}}(U_{\epsilon})\rightarrow{\mathcal{Z}}(U_{\epsilon})[[t]]
and
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})\rightarrow{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})[[t]]
.

Proposition 7.13
.

We have

\displaystyle\exp(t{\mathcal{E}})(x)=x,\qquad\exp(t{\mathcal{F}})(y)=y,\qquad\exp(t{\mathcal{E}})(\Omega)=\exp(t{\mathcal{F}})(\Omega)=\Omega,

\displaystyle\exp(t{\mathcal{E}})(z)=(1-tzx)^{-1}z,\qquad\exp(t{\mathcal{E}})(y)=y+t\big({-}xyz+z-z^{-1}\big)+t^{2}x,

\displaystyle\exp(t{\mathcal{F}})(z)=(1-tzy)^{-1}z,\qquad\exp(t{\mathcal{F}})(x)=x+t\big({-}xyz+z-z^{-1}\big)+t^{2}y.

Proof.

Rather than using the explicit action of
\exp(t{\mathcal{E}})
and
\exp(t{\mathcal{F}})
on
U_{\epsilon}
, we remark that from (
7.30
) we have

\exp(t{\mathcal{E}})({\mathcal{M}})=\exp(-tX){\mathcal{M}}\exp(tX),\qquad\exp(t{\mathcal{F}})({\mathcal{M}})=\exp(-tY){\mathcal{M}}\exp(tY).

(7.34)

The announced expressions follow by writing the matrix elements of these equations.∎

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be the free product of
({\mathbb{C}},+)
with itself; it can be equivalently seen as the group generated by elements
\phi_{s}
,
\psi_{s}
, where
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
, with the relations
\phi_{s}\phi_{s^{\prime}}=\phi_{s+s^{\prime}}
,
\psi_{s}\psi_{s^{\prime}}=\psi_{s+s^{\prime}}
for every
s,s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

We can define a partial action of
{\mathcal{G}}
on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
and on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
, in the sense of
[
37
]
. Let
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
, and denote by
D(\phi_{s})
the set of
\chi\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
such that for all
u\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
the series
(\exp(s{\mathcal{E}})(u))(\chi)
is normally convergent in a small neighborhood of
s
. Equivalently
D(\phi_{s})=\{\chi\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})),\,|sx_{\chi}z_{\chi}|<1\}
. We define an action of
\phi_{s}
on
D(\phi_{s})
by

\displaystyle u(\phi_{s}.\chi)=(\exp(-s{\mathcal{E}})(u))(\chi).

(7.35)

Similarly, put
D(\psi_{s})=\{\chi\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})),|sy_{\chi}z_{\chi}|<1\}
and define an action of
\psi_{s}
on
D(\psi_{s})
by

\displaystyle u(\psi_{s}.\chi)=(\exp(-s{\mathcal{F}})(u))(\chi).

(7.36)

The domains
D(\phi_{s})
(resp.
D(\psi_{s})
) cover
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
as
s
varies in
{\mathbb{C}}
. By results of Exel (
[
37
]
and
[
36
, Examples 1 and 4]
), the set formed by the local actions (
7.35
) (resp. (
7.36
)) on the domains
D(\phi_{s})
(resp.
D(\psi_{s})
) defines a partial action of the one-parameter group
(\phi_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{C}}
(resp.
(\psi_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{C}}
) on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
, and combining the two we get a partial action of their free product
{\mathcal{G}}
on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
. Similarly we get a partial action of
{\mathcal{G}}
on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
by replacing
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
by
\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
.

We call them the
partial quantum coadjoint actions
of
\mathcal{G}
. Orbits (called
partial orbits
in
[
37
]
) are defined as for group actions: the
{\mathcal{G}}
-orbit of a point
\chi\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
is the set of points
g.\chi
, for every possible
g\in\mathcal{G}
such that
g.\chi
is defined.

The next result states the analogs of Theorem
7.8
(c)
–
(f)
obtained by replacing
\mathcal{G}_{\rm DCK}
by
\mathcal{G}
. In particular, it describes the
\mathcal{G}
-orbits.

Theorem 7.14
.

(a)

The diffeomorphism
\bar{\sigma}\circ\bar{\psi}^{-1}
maps the action of
{\mathcal{G}}
on the tangent spaces of
(0,0,\pm 1)\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
to the coadjoint action of
G
on
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2,\mathbb{C})^{*}=T_{\pm I}(G^{0})
.

(b)

For any conjugacy class
\Gamma
in
G
,
\big(\bar{\psi}\circ\bar{\sigma}^{-1}\big)\big(\Gamma\cap G^{0}\big)
is a
(
non empty
)

{\mathcal{G}}
-orbit in
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
, and
\tau^{-1}
of this orbit has
l
connected components, all of whose are
{\mathcal{G}}
-orbits in
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
.

(c)

An element
a\in{\mathcal{Z}}(U_{\epsilon})
is
\mathcal{G}
-invariant if and only if
a\in\mathbb{C}[\Omega]
. Dually, the sets of fixed points of the partial
{\mathcal{G}}
-action on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
and
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
are respectively
(0,0,\pm 1):=\big(\bar{\psi}\circ\bar{\sigma}^{-1}\big)(\{{\rm\pm I}\})
and

\mathcal{D}:=\tau^{-1}((0,0,\pm 1))=\{(0,0,\pm 1,\pm c_{j}),\,j=1,\dots,(l-1)/2\}.

(d)

The
{\mathcal{G}}
-orbits on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
and
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
are the symplectic leaves of
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}
.

Proof.

(a)
Using
\bar{\sigma}^{-1*}\circ\bar{\psi}^{*}
to identify
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
with
\mathcal{O}\big(G^{0}\big)
, this is a direct consequence of (
7.34
) and the fact that
{\mathcal{M}}=\pm I
at the fixed point.

(b)
That
\Gamma\cap G^{0}
is non-empty and connected is classical. Then so is
\big(\bar{\psi}\circ\bar{\sigma}^{-1}\big)\big(\Gamma\cap G^{0}\big)
. Take a point
\chi\in\big(\bar{\psi}\circ\bar{\sigma}^{-1}\big)\big(\Gamma\cap G^{0}\big)
. Evaluating (
7.34
) at
\chi
shows that
\mathcal{G}\cdot\chi
is contained in
\big(\bar{\psi}\circ\bar{\sigma}^{-1}\big)\big(\Gamma\cap G^{0}\big)
. We claim that
\mathcal{G}\cdot\chi
is an open and closed subset, so by connectedness of
\big(\bar{\psi}\circ\bar{\sigma}^{-1}\big)\big(\Gamma\cap G^{0}\big)
it coincides with it. Indeed, it is an open subset because any point of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
(whence of
\mathcal{G}\cdot\chi
) belongs to the domains
D(\phi_{s})
and
D(\psi_{s})
for
s
small enough. Since the one-parameter groups
(\phi_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{C}}
and
(\psi_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{C}}
are obtained by integrating the derivations
{\mathcal{E}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}
, it follows from Lemma
7.10
that
\mathcal{G}\cdot\chi
contains a neighborhood in
\big(\bar{\psi}\circ\bar{\sigma}^{-1}\big)\big(\Gamma\cap G^{0}\big)
of each of its points. By the same reason any limit point of
\mathcal{G}\cdot\chi
in
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
has a neighborhood where the partial
{\mathcal{G}}
-action is defined and which intersects
\mathcal{G}\cdot\chi
. Therefore it belongs to
\mathcal{G}\cdot\chi
, which shows that
\mathcal{G}\cdot\chi
is also closed in
\big(\bar{\psi}\circ\bar{\sigma}^{-1}\big)\big(\Gamma\cap G^{0}\big)
. Because the covering map
\tau
is unramified of degree
l
, the result for
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
follows at once.

(c)
Any
\chi\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
belongs to the domains
D(\phi_{s})
,
D(\psi_{s})
for
s
small enough. Solving the equations
\phi_{s}(\chi)=\chi
and
\psi_{s}(\chi)=\chi
by using the formulas in Proposition
7.13
imposes
\chi=(0,0,\pm 1)
. This gives the fixed point set of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
; the result for
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
follows immediately. As for the first claim, note that for any conjugacy class
\Gamma
of maximal dimension the set
\Gamma\cap G^{0}
contains a diagonal matrix, and that the union of such sets forms a Zariski open and dense subset of
G
. Hence (b) above implies that a central invariant element is completely determined by its value at points
(0,0,z_{\chi},\Omega_{\chi})
. Therefore it is non zero if and only if it belongs to
\mathbb{C}[\Omega]
.

(d)
The groups
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\rm DCK}
are obtained by integrating the derivations
{\mathcal{E}}
,
{\mathcal{F}}
, and
D_{e}
,
D_{f}
respectively, and the Lie algebras generated by these two pairs of derivations have the same span at every point of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))
or
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
. Then the conclusion follows from Theorem
7.8
(f)
.∎

Remark 7.15
.

A difficulty with the group
\mathcal{G}
is that its elements act only on subsets of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
: in the formulas (
7.34
), the parameter
t\in\mathbb{C}
must be such that the lower right entries of the computed matrices are non zero. However it has various merits as compared to the group
\mathcal{G}_{\rm DCK}
of Theorem
7.8
:

(i)

\mathcal{G}
is finite dimensional, associated to the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
(by Lemma
7.10
), whereas
\mathcal{G}_{\rm DCK}
is infinite-dimensional.

(ii)

The partial action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
is by birational transformations (by Proposition
7.13
), whereas
\mathcal{G}_{\rm DCK}
is a subgroup of
{\rm Aut}(\hat{U}_{\epsilon})
, acting on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
by holomorphic (entire) transformations.

(iii)

\mathcal{G}
can be generalized straightforwardly to
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
, whereas
\mathcal{G}_{\rm DCK}
does not. This is our main motivation for developing this construction. We do it in the next section.

Remark 7.16
.

We think useful to have in mind the following description of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
(see
[
27
]
). Denote by
G/\!/G
the affine variety with coordinate ring
\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
, the ring of regular functions on
G
invariant under the coadjoint action of
G
. We have an isomorphism

G/\!/G\cong\mathbb{C}^{*}/\big(t\sim t^{-1}\big).

In fact, denote by
T
the torus of
G
formed by the diagonal matrices, and by
\mathcal{O}(T)
its coordinate ring. Then
T\cong\mathbb{C}^{*}
and
\mathcal{O}(T)\cong\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
, where
t
is the coordinate function of the upper left entry of elements of
T
. The Weyl group
W
of
G
acts on
T
by inversion, and

\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\cong\mathcal{O}(T)^{W}=\mathbb{C}\big[t+t^{-1}\big].

Consider the maps

p\colon\ G\longrightarrow G/\!/G,\qquad p_{k}\colon\ G/\!/G\longrightarrow G/\!/G,

where
p\colon G\rightarrow G/\!/G
is the quotient map, and
p_{k}
is induced by the
k
-th power map
g\mapsto g^{k}
,
g\in G
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Note here that on coordinate functions we have
p_{k}(t+t^{-1})=t^{k}+t^{-k}
, so
p_{k}
is just realized by the
k
-th Chebyshev polynomial
T_{k}
. Consider the fibered product of
p
and
p_{l}
, that is, the affine variety

G\times_{G/\!/G}G/\!/G=\{(g,[t])\in G\times G/\!/G\ |\ p(g)=p_{l}([t])\}.

Set

\tilde{G}=G\times_{G/\!/G}G/\!/G,\qquad\tilde{G}^{0}=G^{0}\times_{G/\!/G}G/\!/G.

Then
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
is isomorphic to
\tilde{G}^{0}
. In fact, by Theorem
7.7
and the defining relation (
7.1
) we know that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}(G^{0})
, and that
\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})\cap\mathbb{C}[\Omega]}\mathbb{C}[\Omega]
. By the quantum Harish-Chandra homomorphism, see
[
27
]
for details,
\mathbb{C}[\Omega]\cong\mathbb{C}\big[K+K^{-1}\big]
and
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})\cap\mathbb{C}[\Omega]\cong\mathbb{C}\big[K^{l}+K^{-l}\big]
. Let us identify
\mathbb{C}\big[K,K^{-1}\big]
with
\mathcal{O}(T)
. Then
\mathbb{C}[\Omega]\cong\mathcal{O}(T)^{W}
and
\mathbb{C}[K^{l}+K^{-l}]\cong\mathcal{O}(T/\mu_{l})^{W}
, where
\mu_{l}
is the subgroup of
T
corresponding to the
l
th-roots of unity under the isomorphism
T\cong\mathbb{C}^{*}
. Hence

\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})\cong\mathcal{O}\big(G^{0}\big)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(T/\mu_{l})^{W}}\mathcal{O}(T)^{W}.

The isomorphism of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}))
with
G^{0}\times_{G/\!/G}G/\!/G
follows by duality.

7.2.2
Extension to
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}}

The results of the previous section extend naturally to
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
. First we consider the generalization of Theorem
7.7
.

The bracket
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}
on
G
has been extended to
G^{n}
by Fock–Rosly
[
43
]
. From its very definition, it is readily checked to be defined by

\displaystyle\big\{\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)}\big\}_{\rm FR}=\mathfrak{r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)}\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)}-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)}\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)}\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}+\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)}\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)}-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)}\mathfrak{r}\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)},

(7.37)

\displaystyle\big\{\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!{}^{(j)}\big\}_{\rm FR}=\mathfrak{r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)}\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!{}^{(j)}+\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)}\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!{}^{(j)}\mathfrak{r}-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!{}^{(j)}\mathfrak{r}\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)}-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)}\mathfrak{r}\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!{}^{(j)},

(7.38)

where
L^{(i)}=\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(i)}
(see (
7.12
)),
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, and
i<j
.

The formula (
7.23
) extends naturally to define derivations
D_{a}\colon{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
preserving
\mathcal{Z}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
, for every
a\in\mathcal{Z}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
. Hence we can define a Poisson bracket
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}
on
\mathcal{Z}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
(keeping voluntarily the same notation as on
\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
) by

\{a,b\}_{\rm QCA}=D_{a}(b)

for every
a,b\in\mathcal{Z}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}).
As
\Phi_{n}
is an isomorphism of algebras, both derivations and Poisson bracket can be defined and computed by means of their pushforwards on
U_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
. Indeed, for any
a\in\mathcal{Z}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
,
\Phi_{n}(a)\in\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n})
, so the derivation
D_{\Phi_{n}(a)}\colon U_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow U_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
is defined and we can put
D_{a}=\Phi_{n}^{-1}D_{\Phi_{n}(a)}\Phi_{n}
. For every
a\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
, by the inclusion
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\subset{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
it yields a derivation
D_{a}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
preserving
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
.

Denote by
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
(resp.
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
) the subalgebras of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})

(
resp.
\mathcal{Z}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
)
generated by
T_{l}\big(\omega^{(i)}\big)
,
b^{(i)l}
,
c^{(i)l}
,
d^{(i)l}

(
resp.
T_{l}\big(\omega^{(i)}\big)
,
b^{(i)l}
,
c^{(i)l}
,
d^{(i)l}
and
\delta^{(i)\pm l}
)
for
i=1,\dots,n
.
Recall the Frobenius morphism
{\rm Fr}\colon\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{1}\big)^{\otimes n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
(see (
7.12
)), and the identification
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{1}=\mathcal{O}(G)
.

Theorem 7.17
.

The map

\Phi:=\big(\bar{\sigma}^{-1*}\circ\bar{\psi}^{*}{}\big)^{\otimes n}\circ\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}\colon\ \big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}),\{\ ,\}_{\rm QCA}\big)\rightarrow\big(\mathcal{O}(G^{0})^{\otimes n},\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}\big)

is an isomorphism of Poisson algebras, and
\Phi^{-1}
restricted to
\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes n}\subset\mathcal{O}(G^{0})^{\otimes n}
coincides with the Frobenius homomorphism. Hence
{\rm Fr}\colon\big(\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes n},\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}\big)\rightarrow\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}),\{\ ,\}_{\rm QCA}\big)
is an isomorphism of Poisson algebras.

Proof.

The map
\bar{\sigma}^{-1*}\circ\bar{\psi}^{*}{}\colon\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}),\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}\big)\rightarrow\big(\mathcal{O}(G^{0}),\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}\big)
is an isomorphism of Poisson algebras by the results recalled in the previous section. Since
\Phi_{1}\colon{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\rightarrow U_{A}^{\prime}
is an algebra isomorphism by Lemma
5.7
, when specializing at
q=\epsilon
it yields a Poisson isomorphism between
\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}),\{\ ,\}_{\rm QCA}\big)
and
\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}),\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}\big)
. This proves the case
n=1
.

When
n\geq 2
, the map is well-defined because
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})={}_{\rm loc}\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
(the localization by the powers
\delta^{(i)lk}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
),
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})=\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}
by Proposition
7.2
, and
\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}
extends from
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
to
{}_{\rm loc}\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
for obvious reasons (the algebra being commutative). On another hand, since
\Phi_{n}\colon{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow U_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}
is an equivariant algebra isomorphism (see (
6.26
)) and
\Phi_{n}\big(\omega^{(i)}\big)=\Omega^{(i)}
, it yields a Poisson isomorphism between
\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}),\{\ ,\}_{\rm QCA}\big)
and
(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}),\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA})^{\otimes n}
, i.e., the algebra
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}
with the product Poisson structure. Hence it is enough to prove that

\Phi\circ\Phi_{n}^{-1}\colon\ \big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}\big),\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA})^{\otimes n}\rightarrow\big(\mathcal{O}\big(G^{0}\big)^{\otimes n},\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}\big)

is an isomorphism of Poisson algebras. This can be checked on generators, which is most easily done by using the inverse map. Put
\Pi={\rm id}_{V_{2}}\otimes(\Phi_{n}\circ(\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n})^{-1})
. By Corollary
7.4
we have

\Pi\big(\mathcal{M}^{(i)}\big)=\mathcal{R}^{(i)}\mathcal{M}^{(i)}\mathcal{R}^{(i)-1}.

(7.39)

Moreover, (
7.14
) and (
7.29
) show that
\bar{\sigma}^{-1*}\circ\bar{\psi}^{*}
identifies the matrix coefficients of
\Phi_{1}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}))
in the fundamental representation
V_{2}
of
U_{\epsilon}
, and the matrix coefficients of the fundamental representation of
G
on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Therefore it is enough to check that

\big\{\Pi^{*}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)}\big),\Pi^{*}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!{}^{(j)}\big)\big\}_{\rm QCA}^{\otimes n}=\big\{\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{L}}\!{}^{(i)},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{L}}\!{}^{(j)}\big\}_{\rm FR}

(7.40)

for every
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, where
\Pi^{*}\big(L^{(i)}\big)
is the pull-back of
L^{(i)}
via
\Pi
, i.e., its expression as a function of the matrices
\mathcal{M}^{(i)}
. Now we have

\mathcal{M}^{(i)}=\mathcal{M}^{(i)}_{+}\mathcal{M}^{(i)-1}_{-},

(7.41)

where

\mathcal{M}^{(i)}_{+}=\begin{pmatrix}z^{\prime}{}^{(i)}&z^{\prime}{}^{(i)}y{}^{(i)}\\
0&z^{\prime}{}^{(i)-1}\end{pmatrix}\!,\qquad\mathcal{M}^{(i)}_{-}=\begin{pmatrix}z^{\prime}{}^{(i)-1}&0\\
z^{\prime}{}^{(i)}x{}^{(i)}&z^{\prime}{}^{(i)}\end{pmatrix}\!.

Note also that (
7.15
) and (
7.16
) imply

\mathcal{R}^{(i)}=\mathcal{M}^{(n)}_{+}\cdots\mathcal{M}^{(i+1)}_{+}.

(7.42)

Using the map
\sigma^{-1*}\circ\psi^{*}
to identify
\mathcal{M}^{(i)}_{+}
,
\mathcal{M}^{(i)}_{-}
with matrices
L_{+}
,
L_{-}
of coordinate functions in
G^{*}
, we see that they satisfy the bracket identities (
7.22
). Since
G^{*}
is a Poisson–Lie group, products of matrices
L_{+}
have the same bracket as
L_{+}
. In particular this applies to
\mathcal{R}^{(i)}
, and we can write

\Pi^{*}(L^{(i)})=\mathcal{R}^{(i)}\!\!\mathcal{M}^{(i)}_{+}\!\!\mathcal{M}^{(i)-1}_{-}\mathcal{R}^{(i)-1},

where all matrices in the product have known brackets. Then (
7.40
) can be straightforwardly compared with (
7.37
) and (
7.38
), using the Leibniz rule for simplifications, and the fact that
r+r^{\prime}
is ad-invariant. We leave the verifications to the reader.

The equality of
\Phi^{-1}
with Fr on
\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes n}
follows immediatly from the fact that
\bar{\sigma}^{-1*}\circ\bar{\psi}^{*}
identifies matrix coefficients, as discussed above. The image of
Fr
is
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
, by Proposition
7.2
. This achieves the proof.
∎

Next we turn to our generalization of Theorem
7.14
. First we define a partial action of the group
\mathcal{G}
on
\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{Z}}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}))
and
\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{Z}}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}))
by generalizing the method we used in the
n=1
case.

Recall the derivations
D_{a}\colon{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
, defined for every
a\in\mathcal{Z}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
. Denote by
\hat{x}^{(i)},\hat{y}^{(i)},\hat{z}^{(i)}\in\mathcal{Z}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
the inverse images by
\Phi_{n}
of the elements
x^{(i)}
,
y^{(i)}
,
z^{(i)}
, and let
{\mathcal{E}}^{(i)}
,
{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}
,
{\mathcal{H}}^{(i)}
be the derivations of
{}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
defined by

{\mathcal{E}}^{(i)}=\hat{z}^{(i)}D_{\hat{x}^{(i)}},\qquad{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}=-\hat{z}^{(i)}D_{\hat{y}^{(i)}},\qquad{\mathcal{H}}^{(i)}=-2\hat{z}^{(i)-1}D_{\hat{z}^{(i)}}.

Note that because of the relations (
7.1
) and the definition of
\delta^{(i)}
we can obtain simple formulas for
\hat{z}^{(i)}
and
\hat{x}^{(i)},
namely:

\hat{z}^{(i)\pm 1}=\delta^{(i)\mp l},\qquad\hat{x}^{(i)}=-c^{(i)l}\prod_{k=i+1}^{n}\delta^{(k)-l}.

Set

{\mathcal{E}}^{\Delta}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{E}}^{(i)},\qquad{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)},\qquad{\mathcal{H}}^{\Delta}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{H}}^{(i)}.

Proposition 7.18
.

(1)

The derivations
{\mathcal{E}}^{\Delta}
,
{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{\Delta}
satisfy the following commutation relations, generating the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
:

\big[\mathcal{H}^{\Delta},\mathcal{E}^{\Delta}\big]=2\mathcal{E}^{\Delta},\qquad\big[\mathcal{H}^{\Delta},\mathcal{F}^{\Delta}\big]=-2\mathcal{F}^{\Delta},\qquad\big[\mathcal{E}^{\Delta},\mathcal{F}^{\Delta}\big]=\mathcal{H}^{\Delta}.

(2)

The power series
\exp\big(t{\mathcal{E}}^{\Delta}\big)
,
\exp\big(t{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}\big)
define morphisms of algebras from
{}_{\rm loc}{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
to
{}_{\rm loc}{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}[[t]]
, sending
{\mathcal{Z}}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
to
{\mathcal{Z}}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})[[t]]
and fixing the elements
\omega^{(i)}
. Moreover

\displaystyle\exp\big(t\Phi_{n}\big({\mathcal{E}}^{\Delta}\big)\big)\big({\mathcal{M}}^{(i)}\big)=\exp(-tX){\mathcal{M}}^{(i)}\exp(tX),

\displaystyle\exp\big(t\Phi_{n}\big({\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}\big)\big)\big({\mathcal{M}}^{(i)}\big)=\exp(-tY){\mathcal{M}}^{(i)}\exp(tY).

As a result
\exp\big(t{\mathcal{E}}^{\Delta}\big)
and
\exp\big(t{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}\big)
are sending
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
to
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})[[t]]
.

Proof.

(1) Straighforward from (
7.32
) and from the local Poisson-commutativity, i.e., the property that
\{a,b\}=0
when
a\in\big\{x^{(i)},y^{(i)},z^{(i)}\big\}
,
b\in\big\{x^{(j)},y^{(j)},z^{(j)}\big\}
,
i\not=j
.

(2) The first claim is straightforward, the second is a direct application of (
7.34
) and the local Poisson-commutativity.
∎

As in (
7.35
) let
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
, and denote by
D\big(\phi_{s}^{(i)}\big)
the set of points
\chi\in\operatorname{Spec}(\!{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}))
such that for all
u\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
the series
\big(\!\exp(s{\mathcal{E}}^{(i)})(u)\big)(\chi)
is normally convergent in a small neighborhood of
s
. Thus
D\big(\phi_{s}^{(i)}\big)=\big\{\chi\in\operatorname{Spec}\big({\mathcal{Z}}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})\big),\big|s\hat{x}^{(i)}_{\chi}\hat{z}^{(i)}_{\chi}\big|<1\big\}
. Define an action of the element
\phi_{s}^{(i)}
on
D\big(\phi_{s}^{(i)}\big)
by

u\big(\phi_{s}^{(i)}.\chi\big)=\big(\!\exp\big({-}s{\mathcal{E}}^{(i)}\big)(u)\big)(\chi).

(7.43)

Similarly, put
D\big(\psi_{s}^{(i)}\big)=\big\{\chi\in\operatorname{Spec}\big({\mathcal{Z}}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})\big),\big|s\hat{y}^{(i)}_{\chi}\hat{z}^{(i)}_{\chi}\big|<1\big\}
, and define an action of

\psi_{s}^{(i)}
on
D\big(\psi_{s}^{(i)}\big)
by

u\big(\psi_{s}^{(i)}.\chi\big)=\big(\!\exp\big({-}s{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}\big)(u)\big)(\chi).

(7.44)

Denote by
{\mathcal{G}}^{(i)}
the group generated by
\phi_{s}^{(i)},\psi_{s}^{(i)},s\in{\mathbb{C}}
. It is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{G}}
. Denote by
{\mathcal{G}}^{\rm tot}
the direct product of the groups
{\mathcal{G}}^{(i)}
, and by
{\mathcal{G}}^{\Delta}
the subgroup of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\rm tot}
generated by the diagonal elements
\phi_{s}^{\Delta}=\big(\phi_{s}^{(1)},\dots,\phi_{s}^{(n)}\big)

(
resp.
\psi_{s}^{\Delta}=\big(\psi_{s}^{(1)},\dots,\psi_{s}^{(n)}\big)
)
. These elements act on
D\big(\phi_{s}^{\Delta}\big)=\cap_{i=1}^{n}D\big(\phi_{s}^{(i)}\big),

(
resp.
D(\psi_{s}^{\Delta})=\cap_{i=1}^{n}D\big(\psi_{s}^{(i)}\big)\big)
, by acting dually with the series
\exp\big(s{\mathcal{E}}^{\Delta}\big)

(
resp.
\exp\big(s{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}\big)
)
.

As in the case
n=1
, the results of Exel
[
37
,
36
]
imply that (
7.43
) and (
7.44
) define partial action of the group
{\mathcal{G}}^{\rm tot}\cong{\mathcal{G}}^{n}
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{\Delta}\cong{\mathcal{G}}
on
\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{Z}}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})).
We call the first one the
total partial quantum coadjoint action
and the second one the
diagonal partial quantum coadjoint action
.

Next we need the following result of Fock–Rosly. Recall that we denote by
X_{G}(\Sigma)
the variety of
G
-characters of the sphere
\Sigma
with
n+1
punctures, and by
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm Gold}
the Goldman Poisson bracket on
X_{G}(\Sigma)
. Denote by
G^{n}/\!/G
the algebraic quotient of
G^{n}
by the adjoint action of
G
. It is the affine variety with coordinate ring
\mathcal{O}(G^{n})^{G}
, the ring of regular functions on
G^{n}
invariant under the coadjoint action of
G
. The points of
X_{G}(\Sigma)
are in one-to-one correspondence with the trace equivalence classes of representations
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\rightarrow G
. Therefore, choosing a basepoint and generators of the fundamental group of
\Sigma
affords an isomorphism of algebraic sets

\mathfrak{c}\colon\ X_{G}(\Sigma)\rightarrow G^{n}/\!/G.

Theorem 7.19

(
[
43
]
)
.

The adjoint action of the Poisson–Lie group
(G,\{\,,\,\})
on the Poisson manifold
(G^{n},\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR})
is a Poisson map. Hence
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}
defines a Poisson bracket on
G^{n}/\!/G
. Moreover the map
\mathfrak{c}
is a Poisson isomorphism:
\mathfrak{c}_{*}\{\,,\,\}_{\rm Gold}=\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}
.

The Theorems
7.17
and
7.19
relate
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
with
\mathcal{O}(G^{n})
and
\mathcal{O}(G^{n})^{G}
with
\mathcal{O}(X_{G}(\Sigma))
. We need to “lift” these results to the whole center
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
and corresponding rings of regular functions.

At first, recall the isomorphism
\mathcal{O}(\tilde{G}^{0})\cong\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
of Remark
7.16
. It maps
\mathcal{O}(T)^{W}
to
\mathbb{C}[\Omega]
, the
\mathcal{G}
-invariant subalgebra of
\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon})
. Composing it with
\Phi_{1}^{-1}
, we get an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}(\tilde{G}^{0})
with
\mathcal{Z}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
, mapping the subalgebras
\mathcal{O}(\tilde{G})
and
\mathcal{O}(G)
to
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
and
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
respectively. It follows straightforwardly from the arguments of Theorem
7.17
that
Fr
extends to an isomorphism

\widetilde{\rm Fr}\colon\ \big(\mathcal{O}\big(\tilde{G}\big)^{\otimes n},\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}\big)\rightarrow\big(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}),\{\ ,\}_{\rm QCA}\big)

mapping the
n
copies of
\mathcal{O}(T)^{W}
associated to the factors of
\mathcal{O}\big(\tilde{G}^{n}\big)
to
\mathbb{C}\big[\omega^{(1)}\big],\dots,\mathbb{C}\big[\omega^{(n)}\big]
. Note in particular that
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}
extends trivially from
\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes n}
to
\mathcal{O}(\tilde{G})^{\otimes n}
. Also, the restriction map (keeping voluntarily the notation of (
7.19
))

\tau\colon\ \operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}))\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})),

which by Proposition
7.2
is a regular map of degree
l^{n}
, corresponds under
\widetilde{\rm Fr}
to the projection map
\tilde{G}^{n}\rightarrow G^{n}
.

Now, let us identify as above
G^{n}
with the space of representation
R_{G}(\Sigma)=\operatorname{Hom}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),G)
(fixing a basepoint and generators of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
). The conjugation action extends trivially from
G^{n}
to
\tilde{G}^{n}
. Then the projection map
\tilde{G}^{n}\rightarrow G^{n}
provides an identification of
\tilde{G}^{n}
with a branched covering space
\tilde{R}_{G}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
of
R_{G}(\Sigma)
, endowed with the conjugation action of
G
. The points of
\tilde{R}_{G}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
are given by representations
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\rightarrow G
endowed with a choice of solution
x\in\mathbb{C}
of the equation
T_{l}(x)=\operatorname{Tr}(\rho(\gamma_{i}))
,
1\leq i\leq n
, where
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
are the
n
chosen generators of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. Taking algebraic quotients yields a branched covering map
\tilde{X}_{G}^{\prime}(\Sigma)\rightarrow X_{G}(\Sigma)
of the same degree
l^{n}
, and one can lift Theorem
7.19
to an isomorphism (with, again,
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm Gold}
trivially lifted from
X_{G}(\Sigma)
):

\tilde{\mathfrak{c}}\colon\ \big(\tilde{X}_{G}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\{\,,\,\}_{\rm Gold}\big)\rightarrow\big(\tilde{G}^{n}/\!/G,\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}\big).

We can now state and prove our generalization of Theorem
7.14
. When considering the (partial) action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}))
it will always be meant to be the diagonal action, by means of
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}
.

Corollary 7.20
.

(1)

The dual diffeomorphism
{\rm Fr}^{*}\colon\big(\!\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})),\{\ ,\}_{\rm QCA}\big)\rightarrow(G^{n},\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR})
maps the action of
{\mathcal{G}}
on the tangent spaces of
(0,0,\pm 1)^{n}
onto the coadjoint action of
G
on
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2,\mathbb{C})^{*n}
.

(2)

For any conjugacy class
\Gamma
in
G^{n}
,
({\rm Fr}^{*})^{-1}(\Gamma)
is a
(
non empty
)

{\mathcal{G}}
-orbit in
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}))
, and
\tau^{-1}
of this orbit has
l^{n}
connected components, all of whose are
{\mathcal{G}}
-orbits in
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}))
.

(3)

The map
\widetilde{\rm Fr}\circ\tilde{\mathfrak{c}}^{-1*}
takes values in
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
, and therefore affords an isomorphism of Poisson algebras

\widetilde{\rm Fr}\circ\tilde{\mathfrak{c}}^{-1*}\colon\ \big(\mathcal{O}(\tilde{X}_{G}^{\prime}(\Sigma)),\{\,,\,\}_{\rm Gold}\big)\rightarrow\big(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}},\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}\big).

(4)

The orbits of the group
{\mathcal{G}}^{\rm tot}
in
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}))
are the symplectic leaves of
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}
. These project onto the symplectic leaves of
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}
in
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}})
.

Proof.

(1) Using as usual the isomorphism
\bar{\sigma}^{-1*}\circ\bar{\psi}^{*}
to identify
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon})
with
\mathcal{O}(G^{0})
, it follows easily from (
7.39
), (
7.41
) and (
7.42
) that the automorphism
\Phi_{n}\circ\big(\Phi_{1}^{-1}\big)^{\otimes n}
of
\mathcal{O}(G^{0})^{\otimes n}
is equivariant with respect to the coadjoint action of
G
. Since
\Phi_{n}^{-1*}((0,0,\pm 1)^{n})=(\pm I,\dots,\pm I)
, the formulas in Proposition
7.18
(2) show that
d\Phi_{n}^{-1*}
maps the action of
{\mathcal{E}}^{\Delta}
,
{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}
on the tangent spaces of
(0,0,\pm 1)^{n}
to the coadjoint action of
X
,
Y
on
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2,\mathbb{C})^{*n}
. Post-composing
\Phi_{n}^{-1*}
with
\big(\Phi_{1}^{-1*}\big)^{\otimes n}\circ\Phi_{n}^{*}
, Proposition
7.18
(1) proves that
\Phi^{-1*}
maps the action of
{\mathcal{G}}
on the tangent spaces of
(0,0,\pm 1)^{n}\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}))
onto the coadjoint action of
G
on
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2,\mathbb{C})^{*n}\cong T_{(\pm I,\dots,\pm I)}^{*}\big(G^{0}\big)^{n}
. The result follows,
{\rm Fr}^{*}
being an extension of
\Phi^{-1*}
.

(2) This is an integrated version of (1) above. It follows from the arguments used to prove Theorem
7.14
(c)
, by replacing (
7.34
) with the formulas in Proposition
7.18
(2), and Lemma
7.10
with Proposition
7.18
(1).

(3) It is enough to prove that
{\rm Fr}\circ\mathfrak{c}^{-1*}
establishes a Poisson isomorphism between
\mathcal{O}(X_{G}(\Sigma))
and
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
. By Theorem
7.17
,
{\rm Fr}=\Phi^{-1}_{|\mathcal{O}(G^{n})}\colon\big(\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes n},\{\,,\,\}_{\rm FR}\big)\rightarrow\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}),\{\ ,\}_{\rm QCA}\big)
is an isomorphism of Poisson algebras. By (2) above
Fr
maps invariants functions to invariant functions. That the bracket
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}
is well-defined on
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
is an immediate consequence of its definition and the structure of module algebra of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
. Then the conclusion follows from the last claim of Theorem
7.19
.

(4) The first claim follows from Theorem
7.14
(d)
, and the facts that
\Phi_{n}
is a Poisson isomorphism from
\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({}_{\rm loc}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}),\{\ ,\}_{\rm QCA}\big)
to
\big(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}),\{\,,\,\}_{\rm QCA}\big)^{\otimes n}
(see the proof of Theorem
7.17
), and that
\Phi_{n}^{-1*}
maps the partial action of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\rm tot}
on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}))
to the partial action of
{\mathcal{G}}^{n}
(the
n
-fold direct product) on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}))^{n}
(by the definition of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\rm tot}
). The second claim follows from the first one in Theorem
7.19
.
∎

8
Topological formulation

8.1
The Wilson loop functor

Recall that we denote by
\mathcal{C}_{A}
the category of
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-modules of type
1
, and that
\mathcal{C}_{A}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]
is a ribbon category.

Recall also the following notions (see
[
78
]
). Denote by
{\rm Rib}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
the category whose morphisms are the isotopy classes rel
(\partial)
of oriented ribbon graphs in
[0,1]^{3}

colored
over
\mathcal{C}_{A}
(ie. with each component labelled by an object of
\mathcal{C}_{A}
), with boundary segments (if any) in
]0,1[\times\{1/2\}\times\{i\}
, where
i\in\{0,1\}
. The objects of
{\rm Rib}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
are the tuples
((V_{1},\varepsilon_{1}),\dots,(V_{k},\varepsilon_{k}))
, where
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{k}=\pm
and
V_{1},\dots,V_{k}
are objects of
\mathcal{C}_{A}
. The source and target objects of a morphism of
{\rm Rib}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
thus correspond to tuples of segments in
]0,1[\times\{1/2\}\times\{i\}
,
i\in\{0,1\}
, endowed with normal co-orientations specifying the associated signs
\pm
. We denote by
\mathbb{RT}
the Reshetikhin–Turaev functor

\displaystyle\mathbb{RT}\colon\ {\rm Rib}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}\to\mathcal{C}_{A}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big].

Now, fix points
p_{1}<\cdots<p_{n}
in
]0,1/2[
, and define
{\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}
as the category with the same objects as
{\rm Rib}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
but morphisms the isotopy classes rel
(\partial)
of oriented ribbon graphs in
[0,1]^{3}\setminus(\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}\times\{1/2\}\times[0,1])
, colored over
\mathcal{C}_{A}
and with boundary segments (if any) in
]1/2,1[\allowbreak\times\{1/2\}\times\{i\}
,
i\in\{0,1\}
. Figure
8.1
shows an example.

Figure 8.1:
A morphism of
{\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}
.

The composition of morphisms of
{\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}
is defined as for
{\rm Rib}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
. That is, given morphisms
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
with a same pattern of co-oriented boundary segments on the bottom of
T_{1}
and top of
T_{2}
,
T_{1}\circ T_{2}
is obtained by placing
T_{1}
atop
T_{2}
, gluing the corresponding boundary segments, and deforming the result by isotopy into
[0,1]^{3}\setminus(\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}\times\{1/2\}\times[0,1])
. Identifying
[0,1]^{3}
with the “right half” cube
[1/2,1]\times[0,1]^{2}
in
[0,1]^{3}\setminus(\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}\times\{1/2\}\times[0,1])
yields an obvious faithful functor

\iota\colon\ {\rm Rib}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}\rightarrow{\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}.

The morphisms of
{\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}
are obtained by composing morphisms of
{\rm Rib}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
and elementary morphisms as shown in the following picture, for
a=1,\dots,n
.

Figure 8.2:
A generating morphism of
{\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}
not in
{\rm Rib}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
.

Recall that we denote by
\Sigma
the sphere with
n+1
open disks removed. Denote by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}(\Sigma)
the
A
-module freely generated by the empty set and the isotopy classes of closed oriented ribbon graphs in
\Sigma\times[0,1]
colored over
\mathcal{C}_{A}
. The stacking product
L.L^{\prime}
of elements
L,L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}(\Sigma)
is defined as the isotopy class of the disjoint union of representatives of
L
and
L^{\prime}
, pushed in
\Sigma\times[0,1/2]
and
\Sigma\times[1/2,1]
respectively. The stacking product makes
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}(\Sigma)
an associative
A
-algebra. Since
\Sigma
is diffeomorphic to the closure of
[0,1]^{2}\setminus(\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}\times\{1/2\})
,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}(\Sigma)
can be identified with the
A
-algebra generated by the morphisms
\varnothing\rightarrow\varnothing
of
{\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}
, the stacking product being the
A
-linear extension of the composition of morphisms.

Define a new category
\mathcal{C}_{A}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)
with objects the couples
(n,V)
, where
V
is an object of
\mathcal{C}_{A}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}]
, and with spaces of morphisms

\operatorname{Hom}((n,V),(n,W))=\big(\!\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{A}^{\rm res}}(V,W)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]\big)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A},

where the composition is the tensor product of the composition of
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-module homomorphisms and the product in
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
.

The following result follows from the arguments in Sections 6.1 and 6.2 of Faitg’s PhD Thesis
[
39
]
, to which we refer for full details (see also
[
41
]
). These arguments generalize and simplify those of
[
20
]
. In these works only the Wilson loop map
W
was considered, but the extension to
\mathbb{W}
is straightforward.

Theorem 8.1
.

There is unique functor
{\mathbb{W}}\colon{\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{A}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)
such that on objects we have
{\mathbb{W}}((V_{1},\varepsilon_{1}),\dots,(V_{k},\varepsilon_{k}))=\big(n,V_{1}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes V_{k}^{\varepsilon_{k}}\big)
, where
V_{j}^{+}=V_{j}
and
V_{j}^{-}=V_{j}^{*}

(
the dual of
V_{j})
, and on morphisms:

•

{\mathbb{W}}(\iota(T))=\mathbb{RT}(T)\otimes 1
for every morphism
T
of
{\rm Rib}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
,

•

{\mathbb{W}}
gives the value
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(a)}
to the morphism shown in Figure
8.2
, in the case where there is a single ribbon colored by
V:=V_{1}
.

Moreover the morphism of algebras
W\colon\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}(\Sigma)\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]
obtained by restricting
\mathbb{W}
to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}(\Sigma)
takes values in the invariant subalgebra
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]
.

We call
{\mathbb{W}}
the Wilson loop functor, and
W
the Wilson loop map. By construction, for every element
\hat{T}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}(\Sigma)
, and any morphism
T
of
{\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}
obtained from
\hat{T}
by cutting open ribbons with colors
V_{1},\dots,V_{k}
, setting
V=V_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes V_{k}
we have

W(\hat{T})=\operatorname{qTr}_{V}\left({\mathbb{W}}(T)\right).

(8.1)

Here is an alternative way of defining
{\mathbb{W}}
, purely in terms of the Reshetikhin–Turaev functor
\mathbb{RT}
. Define a category
\mathcal{C}_{A}\big(\tilde{U}_{A}^{\otimes n}\big)
with same objects
(n,V)
as
\mathcal{C}_{A}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)
but with spaces of morphisms

\operatorname{Hom}((n,V),(n,W))=\big(\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{A}^{\rm res}}(V,W)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\big[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}\big]\big)\otimes\tilde{U}_{A}^{\otimes n},

where the composition is the tensor product of the composition of
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-module homomorphisms and the product in
\tilde{U}_{A}^{\otimes n}
.

The Alekseev map being a morphism of algebra, it defines a functor

\Phi_{n}\colon\ \mathcal{C}_{A}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{A}\big(\tilde{U}_{A}^{\otimes n}\big)

by setting
\Phi_{n}(f\otimes a)=f\otimes\Phi_{n}(a)
for every
a\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
,
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{A}^{\rm res}}(V,W)
. To any morphism
T
of
{\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}
we can associate a colored oriented ribbon graph in
[0,1]^{3}
,

T^{\sharp}=\bigg(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[p_{i}-\delta,p_{i}+\delta]\times\{1/2\}\times[0,1]\bigg)_{\tilde{U}_{A}}\cup T,

where
\delta>0
is small and the cores of the ribbons
[p_{i}-\delta,p_{i}+\delta]\times\{1/2\}\times[0,1]
are oriented from
\{p_{i}\}\times\{1/2\}\times\{1\}
to
\{p_{i}\}\times\{1/2\}\times\{0\}
, and the subscript
\tilde{U}_{A}
means that these ribbons are colored by the regular representation of
\tilde{U}_{A}
. Denote by
\overline{\rm Rib}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}
the category with morphisms given by the ribbon graphs
T^{\sharp}
; clearly the map
T\mapsto T^{\sharp}
yields a functor

\sharp\colon\ {\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}\rightarrow\overline{\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}.

By associating
\sigma\circ({\rm id}\otimes\pi_{V})\big(R^{\pm}\big)\colon\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes\tilde{U}_{A}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{A}\otimes\operatorname{End}(V)
to crossings of index
\pm 1
colored over
\mathcal{C}_{A}
and
\tilde{U}_{A}
, in the same way as
\sigma\circ(\pi_{W}\otimes\pi_{V})(R^{\pm 1})
is associated by
\mathbb{RT}
to crossings colored by objects
V
,
W
of
\mathcal{C}_{A}
, we extend
\mathbb{RT}
to a functor

\mathbb{RT}\colon\ \overline{\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{A}\big(\tilde{U}_{A}^{\otimes n}\big).

The next fact is a direct consequence of the definition of
\Phi_{n}
, and its representation by Figure
6.1
. Note that
\Phi_{n}
being injective, it defines
{\mathbb{W}}
uniquely from
\mathbb{RT}
.

Proposition 8.2
.

We have a commutative diagram of functors:

\textstyle{{\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
♯
\scriptstyle{{\mathbb{W}}}
\textstyle{\overline{\rm Rib}_{n,\mathcal{C}_{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{RT}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{A}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{n}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{C}}_{A}\big(\tilde{U}_{A}^{\otimes n}\big).}

8.2
The Wilson loop isomorphism

One can adjoin to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}(\Sigma)
the
A
-span of closed oriented ribbon graphs in
\Sigma\times[0,1]
colored over
\mathcal{C}_{A}

and possibly having coupons
(see
[
78
]
for this notion). Denote the resulting set by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}(\Sigma)^{c}
. It is an
A
-algebra for the stacking product, containing
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}(\Sigma)
as a subalgebra. Define a
one-coupon multicurve
as an element of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}(\Sigma)^{c}
that can be represented by a (oriented,
\mathcal{C}_{A}
-colored) ribbon graph embedded in
\Sigma\times\{0\}
(whence edges are unlinked and unknotted) and having a single coupon. An example of one-coupon multicurve with the coupon colored by
a_{X}
is shown in Figure
8.3
, where
\Sigma
is the four-holed sphere (i.e.,
n=3
).

Figure 8.3:
A one-coupon multicurve in
\Sigma_{0,4}\times[0,1]
.

Recall the matrices
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle X}}{{\mathbb{M}}}
and the invariant elements
v_{X}(a_{X})
defined for any
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-module
X=X_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes X_{n}
of type
1
and any
a_{X}\in\operatorname{End}_{U_{A}^{\rm res}}(X)
(see the comments after Proposition
6.22
). Denote
by
L(a_{X})
the one-coupon multicurve in Figure
8.3
; so
n=3
. By (
8.1
) it is clear that

W(L(a_{X}))=v_{X}(a_{X}).

(8.2)

This generalizes immediately to any
n\geq 1
. By the comments after Proposition
6.22
elements of the form
v_{X}\big(a_{X}^{(k)}\big)
form a basis of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}
. So Theorem
8.1
gives
(
with coefficients in
q^{1/D}
)
:

Lemma 8.3
.

The Wilson loop map
W\colon\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}(\Sigma)^{c}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathbb{C}\big(q^{1/D}\big)
is surjective.

In the sequel we assume that
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
. We are going to see that in this case we can strengthen Lemma
8.3
.

Set
\zeta:={\rm i}q^{1/2}
, and denote by
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}(\Sigma)\subset\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}_{A}}(\Sigma)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
the subalgebra freely generated as a
\mathbb{Z}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
-module by the empty set and the isotopy classes of oriented ribbon links in
\Sigma\times[0,1]
colored by the fundamental representation
V_{2}
. Recall that the Kauffman bracket skein algebra
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
is the
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}]
-algebra obtained from
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
by forgetting the link orientations, and taking the quotient by the ideal generated by the relations

\displaystyle L=\zeta L_{+}+\zeta^{-1}L_{-},

(8.3)

\displaystyle L\sqcup\bigcirc=-\big(\zeta^{2}+\zeta^{-2}\big)L,

(8.4)

where in the second identity
\bigcirc
is the trivial ribbon link in a ball disjoint from
L
, and in the first identity
L,L_{+},L_{-}\in\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
are identical up to isotopy except in a ball in which they look like (the strands representing flat ribbons on a meridional projection disk):

Figure 8.4:
Skein related ribbon links.

The following result is
[
23
, Theorem 1]
(see also
[
22
, Theorem 10]
). For completeness we give a proof by using the notions we have introduced.

Theorem 8.4
.

The linear map defined by
\mathcal{W}(L)={\rm i}^{{\rm lk}(L)}W(L)
, where
L\in\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
and
{\rm lk}(L)
is the linking number of
L
, descends to an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}(\zeta)
-algebras
\big(
where
\zeta:={\rm i}q^{1/2}\big)
:

\mathcal{W}\colon\ K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathbb{C}(\zeta)\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\otimes\mathbb{C}(\zeta).

Proof.

The facts that the values of
\mathcal{W}
do not depend on the link orientations, and that
\mathcal{W}
maps to
0
the ideal of
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
generated by the skein relations (
8.3
) and (
8.4
), follow from Proposition
8.2
, the injectivity of
\Phi_{n}
, and the properties of
\mathbb{RT}
and the
R
-matrix
R_{V_{2},V_{2}}
proved, e.g., in
[
56
, Lemma 3.18, Theorem 4.3 and Corollary 4.13]
. We stress that, using as usual the standard pivotal element
K
in the definition of
\mathbb{RT}
, the choice of variable
\zeta:={\rm i}q^{1/2}
and the normalisation of the map
W
by
i^{{\rm lk}(L)}
are necessary for the skein relations to be in the kernel of
\mathcal{W}
. Therefore
\mathcal{W}
descends to a module map defined on
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
. Since the stacking product is induced from
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
,
\mathcal{W}
is a morphism of algebras.

To show that
\mathcal{W}
is an isomorphism we use the following basis of
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathbb{C}(\zeta)
(see, e.g.,
[
55
,
63
]
). Let
\Gamma
be a trivalent ribbon graph onto which
\Sigma
retracts by deformation. Recall that an admissible coloring of
\Gamma
is an assignment of a nonnegative integer to each edge, called the color of the edge, so that the colors adjacent to each vertex form admissible triples. A triple of colors
(a,b,c)
is admissible if
a\leq b+c
,
b\leq a+c
,
c\leq a+b
and
a+b+c
is even. Admissible colorings
\gamma
of
\Gamma
parametrize multicurves carried by
\Gamma
, a color on an edge
e
giving the number of components running parallel to
e
. Given any admissible coloring
\gamma
of
\Gamma
, there is a skein
\Gamma_{\gamma}
in
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathbb{C}(\zeta)
corresponding to
\gamma
, obtained by replacing an edge with color
m
by the
m
-th Jones–Wenzl idempotent and vertices with Kauffman triads (these being defined in, e.g.,
[
63
, p. 136 and Figure 14.7]
). The set of multicurves on
\Sigma
forms a basis of
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
; since
\Gamma
is a spine of
\Sigma
, the set of skeins
\Gamma_{\gamma}
forms a basis of
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathbb{C}(\zeta)
as well. Note that, because the Jones–Wenzl idempotents have denominators, the skeins
\Gamma_{\gamma}
must be defined over
\mathbb{C}(\zeta)
.

By an isotopy of
\Sigma\times[0,1]\cong[0,1]^{3}\setminus(\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}\times\{1/2\}\times[0,1])
we can deform
\Gamma
(keeping the same notation) so that a neighborhood of the vertices lies inside a coupon embedded in
]1/2,1[\times\{1/2\}\times[0,1]
, and the
n
ribbons forming the portion
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
outside the coupon are attached to its left side, encircling
\{p_{i}\}\times\{1/2\}\times[0,1]
, for
i=1,\dots,n
. Let
\gamma
be an admissible coloring of
\Gamma
. Put
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}=\Gamma\setminus\Gamma^{\prime}
, and denote by
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\gamma}
,
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{\gamma}
the graphs
\Gamma^{\prime}
,
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
with edges colored by
\gamma
. Denote by
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
the edges of
\Gamma^{\prime}
ordered by increasing height, by
\gamma_{i}
the color of
e_{i}
plus
1
, and fix some orientation of the edges of
\Gamma
so that each
e_{i}
is oriented from top to bottom. In order to fit with the standard framework for computations with
\mathbb{RT}
, let us rotate the cube
[0,1]^{3}
clockwise by an angle of
\pi/2
around the axis
\{1/2\}\times[0,1]\times\{1/2\}
. Then, using Theorem
8.1
and the fact that the
m
-th Jones–Wenzl idempotent projects
V_{2}^{\otimes m}
onto a subspace isomorphic to
V_{m+1}
, we see that

\mathbb{W}(\Gamma^{\prime}_{\gamma})=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\gamma_{1}}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{\gamma_{n}}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n)},

which is an element of
\otimes_{i=1}^{n}\big(V_{\gamma_{i}}\otimes V_{\gamma_{i}}^{*}\big)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
by identifying
\operatorname{End}(V)
with
V\otimes V^{*}
, and

\mathbb{W}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{\gamma})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{q}}\big(\otimes_{i=1}^{n}\big(V_{\gamma_{i}}\otimes V_{\gamma_{i}}^{*}\big),1\big)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathbb{C}(\zeta).

By definitions we have
\mathcal{W}(\Gamma_{\gamma})=\mathbb{W}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{\gamma})(\mathbb{W}(\Gamma^{\prime}_{\gamma}))
, and the map
\gamma\mapsto\mathbb{W}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{\gamma})
maps the admissible colorings of
\Gamma
to a basis of the space of invariant elements of
\otimes_{i=n}^{1}\operatorname{End}(V_{\gamma_{i}})^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathbb{C}(\zeta)
for the action
(\operatorname{ad}^{r})^{\otimes n}
. Therefore the set
\{\mathcal{W}(\Gamma_{\gamma}),\gamma\ {\rm admissible}\}
is a basis of the space of invariant elements of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\otimes n}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathbb{C}(\zeta)
for the action
(\operatorname{coad}^{r})^{\otimes n}
. By applying to it the linear isomorphism
\Phi_{n}^{-1}\circ\psi\circ\Phi_{1}^{\otimes n}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
, with
\psi
the intertwiner of
(\operatorname{ad}^{r})^{\otimes n}
and
\operatorname{ad}^{r}_{n}
defined in the proof of Proposition
6.7
, we get a basis of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathbb{C}(\zeta)
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.5
.

Above we can replace
\mathbb{W}(\Gamma^{\prime}_{\gamma})
with
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}
, where
[\lambda]=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
, by “parsing” the ends of
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
as do the strands just above the coupon
a_{X}:=a_{[\lambda]}
in Figure
8.3
. Correspondingly
\mathbb{W}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{\gamma})
becomes an element of
\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{q}}\big(1,(\otimes_{i=1}^{n}V_{\gamma_{i}})\otimes(\otimes_{i=1}^{n}V_{\gamma_{i}})^{*}\big)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathbb{C}(\zeta)
. With these choices, the pairing of
\mathbb{W}(\Gamma^{\prime}_{\gamma})
and
\mathbb{W}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{\gamma})
is equivariant with respect to the actions
\operatorname{coad}^{r}_{n}
and
\operatorname{ad}^{r}_{n}
, and it puts in duality the product of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}
and the comultiplication map constructed in
[
22
]
.

The following result is a version of Theorem
8.4
over the ring
\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
:

Theorem 8.6
.

We have an isomorphism of algebras
\mathcal{W}\colon K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)\rightarrow\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
.

Proof.

We are going to use stated skein algebras and a result of Faitg (see
[
41
]
). Denote by
\Sigma_{0,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
our surface
\Sigma
with one point removed on its boundary, by
t^{1/2}
an indeterminate, and by
\mathcal{S}_{t}^{s}\big(\Sigma_{0,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\big)
the stated skein algebra of
\Sigma_{0,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
, as defined in
[
62
]
. It is an algebra over
\mathbb{C}[t^{1/2},t^{-1/2}]
, which contains
K_{t}(\Sigma)
as a
\mathbb{Z}\big[t,t^{-1}\big]
-subalgebra. By a result of
[
26
]
,
\mathcal{S}_{t}^{s}\big(\Sigma_{0,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\big)
is a
{\mathcal{O}}_{t^{2}}
-comodule algebra, isomorphic to the braided tensor product of
n
copies of
\mathcal{S}_{t}^{s}\big(\Sigma_{0,1}^{\circ,\bullet}\big)
. Here
{\mathcal{O}}_{t^{2}}
is
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
with variable
q=t^{2}
; by the duality between comodule algebras and module algebras we can canonically regard
\mathcal{S}_{t}^{s}\big(\Sigma_{0,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\big)
as a
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-module algebra, whence a
U_{A}
-module algebra. As coefficients contain
\mathbb{C}
we can replace
t^{1/2}
by
\zeta^{1/2}
so that
\zeta={\rm i}t
. On the other side, by the integral form of the last claim of Proposition
6.2
, which follows immediately from Proposition
6.9
and Lemma
6.10
, we know that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
is isomorphic to the braided tensor product of
n
copies of the
U_{A}
-module algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}
. In
[
41
, Theorem 5.3]
it is constructed an isomorphism of
U_{A}
-module algebras
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}\colon\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}^{s}\big(\Sigma_{0,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\big)\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta^{1/2},\zeta^{-1/2}\big]
. The restriction of
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
on the subalgebra
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
is just the Wilson loop map
\mathcal{W}
. To obtain
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
, an explicit isomorphism is constructed between
\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}^{s}\big(\Sigma_{0,1}^{\circ,\bullet}\big)
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}^{A}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta^{1/2},\zeta^{-1/2}\big]
(see
[
41
, Lemmas 5.7 and 5.8]
).

Let us consider the isomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}^{s}\big(\Sigma_{0,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\big)^{U_{A}}\rightarrow\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta^{1/2},\zeta^{-1/2}\big]
induced by
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
. Since
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta^{1/2},\zeta^{-1/2}\big]=\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big(\zeta^{1/2}\big)\big)\cap\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta^{1/2},\zeta^{-1/2}\big]\big)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}^{s}\big(\Sigma_{0,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\big)^{U_{A}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{-1}\big(\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta^{1/2},\zeta^{-1/2}\big]\big)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{-1}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{U_{q}}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big(\zeta^{1/2}\big)\big)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}^{s}\big(\Sigma_{0,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\big)=\big(K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathbb{C}\big(\zeta^{1/2}\big)\big)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}^{s}\big(\Sigma_{0,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\big)

by Theorem
8.4
, which is just
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta^{1/2},\zeta^{-1/2}\big]
. This proves the extension of
\mathcal{W}
to the scalars
\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta^{1/2},\zeta^{-1/2}\big]
is an isomorphism
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta^{1/2},\zeta^{-1/2}\big]\rightarrow\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\otimes_{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta^{1/2},\zeta^{-1/2}\big]
. Therefore
\mathcal{W}
is an isomorphism as well. This concludes the proof. ∎

Remark 8.7
.

(1)

Faitg results discussed above hold for surfaces of arbitrary genus. Also, in order to have at hands a calculus based on non oriented diagrams he uses a pivotal element different from
K
to define the map
W
(whence
\mathbb{RT}
) (see
[
41
, Remark 5.2]
). This leads to the choice of
t
in his constructions, instead of
\zeta={\rm i}q^{1/2}
as we obtained in the proof of Theorem
8.4
.

(2)

As a by-product of the proof we have
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta^{1/2},\zeta^{-1/2}\big]=\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}^{s}\big(\Sigma_{0,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\big)^{U_{A}}
.

(3)

The module algebra
\mathcal{S}_{\zeta}^{s}\big(\Sigma_{0,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\big)
is defined over
\mathbb{C}\big[\zeta^{1/2},\zeta^{-1/2}\big]
, and
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
over
\mathbb{Z}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
. One can observe that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
is in fact defined over
\mathbb{Z}\big[q,q^{-1}\big]
, for arbitrary
\mathfrak{g}
, and Faitg’s isomorphism
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
holds true over
\mathbb{Z}\big[\zeta^{1/2},\zeta^{-1/2}\big]
. Therefore
\mathcal{W}
in Theorem
8.6
holds true over
\mathbb{Z}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
.

8.3
The threading map and
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}
-invariant central elements

A
multicurve
on
\Sigma
is a union of disjoint simple non trivial (ie. not bounding a disk) closed curves considered up to isotopy. Any multicurve
\gamma
is a stacking product
\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i}^{c_{i}}
, where
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}
are disjoint (hence commuting) non-isotopic simple non trivial closed curves on
\Sigma
, and
\gamma_{i}^{c_{i}}
,
c_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
, consists of
c_{i}
parallel copies of
\gamma_{i}
.

Denote by
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
the set of multicurves on
\Sigma
. Recall the normalized Chebyshev polynomials
T_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, defined in (
7.2
). For
\gamma\in\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
,
\gamma=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i}^{c_{i}}
, set

T(\gamma):=\prod_{i=1}^{k}T_{c_{i}}(\gamma_{i}).

It is standard that
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
is a
\mathbb{Z}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
-basis of
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
(see, e.g.,
[
72
, Theorem 7]
) and that
\{T_{k}\}_{k}
is a basis of
\mathbb{Z}[X]
. Therefore
\{T(\gamma),\gamma\in\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)\}
is a
\mathbb{Z}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
-basis of
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
. It is called the
Chebyshev basis
(see
[
46
]
and the references therein). Theorem
8.6
implies that
\{\mathcal{W}(T(\gamma)),\gamma\in\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)\}
is a
\mathbb{Z}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
-basis of
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{Z}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
.

Now let as usual
l\geq 3
be an odd integer. Define
\mathcal{S}_{l}(\Sigma)\subset\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
as the set of multicurves of the form
\gamma_{\partial}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i}^{c_{i}}
, where
\gamma_{\partial}
is peripheral, i.e., a monomial in the skein classes of the boundary components of
\Sigma
,
\gamma_{i}
is a non peripheral curve, and
l
divides
c_{i}
for every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

Let
\epsilon
be a primitive root of unity of odd order
l
. Recall the element
\eta
in (
6.25
), the specialization
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}
in (
6.27
), and the algebra
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
and the derivations
D_{a}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
,
a\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})
, defined in Section
7.2.2
.

Theorem 8.8
.

(1)

The set
\{\mathcal{W}(T(\gamma)),\gamma\in\mathcal{S}_{l}(\Sigma)\}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-basis of the algebra generated by
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
and
\eta
. In particular, it is a central subalgebra of
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}
.

(2)

The derivations
D_{a}
,
a\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
, act on
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

(1) First note that for every
\gamma\in\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
,
\textstyle\gamma=\gamma_{\partial}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i}^{c_{i}}
with
\gamma_{\partial}
peripheral, we have

\mathcal{W}(T(\gamma))=\mathcal{W}(T(\gamma_{\partial}))\prod_{i=1}^{k}T_{c_{i}}(\mathcal{W}(\gamma_{i})).

(8.5)

Denote by
\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n+1}
the boundary components of
\Sigma
, ordered so that the diffeomorphism identifying
\Sigma
with the closure of
[0,1]^{2}\setminus(\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}\times\{1/2\})
maps
\partial_{n+1}
to
\partial\big([0,1]^{2}\big)
, and
\partial_{i}
to a small loop encircling
(p_{i},1/2)
,
1\leq i\leq n
. By (
8.1
) we have

\mathcal{W}(\partial_{n+1})=\operatorname{qTr}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(1)}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(n)}\big)=\eta,\ \mathcal{W}(\partial_{i})=\operatorname{qTr}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i)}\big)=\omega^{(i)},\qquad 1\leq i\leq n.

(8.6)

Therefore the elements
\mathcal{W}(T(\gamma_{\partial}))
form a basis of
\mathbb{C}\big[\omega^{(1)},\dots,\omega^{(n)},\eta\big]
, which is a central subalgebra of
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}
(see Theorem
6.27
). We can give formulas of the other terms as follows. Deform a ribbon neighborhood of
\gamma
in
\Sigma\times[0,1]
so that for every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
,
\gamma_{i}
is represented by a one-coupon multicurve like in Figure
8.3
, the coupon being filled with
r
simple arcs (possibly pairwise intersecting), for some
r\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
\gamma_{i}
is colored by
V_{2}
, we see that (
8.2
) takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(\gamma_{i})=\operatorname{qTr}_{V_{2}^{\otimes r}}\big(a(\gamma_{i})\mathbb{M}(\gamma_{i})\big),

where
a(\gamma_{i})\in\operatorname{End}_{U_{A}^{\rm res}}\big(V_{2}^{\otimes r}\big)
, and
\mathbb{M}(\gamma_{i})
is defined as
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\lambda]}}{{\mathbb{M}}}
, replacing the modules
V_{\lambda_{1}},\dots,V_{\lambda_{n}}
associated to the sequence
[\lambda]=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})
by
V_{2}
or the trivial module
V_{1}
(this latter case happens when the module labels a strand which has to be removed to get the one-coupon multicurve representing
\gamma_{i}
). In order to get simultaneously a simple expression of all the elements
\mathcal{W}(\gamma_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{W}(\gamma_{k})
, note that each
\gamma_{i}
, being a simple closed curve, separates
\Sigma
in two disks with punctures. We can choose the above diffeomorphism of
\Sigma
with the closure of
[0,1]^{2}\setminus(\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}\times\{1/2\})
so that the ordering of
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
makes
\gamma_{i}
bounding a punctured disk
D_{i}\subset[0,1]^{2}
with successive punctures
p_{j_{i}},p_{j_{i}+1},\dots,p_{j_{i}+n_{i}-1}
. Then, for every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(\gamma_{i})=\operatorname{qTr}_{V_{2}}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(j_{i})}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(j_{i}+n_{i}-1)}\big).

The algebras
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{Z}[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}]
, and therefore the expressions of
\mathcal{W}(\gamma_{i})
, associated to different presentations of
\Sigma
as above are related by isomorphisms induced by the mapping class group of
\Sigma
.

Now assume that
\gamma\in\mathcal{S}_{l}(\Sigma)
, so
l
divides
c_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq k
, in (
8.5
). Put
c^{\prime}_{i}:=c_{i}/l
. Then

\displaystyle T_{c_{i}}(\mathcal{W}(\gamma_{i}))=T_{c_{i}^{\prime}}(T_{l}(\mathcal{W}(\gamma_{i})))=T_{c_{i}^{\prime}}\big(\!\operatorname{Tr}\big(\!\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(j_{i})}\cdots\operatorname{Fr}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(j_{i}+n_{i}-1)}\big)\big),

(8.7)

where we use the standard identity
T_{c_{i}}=T_{c_{i}^{\prime}}\circ T_{l}
in the first equality, and Proposition
7.6
in the second.

By the first fundamental theorem of classical invariant theory for
{\rm SL}_{2}
(see, e.g.,
[
59
]
) and the Cayley–Hamilton identity, the set of trace functions

t_{j_{i},\dots,j_{i}+n_{i}-1}\colon\ \big(\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(1)},\dots,\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(n)}\big)\mapsto\operatorname{Tr}\big(\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(j_{i})}\cdots\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}}{}^{(j_{i}+n_{i}-1)}\big)

for all possible tuples
(j_{i},\dots,j_{i}+n_{i}-1)
are generating functions of
\mathcal{O}(G^{n})^{G}
. By (
8.7
) each of these functions is sent by
Fr
to
T_{l}\left(\mathcal{W}(\gamma_{i})\right)
for some
\gamma_{i}
. By Corollary
7.20
(3),
\widetilde{\rm Fr}
is an isomorphism from
\mathcal{O}(\tilde{G}^{n})^{G}
to
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
, mapping
\mathcal{O}(G^{n})^{G}
to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
, and the
n
copies of
\mathcal{O}(T)^{W}
embedded in the
n
factors of
\mathcal{O}(\tilde{G})^{\otimes n}=\mathcal{O}(\tilde{G}^{n})
to
\mathbb{C}\big[\omega^{(1)}\big],\dots,\mathbb{C}\big[\omega^{(n)}\big]\subset\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}
. Therefore
\mathcal{Z}_{(}{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
is spanned over
\mathbb{C}
by the elements
\mathcal{W}(T(\gamma))
with
\gamma\in\mathcal{S}_{l}(\Sigma)
and
\gamma_{\partial}
a monomial in
\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n}
. In particular
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
is a (central) subalgebra of
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}
.

(2) It is enough to show that the derivations
D_{a}
,
a\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
, restrict to endomorphisms of
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}
. This follows from the inclusion
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}\subset\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}
, since
[\tilde{a},u]\in\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}
for every
\tilde{a},u\in\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}
(
{\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}
being an
U_{A}
-module algebra), which by the definition of
D_{a}
implies that
D_{a}\big(\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}\big)\subset\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}
when
a\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
.
∎

Remark 8.9

(threading
T_{l}(V_{2})
)
.

For every
1\leq i\leq n
,
0\leq k\leq n-i
we have

T_{l}\big(\!\operatorname{qTr}_{V_{2}}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i+1)}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i+k)}\big)\big)=\operatorname{qTr}_{T_{l}(V_{2})}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T_{l}(V_{2})}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T_{l}(V_{2})}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i+1)}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T_{l}(V_{2})}}{{M}}{}^{\!\!(i+k)}\big),

where
T_{l}(V_{2})
is the virtual representation in the Grothendieck ring of
U_{A}^{\rm res}
-modules, obtained by plugging
V_{2}
in the
l
-th Chebyshev polynomial
T_{l}
. Indeed

T_{l}\big(\!\operatorname{qTr}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}\big)\big)=\operatorname{qTr}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\!T_{l}(V_{2})}}{{M}}\!\big)

because (using, e.g., the second picture in Figure
6.2
)

\operatorname{qTr}\big(\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\!V_{2}^{\otimes k}}}{{M}}\!\!\big)=\big(\!\operatorname{qTr}\big(\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle V_{2}}}{{M}}\big)\big)^{k}.

This and the computations in the first half of the proof of Proposition
7.6
prove our claim.

Remark 8.10
.

Recall that in Theorem
8.6
we have set
\zeta:={\rm i}q^{1/2}
. Let
\epsilon^{\prime}
be such that
(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}=-\epsilon
, and define
K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma):=K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}]}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
, where
\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
is the
\mathbb{Z}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
-module
\mathbb{C}
, where
\zeta
acts by multiplication by
\epsilon^{\prime}
. By a result of
[
45
]
, the set
\{T(\gamma),\gamma\in\mathcal{S}_{l}(\Sigma)\}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-basis of the center of
K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma)
. Therefore Theorem
8.6
and the proof of (1) show that
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
and
\eta
generate
\mathcal{Z}\big(\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}\big)
.

Remark 8.11
.

As in the proof of (1), denote by
\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n+1}
the boundary components of
\Sigma
, and fix the generators of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
to be
\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n}
(for some choice of basepoints). Consider the fiber product (with the notations of Remark
7.16
)

\tilde{G}^{n}\times_{G/\!/G}G/\!/G=\big\{((\tilde{g}_{1},\dots,\tilde{g}_{n}),[t])\in\tilde{G}^{n}\times G/\!/G\ |\ p(g_{1}\cdots g_{n})=p_{l}([t])\big\}.

As before Corollary
7.20
, one can identify
\tilde{G}^{n}\times_{G/\!/G}G/\!/G
with an
l
-fold branched covering space
\tilde{R}_{G}(\Sigma)
of
\tilde{R}_{G}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
, whose points are given by representations
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\rightarrow G
endowed with a choice of solution
x\in\mathbb{C}
of the equation
T_{l}(x)=\operatorname{Tr}(\rho(\partial_{i}))
, for every
i=1,\dots,n
and
i=n+1
. Taking algebraic quotients yields a branched covering map
\tilde{X}_{G}(\Sigma)\rightarrow X_{G}(\Sigma)
of degree
l^{n+1}
, and one can lift
\widetilde{\rm Fr}\circ\mathfrak{c}^{*-1}
to an isomorphism from
\mathcal{O}(\tilde{X}_{G}(\Sigma))
onto the algebra generated by
\eta
and
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{\epsilon})^{\mathcal{G}}
.

9
Applications to skein algebras

As usual denote by
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
the skein algebra of the sphere with
n+1
punctures,
n>1
. Recall
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
is an algebra over
\mathbb{Z}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
, where
\zeta
is an indeterminate. In this section we use Theorem
8.6
to reformulate some of our results on
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}
in the case of
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
in terms of
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
.

In
[
16
,
72
]
it was proved that
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
does not have non trivial zero divisors, and its center was computed by topological means. On the contrary the proof we give below of these two facts is purely algebraic, based on properties of
\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}
proved in Section
6
, that hold true for any complex finite dimensional simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.

Corollary 9.1
.

The skein algebra
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
does not have non trivial zero divisors, and its center is the polynomial algebra over
\mathbb{Z}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
generated by the classes
\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n+1}
of the boundary components of
\Sigma
.

Proof.

The claims are direct consequences of Theorem
8.6
, the formulas (
8.6
), and Proposition
6.11
and the last claim of Theorem
6.27
in the case of
\mathfrak{g}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
.
∎

Next we deduce from the results of Section
7
some properties of the center of the specializations of
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
at roots of unity of order
4l
,
l\geq 3
odd. Let
\epsilon^{\prime}
be such that
(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}=-\epsilon
. Define

K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma):=K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}]}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon^{\prime}},

where
\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
is the
\mathbb{Z}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
-module
\mathbb{C}
, where
\zeta
acts by multiplication by
\epsilon^{\prime}
.
The sets
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
and
\{T(\gamma),\gamma\in\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)\}
are
\mathbb{Z}\big[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\big]
-basis of
K_{\zeta}(\Sigma)
as well as
\mathbb{C}
-basis of
K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma)
. By Theorem
8.6
we get an isomorphism of algebras

\mathcal{W}\colon\ K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma)\rightarrow\big({\mathcal{L}}_{0,n}^{A}\big)^{U_{A}}_{\epsilon}.

Denote by
\mathcal{Z}(K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma))
the center of
K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma)
. Theorem
8.8
(1) implies immediately:

Corollary 9.2
.

The set
\{T(\gamma),\gamma\in\mathcal{S}_{l}(\Sigma)\}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-basis of a central subalgebra
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}(K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma))
of
K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma)
, which is generated by
\eta
and the image of the embedding

{\rm Ch}_{\mathcal{W}}:=\mathcal{W}^{-1}\circ\widetilde{\rm Fr}\circ\tilde{\mathfrak{c}}^{*-1}\colon\ \mathcal{O}(\tilde{X}_{G}^{\prime}(\Sigma))\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}(K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma)).

Therefore
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}(K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma))
is endowed with a natural Poisson bracket, the image of
\{\,,\,\}_{\rm Gold}
, which extends to an action by derivations of
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}(K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma))
on
K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma)
.

In fact
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}(K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma))=\mathcal{Z}(K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma))
(see
[
45
]
). One can check that
{\rm Ch}_{\mathcal{W}}
is a version of the threading map
{\rm Ch}\colon\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon^{{}^{\prime}l^{2}}}(\Sigma)\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma)
of Bonahon–Wong (see
[
17
]
, and also
[
46
]
). The point is that it affords an explicit realization of
Ch
in classical invariant theory terms (via the formulas (
8.5
)–(
8.7
)), and pulls the geometric tools of Section
7
onto
K_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\Sigma)
. A more symmetric statement is obtained by extending
{\rm Ch}_{\mathcal{W}}
to the ring
\mathcal{O}(\tilde{X}_{G}(\Sigma))
of Remark
8.11
, so that
\partial_{n+1}
belongs to its image.
